Classification of Hamiltonian S 1 S^{1} -actions on compact symplectic orbifolds with isolated cyclic singular points in dimension four
Leonor Godinho, Grace T. Mwakyoma-Oliveira, Daniele Sepe

Abstract
. In this paper, we classify Hamiltonian S 1 S^{1} -actions on compact, four dimensional symplectic orbifolds that have isolated singular points with cyclic orbifold structure groups, thus extending the classification due to Karshon to the orbifold setting. To such a space, we associated a combinatorial invariant, a labeled multigraph, that determines the isomorphism type of the space. Moreover, we show that any such space can be obtained by applying finitely many equivariant weighted blow-ups to a minimal space, i.e., one on which no equivariant weighted blow-down can be applied.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
53D20, 53D35.

Introduction

In this paper we study Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions on compact, four dimensional
symplectic orbifolds that have isolated singular
points with cyclic orbifold structure groups. Intuitively, an
orbifold
is a space that is locally
modeled on a quotient of Euclidean space by an effective action of a finite group (see Definition

1.6
). First introduced by Satake
in
[
46
]
, not only are they intrinsically interesting since
they are examples of ‘singular spaces’, but in fact play an important
role in complex algebraic and symplectic geometry, and beyond. In the
former, they represent examples of ‘mild’ singular behavior and have
been extensively studied in relation to
desingularizations
,
i.e., ways to resolve the singular locus to obtain a smooth complex
variety (see, for instance,
[
45
]
). In the latter, they arise naturally in the context of
symplectic reduction at a regular value of a moment map of a
Hamiltonian action. Thus, as stated in
[
29
]
,

‘we need to understand symplectic orbifolds even if we only
want to understand symplectic manifolds.’

In this direction, in recent years there has been plenty of interest in
desingularizing symplectic orbifolds to construct
interesting examples of symplectic manifolds (see,
among others,
[
10
,
11
]
). Finally, symplectic
orbifolds also arise naturally when studying the so-called almost
regular (or Besse) contact manifolds, as the latter are principal

S^{1}
-orbi-bundles over the former (see
[
25
]
).

Let
M
be an orbifold of dimension
n
and let
x\in M
be a point. Associated to
x

is its
orbifold structure group

\Gamma
, which, using the above
intuitive definition, corresponds to the isotropy of the orbit of the
finite group action that
x
represents (see Definition

1.17
). We say that
x
is
singular
if
\Gamma

is not trivial and that
M
has
isolated singular points
if the
set of singular points of
M
is discrete. In this paper, we are only
concerned with orbifolds with isolated singular points, the main
reason being that many of the subtleties of the differential topology
of orbifolds are made significantly simpler by imposing this
condition. For instance, for such orbifolds, the standard differential
topological notions of vector fields, differential forms, etc. can be
defined ‘naively’. Hence, the definitions of a symplectic manifold
and of a Hamiltonian action are the usual ones (see Definitions

1.99
and
1.126
).

Let
(M,\omega)
be a compact, four dimensional
symplectic orbifold that has isolated singular
points and suppose that
H\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is the moment map for an
effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on
M
. In this paper, we study
such objects under the extra condition that all orbifold structure groups be
cyclic
. This turns out to be a mild restriction, as
explained below. Let
x\in M
be a singular
point with non-trivial orbifold structure group
\Gamma
. Since
M
has only
isolated singular points, it follows that
x
is a fixed point of the
action (see Corollary
1.89
). Moreover, since
(M,\omega)
is
symplectic and has dimension four, the group
\Gamma
is (isomorphic
to) a subgroup of
\mathrm{U}(2)
– see Theorem

1.111
. If
\Gamma
is not abelian, by Lemma

2.2
and Remark

2.3
, it follows that
x
is an
isolated fixed point that is a local (hence global) extremum. Otherwise, since the singular set of
M
is
isolated, it follows that
\Gamma
has to be cyclic (see Lemma

1.115
). In other words, by restricting
the orbifold structure groups to be cyclic, we are only excluding isolated
extrema with non-abelian structure groups. A Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action
on
(M,\omega)
with the above hypotheses is called a
Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-space
and denoted by
(M,\omega,H)
.

The aim of this paper is to classify Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces up to
the natural notion of isomorphism (see Definition

1.126
), thus generalizing the main results in

[
21
]
to the setting of orbifolds. Given a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
(M,\omega,H)
, we associate a

labeled multigraph
to it that, when
M
is a manifold, coincides with
the invariant introduced in
[
21
, Section 2.1]
. However,
the invariant that we introduce is necessarily a
multigraph even when all fixed points of the action
are isolated and edges that are incident to a vertex need not have
coprime labels (see, for instance, Example
3.13
and Figure
A
).

2
2
{\frac{1}{4}(1,3),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{0}}
{\frac{1}{4}(1,3),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\frac{1}{4}}}
{\frac{1}{2}(1,1),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{8}}\,\,

Figure A.
Example of a labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
(see Example
3.13
).

Moreover, there are more
labels than in the setting of manifolds, reflecting the richness of
the (local) orbifold structure. Our first main
result states that the labeled multigraph is a complete invariant up
to isomorphism (see Lemma

3.14
and Theorem
5.1
).

Main Result 1
.

Two Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces are isomorphic if and only if the
associated labeled multigraphs are equal.

Intuitively, our strategy to prove Main Result
1
is to use an equivariant
desingularization via
weighted blow-ups
to reduce the problem to
one between smooth manifolds, so that we can invoke
[
21
, Theorem
4.1]
. We believe that the existence of an equivariant desingularization
(see Theorem
4.12
) should be of interest in its own
right, especially in light of its relation to Hirzebruch-Jung
continued fractions and, therefore, to the well-known Hirzebruch-Jung
resolution of cyclic quotient singularities in complex algebraic
geometry (see
[
45
]
).

Our second main result is concerned with determining the so-called

minimal spaces
, i.e., those Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces on which no
equivariant weighted blow-down can be performed. Together with
equivariant weighted blow-ups, these can be thought of
as the fundamental building blocks of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces. More
precisely, the following holds (see Lemmas
5.4

and
5.7
, and Theorems

5.6
and
5.10
).

Main Result 2
.

Any Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space can be obtained by applying finitely
many equivariant weighted blow-ups to a minimal space.

We split the proof of Main Result
2
in two cases, depending on
whether the given Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space has at least one fixed orbi-surface (see Theorem

5.6
), or not (see Theorem
5.10
). In each
case, our strategy for the proof is
the same and consists of two steps. First, we use the
localization formula for orbifolds (see Theorem

3.17
) to determine the possible labeled multigraph
of the minimal models (see Theorems
5.5
and

5.8
). Second, we construct
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces that have as labeled multigraphs those
determined in the first step (see Sections
5.2.3
and

5.2.5
). In all but two cases we construct the spaces explicitly; in the remaining two, we
obtain them from quotients of weighted projective spaces after
applying finitely many equivariant weighted blow-ups and
blow-downs. As a simple consequence of Main Result
2
, we
exhibit minimal spaces with isolated fixed points for which
the
S^{1}
-action cannot be extended to a toric one (see Lemma

5.11
). These include an example studied in

[
50
]
; moreover, this phenomenon should be contrasted with

[
21
, Theorem 5.1]
in the case of manifolds.

We view this paper as a step towards studying
‘large’ Hamiltonian actions on symplectic orbifolds, extending the
work in the completely integrable case carried out in

[
29
]
. As such, there are several potential extensions
of the present paper that we intend to work on in the future. These
include removing the (easier) assumption on the orbifold structure
group and the (harder) assumption on the singular set of the orbifold,
as well as computing the equivariant cohomology of these
spaces. Further ahead, there may be scope to extend the classification
of tall complexity one Hamiltonian torus actions in

[
21
,
23
,
24
]
to the realm of orbifolds.

Structure of the paper

In Section
1

we review standard differential topological
constructions for orbifolds, ranging from the definition and examples
of theses spaces
to maps between them, orbi-bundles, quotients, group actions and the study of
symplectic geometry for orbifolds. While most of the material in this
section is probably well-known to experts, we set the notation and
construct some fundamental examples that we use throughout the
paper. In Section
1.1
we
define orbifolds using local charts (but see also

1.38
). We introduce the orbifold structure group of a
point on an orbifold. Depending on whether this group is trivial or
not, the point is regular or singular respectively. We discuss maps
between orbifolds, coming to the notion of good maps introduced in

[
7
, Section 4]
. We conclude this section by recalling the
classification of compact, orientable two dimensional orbifolds with
isolated singular points, which is a simple extension of the analogous
result for manifolds. In Section
1.2
we discuss the
analog of fiber bundles in the setting of orbifolds, which are known
as orbi-bundles. We define these objects using local charts and
construct some examples of vector and principal orbi-bundles that are
used throughout the paper. Special attention is given to Seifert
fibrations, i.e., principal

S^{1}
-orbi-bundles over compact, two dimensional orbifolds with
isolated singular points. In particular, we discuss the construction of the
associated Seifert invariant (see Definition

1.63
). To conclude this section, we consider
orbifold covering maps which generalize topological covering maps. In
Section
1.3
we introduce suborbifolds, placing
particular emphasis on full (or strong) embedded suborbifolds (see Definition

1.75
). These can be thought of as being the
appropriate analog of embedded submanifolds in the setting of
orbifolds. For instance, such suborbifolds have a normal orbi-bundle
and there is a tubular neighborhood theorem (see Theorem

1.77
). We discuss group actions on orbifolds in
Section
1.4
. To start, we define them and
construct several examples that are useful for our purposes. As in the
case of manifolds, there are linearization results near fixed points
(see Corollary
1.91
). To conclude the
section, we state that finite quotients of orbifolds are again
orbifolds (see Corollary
1.94
), and use this
result to describe cyclic quotients of weighted projective
planes. Once endowed with a suitable symplectic form and Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-action, these underlie some of the minimal spaces that
we obtain. In Section
1.5
we discuss aspects
of symplectic geometry on orbifolds, with a view to introduce Hamiltonian
group actions. These are very similar to the better known versions for
manifolds. We start by defining and giving examples of symplectic
orbifolds. The connection between symplectic forms and compatible
almost complex structures is discussed and then the Darboux theorem is
stated (see Theorem
1.111
). We use this result
to classify open neighborhoods of isolated singular points with cyclic
orbifold structure group in a four
dimensional symplectic orbifold (see Corollary

1.120
). For our purposes, it is also useful to
recall the classification of compact symplectic surfaces with isolated
singular points (see Theorem

1.125
), as these appear in
our spaces as connected components of the fixed point set. We conclude
this section by defining Hamiltonian group actions, giving examples,
stating the equivariant Darboux and symplectic neighborhood theorems
(see Theorems
1.135
and
1.137
), and by stating
the connectedness result for fibers of the moment map of Hamiltonian
torus actions on a compact symplectic manifold (see Theorem

1.139
).

In Section
2
we take a closer look at the local
behavior of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions near fixed points. We start in
Section
2.1
by discussing the orbi-weights of a
fixed point of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action. First, we prove a general
result about finite extensions of tori (see Theorem

2.1
), which may be of independent interest and of use
in the study of Hamiltonian torus actions on orbifolds. The crucial
result that we need to define orbi-weights is Lemma

2.2
. In Section
2.2
we prove
the local normal form for an isolated fixed point with cyclic orbifold
structure group of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on a four dimensional
symplectic orbifold (see Theorem
2.9
). Moreover,
this section contains several technical results that we use in the
rest of the paper.

In Section
3
we introduce the complete
combinatorial invariant of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space, the
labeled multigraph. As a first step, in Section

3.1
we discuss properties of the connected
component of the fixed point set of such a space, as well as the
so-called isotropy orbi-spheres, which are the closure of a connected
component of the set of points with finite stabilizer. Then we proceed
to define the desired invariant in Section

3.2
. We also discuss some crucial examples
that, eventually, turn out to be minimal spaces (see Examples

3.12
and
3.13
). Moreover, we prove the ‘easy’
direction of Main Result
1
in Lemma

3.14
. As a first partial converse to this
result, in Section
3.3
we show that the
labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space allows one to
reconstruct invariant open neighborhoods of all of the connected
components of the fixed point set (see Lemma

3.15
and Proposition

3.18
). In the proof of Proposition

3.18
we make use of the localization formula (see
Theorem
3.17
) and so we also give
a short introduction to equivariant cohomology for orbifolds.

Section
4
is the technical heart of
our paper, where we discuss the effects of equivariant weighted
blow-ups and blow-downs on the labeled multigraphs of Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-spaces. In Section
4.1
we deal with
equivariant weighted blow-ups. We start by recalling the original
(equivariant) definition of this operation introduced in
[
14
,
35
]
, as well as
establishing some basic properties (see, for instance,

4.3
). Then we turn to the two fundamental
technical results of the paper, Propositions
4.5
and

4.9
, which describe the effects of certain equivariant
weighted blow-ups on the labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-space. Using Proposition
4.5
, we prove the key
result that every Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space may be desingularized after
finitely many equivariant
weighted blow-ups (see Theorem
4.12
). In Section

4.2
we discuss equivariant weighted
blow-downs. This is a fundamental tool that we use to prove Main
Result
2
. In particular, in this section we prove several
technical results that culminate in Corollary

4.17
, which gives a sufficient condition to
blow-down an
S^{1}
-invariant orbi-sphere.

We prove Main Results
1
and
2
in Section

5
. The ‘hard’ part of Main Result
1

is proved in Section
5.1
, while Section

5.2
deals with Main Result
2
. To prove the
latter, we need to introduce chains of
S^{1}
-invariant orbi-spheres,
which are entirely analogous to those considered in
[
21
, Section
3.1]
. Finally, we prove Main Result
2
by showing a
sequence of results (see Lemma
5.4
, Theorem

5.5
, Lemma
5.7
, Theorem

5.8
, as well as Sections
5.2.3
and

5.2.5
). We conclude this section by showing that certain minimal
models with only isolated fixed points are such that the
S^{1}
-action
cannot be extended to a toric one (see Lemma

5.11
).

In the final section of this paper, Section
6
,
we prove some additional results regarding Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces,
namely that they are Kähler (see Theorem
6.1
), and that
some of them are birationally equivalent to a certain minimal model
(see Theorem
6.2
), thus answering a question
posed to us by Margaret Symington.

Conventions

Given a positive integer
c>1
, we denote the
cyclic group of order
c
by
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
and we identify it as the subgroup
of
S^{1}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z|=1\}
of
c
-th roots of unity. A
generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
is denoted by
\xi_{c}
.

1.
A primer on (symplectic) orbifolds, orbi-bundles and group actions

In this section we review some background material on (symplectic)
orbifolds and group actions (see for example

[
1
,
7
,
18
,
29
,
51
]
for
details).

1.1.
Orbifolds

1.1.1.
Definition and first examples

Throughout this section, we denote a topological vector space with
underlying set
M
by
|M|
and every subset of
|M|
is endowed with
the relative topology.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
U\subseteq|M|
be an open set. A
local uniformizing chart
(l.u.c. for short) on
|M|
is a triple

(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
, where
\widehat{U}
is a connected smooth manifold,
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Diff}(\widehat{U})
is a finite group
acting effectively, and
\varphi:\widehat{U}\to U
is a
\Gamma
-invariant continuous
map such that the map
\bar{\varphi}:\widehat{U}/\Gamma\to U
given by
\hat{\varphi}([\hat{x}])=\varphi(\hat{x})
is a homeomorphism. We say that
U
is
uniformized
by
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
.

The above definition is equivalent to the standard one, in
which
\widehat{U}
is assumed to be a connected open subset of

\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see
[
7
, Lemma 4.1.1]
). Like charts for (topological) manifolds, l.u.c.’s need not be
unique. For this reason, it is useful to introduce the following
notions.

Definition 1.2
.

Given two l.u.c.’s
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
and
(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
on
|M|
,

•

an
embedding

\lambda:(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)

is a smooth open
embedding
\lambda:\widehat{U}\to\widehat{V}
such that
\psi\circ\lambda=\varphi
, and

•

we say that
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
and
(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
are
isomorphic
if
there exists an embedding
\lambda:(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)

that is a diffeomorphism.

Remark 1.3
.

An embedding
\lambda:(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
of l.u.c.’s
induces an injective group homomorphism
\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
(which we
also denote by
\lambda
), satisfying the following property:

\lambda(\gamma\cdot\hat{x})=\lambda(\gamma)\cdot\lambda(\hat{x})\text{ for all }\hat{x}\in\widehat{U}\text{ and
for all }\gamma\in\Gamma.

Moroever, the image of
\lambda:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
can be
characterized as follows:

(1.1)

\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{im}\,\lambda\Leftrightarrow\lambda(\widehat{U})\cap\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\lambda(\widehat{U})\neq\emptyset,

(see
[
1
, Section 1.1]
). In
particular, if
\lambda
is an isomorphism, then
\lambda:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is also an isomorphism. As a
consequence, the above notion of isomorphism is equivalent to the notion of
isomorphism of l.u.c.’s given in
[
7
, Section 4.1]
.

Given a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
on
|M|
and
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
,
the map
\lambda_{\gamma_{0}}:\hat{U}\to\hat{U}
given by
\hat{x}\mapsto\gamma_{0}\cdot\hat{x}
is an automorphism of
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
. In fact,

any
automorphism of
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
is of this form. This is the content
of the following result, stated below without proof (see
[
47
, Lemma 1]
and

[
37
, Proposition A.1]
).

Lemma 1.4
.

Let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be a l.u.c. on
|M|
. Given an automorphism
\lambda:(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\to(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
, there exists a unique
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma
such that

\lambda=\lambda_{\gamma_{0}}
.

Remark 1.5
.

The property of being uniformized behaves well under restriction to
connected subsets. More precisely, let
U\subseteq|M|
be an open
set that is uniformized by the l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
and let
U^{\prime}\subseteq U

be a connected subset. If we set
\widehat{U}^{\prime}
to be a connected
component of
\varphi^{-1}(U^{\prime})
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
to be the subgroup that
fixes
\widehat{U}^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime}:=\varphi|_{\widehat{U}^{\prime}}
, then

(\widehat{U}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a l.u.c. that uniformizes

U^{\prime}
. Moreover, the isomorphism class of

(\widehat{U}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
does not depend on the choices
made (see
[
7
, Lemma 4.1.1]
). We say that

(\widehat{U}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is the l.u.c. on

U^{\prime}\subseteq U

induced
by
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
.

Definition 1.6
.

[
1
, Definition 1.1]

Let
\lvert M\rvert
be a topological space.

(1)

An
orbifold atlas
on
\lvert M\rvert
is a collection

\mathcal{A}=\{(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\}
of l.u.c.’s such that

\{\varphi(\widehat{U})\}
covers
\lvert M\rvert
and
satisfies the following compatibility condition: For every
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi),(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)\in\mathcal{A}
such that
U\cap V\neq\emptyset
(where
U=\varphi(\widehat{U})
and
V=\psi(\widehat{V})
) and every
x\in U\cap V
, there exist an open
subset
W\subset U\cap V
containing
x
, a l.u.c.

(\widehat{W},\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\chi)\in\mathcal{A}
with
W=\chi(\widehat{W})
and embeddings

(\widehat{W},\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\chi)\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
and
(\widehat{W},\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\chi)\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
.

(2)

An orbifold atlas
\mathcal{B}
on
\lvert M\rvert

refines
the orbifold atlas
\mathcal{A}
if every
l.u.c. in
\mathcal{B}
admits an embedding in some
l.u.c. in
\mathcal{A}
.

(3)

Two orbifold atlases
\mathcal{A}
and

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\lvert M\rvert
are
equivalent
if they admit a common
refinement.

(4)

An
(effective) orbifold
is a paracompact, Hausdorff
topological space
\lvert M\rvert
endowed with the equivalence class of an
orbifold atlas. To simplify notation, we denote an orbifold by
M
.

Remark 1.7
.

Using the above notions of isomorphism of l.u.c.’s and of induced
l.u.c.’s, it follows that Definition
1.6
is
equivalent to
[
7
, Definition 4.1.2]
. In particular,
given an orbifold
M
and a point
x\in M
, there exists an open
neighborhood
U
of
x
that is uniformized by some l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
;
moreover the isomorphism class of the germ of
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
at
x
is unique.

Remark 1.8
.

In analogy with the case of manifolds, if
M
is an orbifold and

|M|
is connected, then there is a well-defined notion of
dimension
of
M
, which is given by the dimension of the domain
of any l.u.c. on
|M|
.

The ‘simplest’ families of orbifolds are illustrated in the following
two examples.

Example 1.9
.

Any (paracompact, Hausdorff) manifold is an orbifold: Given a smooth atlas

\mathcal{A}
on a
manifold
M
, we endow each
coordinate chart
(U,\varphi)
with the action of the trivial
subgroup of
\mathrm{Diff}(U)
.
\Diamond

Example 1.10

(Orbi-vector spaces)
.

Let
\widehat{V}
be a finite dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{K}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
and let
\Gamma<\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V})
be a finite
subgroup. The global quotient
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is an orbifold that is
known as an
orbi-vector space
.
\Diamond

Example
1.10
can be greatly generalized as follows. Let
G
be a Lie group acting on (the left on) a manifold

\widehat{M}
. We say that the action is
proper
if the
map
\phi:G\times\widehat{M}\to\widehat{M}\times\widehat{M}
given by
\phi(g,p)=g\cdot p
is proper, and
that it is
locally free
if all stabilizers are finite. If the

G
-action on
\widehat{M}
is proper, then the quotient space
\widehat{M}/G
is paracompact and Hausdorff (see
[
8
, Section
2.5]
). If the action is also locally free, then the Slice Theorem
(see
[
8
, Theorem 2.4.1]
),
implies that
\widehat{M}/G
can naturally be endowed with
the structure of an orbifold (see
[
1
, Section 1.1]
): A l.u.c. centered at a point
[p]\in\widehat{M}/G
is given by the triple
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
,
where

•

\widehat{U}\subseteq T_{p}\widehat{M}
is an open neighborhood of
the origin that is invariant under the the linear action of the isotropy
group
G_{p}
,

•

\Gamma=\rho(G_{p})
, where

\rho:G_{p}\to\mathrm{GL}(T_{p}M)
is the slice
representation
1
1

1

It is a consequence of the Slice Theorem
that
\rho
is injective if the
G
-action is effective.
, and

•

\varphi:\widehat{U}\to\widehat{M}/G
is the
composite of the quotient map
\widehat{U}\to\widehat{U}/\Gamma
and the inclusion
\widehat{U}/\Gamma\hookrightarrow\widehat{M}/G
arising from the
Tube Theorem.

Following
[
1
, Definitions 1.7 and 1.8]
, we call

\widehat{M}/G
a(n
effective
)
quotient
orbifold
; moreover, if
G
is finite, we say that
\widehat{M}/G
is a(n
effective
)
global quotient
.

Example 1.11

(Weighted projective spaces)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying
\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
. Consider the smooth function
H:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

H(z_{0},\dots,z_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}m_{j}\lvert z_{j}\rvert^{2}.

The level set
E:=H^{-1}(\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{j=0}^{n}\,\,m_{j})
is regular (and
diffeomorphic to a
2n+1
dimensional sphere). Moreover it is invariant under the smooth
S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
given by

(1.2)

\begin{split}S^{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{n+1}&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\\
\left(\lambda,(z_{0},\dots,z_{n})\right)&\mapsto(\lambda^{m_{0}}z_{0},\dots,\lambda^{m_{n}}z_{n}).\end{split}

The induced
S^{1}
-action on
E
is proper,
effective and locally free. Hence, the quotient

\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\dots,m_{n}):=E/S^{1}

is an
orbifold, known in the literature as a
weighted
projective space
of (complex) dimension
n
.

\Diamond

Figure 1.1.
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(1,a)
, also called a teardrop.

Example 1.12
.

The following example was constructed by Singer, Talvacchia and
Watson in
[
50
]
, and is carried out
throughout the text. The

effective action of

\mathbb{Z}_{4}
on
\widehat{M}:=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}

uniquely determined by

(1.3)

e^{\frac{\pi\imath}{2}}\cdot\left([z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]\right)=\left([-w_{0}:w_{1}],[z_{0}:z_{1}]\right),

is proper and locally free. Hence, the
quotient
M:=\widehat{M}/\mathbb{Z}_{4}
inherits the
structure of an orbifold.
\Diamond

Example 1.13
.

Let
c>1
be an integer and fix
1\leq l<c
such that

\gcd(l,c)=1
. The effective action of
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
on

\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
given by

(1.4)

\lambda\cdot\left([z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]\right)=\left([z_{0}:\lambda z_{1}],[w_{0}:\lambda^{-l}w_{1}]\right),

is proper and locally free. Hence, the quotient
M_{c}:=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{c}
inherits the structure of an
orbifold. In order to stress the dependence on
c
, we denote the
elements of
M_{c}
by
[[z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0},w_{1}]]_{c}
.
\Diamond

To conclude this section, we introduce the notion of orientation and
orientability for orbifolds. To this end, we fix once and for all the
standard orientation on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 1.14
.

Let
|M|
be a topological space.

•

A l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
on
|M|
is
oriented
if
\Gamma
is a
subgroup of the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of

\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

An embedding
\lambda:(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\to(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
between oriented l.u.c.’s on

|M|
is
orientation-preserving
if
\lambda:\widehat{U}\to\widehat{V}
is orientation-preserving.

•

An orbifold atlas
\mathcal{A}=\{(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\}
on
|M|
is
oriented
if
each l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
in
\mathcal{A}
is oriented and the embeddings
of part
(1)
of Definition
1.6
are
orientation-preserving.

•

An orbifold
M
is
orientable
if it admits an oriented
orbifold atlas. In this case, an
orientation
on
M
is a
choice of (equivalence class of) such an orbifold atlas.

Remark 1.15
.

•

A manifold is orientable as an orbifold if and only if it is
orientable as a manifold.

•

An orbi-vector space
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is orientable if and only if

\Gamma
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(\widehat{V})
.

•

Let
\widehat{M}
be an oriented manifold and let
G
be a Lie
group acting on
\widehat{M}
properly, in a locally free fashion,
and by orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms. The quotient orbifold

\widehat{M}/G
inherits a natural orientation.

Remark 1.16
.

If
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are orbifolds, there is a natural equivalence
class of orbifold atlases on the product
|M_{1}|\times|M_{2}|=|M_{1}\times M_{2}|
: If

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\}
and

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\}
are
orbifold atlases representing the orbifold structures on
|M_{1}|
and

|M_{2}|
respectively, the orbifold structure on
|M_{1}\times M_{2}|

is represented by the ‘product’ atlas

\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{(\widehat{U}_{1}\times\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2})\}.

The resulting orbifold is denoted by
M_{1}\times M_{2}
.
Moreover, if both
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are orientable, so is
M_{1}\times M_{2}
.

1.1.2.
Orbifold structure group

Let
M
be an orbifold, fix a point
x\in\lvert M\rvert
and let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be an
l.u.c. such that
x\in U=\varphi(\widehat{U})
. The stabilizers
of any two points
\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime}\in\widehat{U}
such that

\varphi(\hat{x})=x=\varphi(\hat{x}^{\prime})
are conjugate
as subgroups of
\Gamma
. We denote the underlying abstract group by
\Gamma_{x}(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
. If
(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
is an orbifold
chart satisfying the above properties, then

\Gamma_{x}(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)\cong\Gamma_{x}(\widehat{V},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
– for a proof,
see the discussion preceding
[
1
, Definition 1.5]
.

Definition 1.17
.

Let
M
be an orbifold. Given a point
x\in\lvert M\rvert
, let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be an
l.u.c. such that
x\in\varphi(\widehat{U})
. We call

\Gamma_{x}(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
the
orbifold structure group of

\boldsymbol{x}
and denote it simply by
\Gamma_{x}
.

Example 1.18
.

(Orbi-vector spaces, continued)
Let
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
be an orbi-vector space in the sense of Example

1.10
. The
orbifold structure group of
[0]\in\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is
\Gamma
.
\Diamond

Remark 1.19
.

For
i=1,2
, let
M_{i}
be an orbifold and let
x_{i}\in M_{i}
be a point with
orbifold structure group
\Gamma_{i}
. The point
(x_{1},x_{2})\in M_{1}\times M_{2}
has orbifold structure group given by
\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
.

Remark 1.20
.

By the Slice Theorem
(see
[
8
, Theorem
2.4.1]
) and since l.u.c.’s behave well with respect
to restriction (see Remark
1.5
), for every
x\in\lvert M\rvert
, there exists
a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
with the
following properties

•

\Gamma
is the orbifold structure group of
x
and
\Gamma<\mathrm{O}(n)
,

•

the open subset
\widehat{U}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
invariant under the standard linear action of
\mathrm{O}(n)
, and

•

the point
0\in\widehat{U}
satisfies
\varphi(0)=x
.

In this case, we say that
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
is a l.u.c.
centered
at
\boldsymbol{x}
. If, in addition,
M
is
oriented, then
\Gamma
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}(n)
.

Definition 1.21
.

Let
M
be an effective orbifold. We say that a point
x\in|M|
is

singular
if
\Gamma_{x}\neq 1
, and
regular
otherwise.

Remark 1.22
.

Since the group action in any l.u.c. is assumed to be effective, it follows that the set of regular points of
an effective orbifold is necessarily open and dense.

Remark 1.23
.

Remark
1.20
implies that any orbifold that has no
singular point can be viewed as a manifold, the smooth atlas being
given by (the equivalence class of) the union of the l.u.c.’s
centered at each point.

Definition 1.24
.

We say that an orbifold
M
has
isolated singular
points
if the set of singular points is discrete.

Example 1.25

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

If, in Example
1.11
, the positive integers

m_{0},\ldots,m_{n}
are chosen to be pairwise
coprime, then the weighted
projective space
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})
has isolated
singular points. These are
[(2\prod\limits_{j\neq 0}m_{j})^{1/2}:0:\ldots:0],[0:(2\prod\limits_{j\neq 1}m_{j})^{1/2}:\ldots:0],\ldots,[0:0:\ldots:(2\prod\limits_{j\neq n}m_{j})^{1/2}]
with orbifold structure groups given by

\mathbb{Z}_{m_{0}},\ldots,\mathbb{Z}_{m_{n}}
respectively.
\Diamond

Example 1.26

(Example
1.12
, continued)
.

The orbifold of Example
1.12
has isolated singular
points. These are
[[0:1],[0:1]],[[1:0],[1:0]]
and
[[0:1],[1:0]]

with orbifold structure groups given by
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
,

\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
respectively.
\Diamond

Example 1.27

(Example
1.13
, continued)
.

The orbifold of Example
1.13
has
isolated singular points. These are
[[1:0],[1:0]]_{c},[[1:0],[0:1]]_{c},[[0:1],[1:0]]_{c}
and
[[0:1],[0:1]]_{c}
and all have orbifold structure
group given by
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
.
\Diamond

1.1.3.
Maps between orbifolds

In this section, we define a certain class of maps between
orbifolds. We start by considering what happens for fixed l.u.c.’s.

Definition 1.28
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space and let
U_{i}\subseteq|M_{i}|
be an
open set that is uniformized by the l.u.c.
(\widehat{U}_{i},\Gamma_{i},\varphi_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Let
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
be a
continuous map.

•

A
\boldsymbol{C^{\infty}}
lift
of
f
is a

C^{\infty}
map
\widehat{f}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}

such that:

–

\varphi_{2}\circ\widehat{f}=f\circ\varphi_{1}
, and

–

for all
\gamma_{1}\in\Gamma_{1}
, there exists a

\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma_{2}
such that

\widehat{f}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\hat{x})=\gamma_{2}\cdot\widehat{f}(\hat{x})

for all
\hat{x}\in\widehat{U}_{1}
.

•

A
good
\boldsymbol{C^{\infty}}
lift
of
f
is a pair

(\widehat{f},\Theta)
such that:

–

\widehat{f}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}

is a
C^{\infty}
map with
\varphi_{2}\circ\widehat{f}=f\circ\varphi_{1}
, and

–

\Theta:\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
is a homomorphism making

\hat{f}
is
\Theta
-equivariant.

Remark 1.29
.

It is immediate from Definition
1.28
that the

C^{\infty}
map of a good
C^{\infty}
lift is, in fact, a
C^{\infty}
lift. However,
the converse need not hold (see
[
7
, Example 4.4.2a]
).

Just as l.u.c.’s are not unique, neither are (good)
C^{\infty}
lifts of
continuous maps between l.u.c.’s (whenever they exist).

Remark 1.30
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space and let
U_{i}\subseteq|M_{i}|
be an
open set that is uniformized by the l.u.c.
(\widehat{U}_{i},\Gamma_{i},\varphi_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Let
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
be a
continuous map and let
\widehat{f}
be a
C^{\infty}
lift of
f
. If
\lambda:(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
(respectively
\lambda:(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})
) is an isomorphism in the sense of Definition

1.2
, it follows from Remark

1.3
that
\widehat{f}\circ\lambda

(respectively
\lambda^{-1}\circ\widehat{f}
) is a

C^{\infty}
lift of
f
. Analogously, if
(\widehat{f},\Theta)
is
a good
C^{\infty}
lift of
f
, then by Remark

1.3
(and, in particular,
(
1.1
)),
(\widehat{f}\circ\lambda,\Theta\circ\lambda)

(respectively
(\lambda^{-1}\circ\widehat{f},\lambda^{-1}\circ\Theta)
) is a good
C^{\infty}
lift of
f
.

In light of Remark
1.30
, we introduce the following equivalence relation between (good)

C^{\infty}
lifts of
continuous maps between l.u.c.’s.

Definition 1.31
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space and let
U_{i}\subseteq|M_{i}|
be an
open set that is uniformized by the l.u.c.’s
(\widehat{U}_{i},\Gamma_{i},\varphi_{i})
and
(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{i},\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},\varphi^{\prime}_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Let
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
be a
continuous map. We say that

•

two
C^{\infty}
lifts
\widehat{f}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}
and
\widehat{f}^{\prime}:\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}\to\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2}
of
f
are
isomorphic
if there exist isomorphisms
\lambda_{1}:(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\to(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1})
and
\lambda_{2}:(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\to(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{2})
of l.u.c.’s such that
\widehat{f}^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\circ\widehat{f}
, and

•

two good
C^{\infty}
lifts
(\widehat{f},\Theta:\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2})
and
(\widehat{f}^{\prime},\Theta^{\prime}:\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}\to\Gamma^{\prime}_{2})
are
isomorphic
if there exist isomorphisms
\lambda_{1}:(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\to(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1})
and
\lambda_{2}:(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\to(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{2})
of l.u.c.’s such that
\widehat{f}^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\circ\widehat{f}
and
\Theta^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\circ\Theta
.

Remark 1.32
.

Whenever they exist, (good)
C^{\infty}
lifts behave well with respect to
restriction to connected open subsets. More precisely, let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space
and let
V_{i}\subseteq|M_{i}|
be an open set that is uniformized by
the l.u.c.
(\widehat{V}_{i},\Gamma^{\prime}_{i},\psi_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Let

f:V_{1}\to V_{2}
be a continuous map that has a
C^{\infty}
lift

\widehat{f}:\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2}
. Let
U_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
be an
open set that is uniformized by
(\widehat{U}_{i},\Gamma_{i},\varphi_{i})
for
i=1,2
such that
f(U_{1})\subseteq U_{2}
. Suppose that we are given an embedding

\lambda_{1}:(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\hookrightarrow(\hat{V}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\psi_{1})
. By construction,

\psi_{2}(\hat{f}\circ\lambda_{1}(\widehat{U}_{1}))\subseteq U_{2}
. Therefore, there exists an
embedding

\lambda_{2}:(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\hookrightarrow(\hat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{2})
such that

\hat{f}\circ\lambda_{1}(\widehat{U}_{1})\subseteq\lambda_{2}(\widehat{U}_{2})

(see
[
7
, Section 4.1]
). Using Remark

1.3
(and, in particular,
(
1.1
)), it follows that the map

\lambda_{2}^{-1}\circ\widehat{f}\circ\lambda_{1}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}
is a
C^{\infty}
lift of the restriction of
f
to

U_{1}
. Analogously, if
(\widehat{f},\Theta)
is a good

C^{\infty}
lift of
f
, then
(\lambda_{2}^{-1}\circ\widehat{f}\circ\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\lambda_{1})
is a
good
C^{\infty}
lift of the restriction of
f
to

U_{1}
. We refer to the
C^{\infty}
lift (respectively good
C^{\infty}
lift) constructed above as the
restriction
of

\widehat{f}
(respectively
(\widehat{f},\Theta)
) to
\widehat{U}_{1}
.

Following
[
7
, Section 4]
, we break the definition of the
maps of our interest in two parts. First, we introduce
C^{\infty}

lifts of maps between orbifolds.

Definition 1.33
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be orbifolds and let
f:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a
continuous map. A
\boldsymbol{C}^{\infty}
lift
of
f

consists of the following data:

•

orbifold atlases
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\}
such that, for all

(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, there
exists
(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
with

f(\varphi(\widehat{U}_{1}))\subseteq\varphi_{2}(\widehat{U}_{2})
,

•

for all
(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that

f(\varphi(\widehat{U}_{1}))\subseteq\varphi_{2}(\widehat{U}_{2})
, a

C^{\infty}
lift of the restriction of
f
to
\varphi_{1}(\widehat{U}_{1})
,

satisfying the following compatibility condition:

Given
(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1}),(\widehat{V}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\psi_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
with
U_{1}\cap V_{1}\neq\emptyset
(where
U_{1}=\varphi(\widehat{U_{1}})
and
V_{1}=\psi(\widehat{V_{1}})
), and given a point
x\in U_{1}\cap V_{1}
,
there exist

•

an open subset
W_{1}\subseteq U_{1}\cap V_{1}

uniformized by a l.u.c.
(\widehat{W}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1},\chi_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
containing
x
,

•

an open subset
W_{2}\subseteq|M_{2}|
uniformized by

(\widehat{W}_{2},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime},\chi_{2})
such that

f(W_{1})\subseteq W_{2}
, and

•

embeddings
(\widehat{W}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1},\chi_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
and

(\widehat{W}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1},\chi_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\psi_{1})
,

such that the
C^{\infty}
lift of the restriction
of
f
to
W_{1}
is isomorphic to the restrictions of the

C^{\infty}
lifts of
f|_{U_{1}}
and
f|_{V_{1}}
to
W_{1}
.
We denote a
C^{\infty}
lift of
f
by the triple

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\{\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}\})
.

In order to make Definition
1.33
independent of
choices, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 1.34
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be orbifolds and let
f:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a
continuous map. We say that the
C^{\infty}
lifts
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\{\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}\})
and
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2},\{\widehat{f}^{\prime}_{\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}}:\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}\to\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2}\})
of
f
are
equivalent
if there exists a

C^{\infty}
lift
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2},\{\widehat{f}_{\widehat{V}_{1}}:\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2}\})
such that

•

the orbifold atlas
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is a common refinement of

\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,2
, and

•

for all l.u.c.’s
(\widehat{V}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{1})
and for all embeddings
(\widehat{V}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
(\widehat{V}_{1},\tilde{\Gamma}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1})
, the
C^{\infty}
lift

\widehat{f}_{\widehat{V}_{1}}
of the restriction of
f
to
V_{1}=\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(\widehat{V}_{1})
is equivalent to the restrictions of

\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}}
and
\widehat{f}^{\prime}_{\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}}

to
V_{1}
.

Using Definitions
1.33
and

1.34
, we can introduce smooth maps between
orbifolds.

Definition 1.35
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be orbifolds. A
\boldsymbol{C^{\infty}}

map
between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
is a continuous map
f:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
together with an equivalence class of
C^{\infty}
lifts of

f
. For simplicity, we denote a
C^{\infty}
map by
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
.

Unfortunately,
C^{\infty}
maps in the sense of Definition

1.35
do not have good geometric properties (for
instance, in general they do not pull vector bundles back to vector bundles). To
rectify this issue, following
[
7
, Section 4.4]
, we consider those
C^{\infty}
maps that have the
following type of representative in the equivalence class of
C^{\infty}
lifts.

Definition 1.36
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}
be orbifolds and let
f:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a
continuous map. A
C^{\infty}
lift
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\{\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}}:\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2}\})
of
f
is
good
if

•

for all
(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, there exists a homomorphism
\Theta_{\widehat{U}_{1}}:\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma_{2}
such that
(\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}},\Theta_{\widehat{U}_{1}})
is a good
C^{\infty}
lift of the
restriction of
f
to
U_{1}
,

•

if
\lambda_{1}:(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\varphi^{\prime}_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
is an embedding between
l.u.c.’s in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, then there
exists an embedding
\lambda_{2}:(\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\varphi^{\prime}_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})
between the corresponding
l.u.c.’s in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that the restriction of
(\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}_{1}},\Theta_{\widehat{U}_{1}})
to
\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}
equals
(\widehat{f}_{\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}},\Theta_{\widehat{U}^{\prime}_{1}})
.

Definition 1.37
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be orbifolds.

•

A
C^{\infty}
map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is
good
if there
exists a good
C^{\infty}
lift in the equivalence class of
f
.

•

A
diffeomorphism
between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
is a good

C^{\infty}
map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
that admits an inverse
g:M_{2}\to M_{1}
that is also a good
C^{\infty}
map.

Remark 1.38

(Good maps via Lie groupoids)
.

The above definition of good
C^{\infty}
maps is somewhat
cumbersome and can be made more transparent using the approach to
orbifolds using
Lie groupoids
(see
[
1
, Section 1.4]

and references therein for more
details). Loosely speaking, an orbifold
M
is an
equivalence class of certain Lie groupoids, where the topological
space
|M|
is homeomorphic to the orbit space of any representative
in the equivalence class. (The equivalence relation is under the
so-called
Morita equivalences
, see
[
1
, Definition
1.43]
.) In the case of a global quotient

\widehat{M}/G
, a Lie groupoid representing
\widehat{M}/G
is the
action groupoid
G\ltimes\widehat{M}
. The orbifold structure
group at a point
x\in M
corresponds to the isotropy Lie group of
the orbit representing
x
with respect to any Lie groupoid
representing
M
.

From this perspective, if

M_{1},M_{2}
are orbifolds, a
continuous map
f:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
is a good
C^{\infty}
map if
and only if there exist Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and

\mathcal{G}_{2}
representing
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
respectively, and a Lie
groupoid homomorphism
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{2}
, such that the induced map on the orbit spaces equals

f
– once the homeomorphisms identifying the orbit spaces of

\mathcal{G}_{1}
and of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
with
|M_{1}|
and
|M_{2}|

respectively are
taken into account – (see
[
30
, Proposition 5.1.7]
).

The perspective on orbifolds via Lie groupoids goes beyond the scope
of this paper. However, it is used sparingly to give
quick sketches of proofs (see, for instance, Lemma
1.41

below).

In what follows we give families of
C^{\infty}
maps between
orbifolds that are good. We start by introducing the following notion (see

[
7
, Definition 4.4.10]
).

Definition 1.39
.

A
C^{\infty}
map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
between orbifolds is
regular
if the preimage of the subset of
M_{2}
consisting of
regular points under
f
is open, dense and connected in
M_{1}
.

The following result, stated below without proof, is proved in

[
7
, Lemma 4.4.11]
.

Lemma 1.40
.

Regular
C^{\infty}
maps are good.

The next result provides another large class of good
C^{\infty}

maps.

Lemma 1.41
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{i}
be a manifold and let
G_{i}
be a Lie group
acting on
\widehat{M}_{i}
so that the action is proper and locally
free for
i=1,2
. If
\widehat{f}:\widehat{M}_{1}\to\widehat{M}_{2}

is a smooth map and if
\Theta:G_{1}\to G_{2}
is a homomorphism of
Lie groups such that
\widehat{f}
is
\Theta
-equivariant, then the
continuous map
f:\widehat{M}_{1}/G_{1}\to\widehat{M}_{2}/G_{2}
given
by
[\widehat{x}]\mapsto[\widehat{f}(\widehat{x})]
is a good

C^{\infty}
map.

Sketch of proof.

We give two strategies to prove the above result. The first is to
use the l.u.c.’s for quotients given in Section

1.1.1
, i.e., to check the result in ‘local
coordinates’. It can be checked directly that
\widehat{f}
and

\Theta
induce a good
C^{\infty}
lift of the restriction of
f

to any open neighborhood that is uniformized by a l.u.c., and that
the compatibility condition of Definition
1.36

holds.

The second (more direct) strategy is to use the Lie groupoid approach to orbifolds
(see Remark
1.38
). The map
\widehat{f}
and the
homomorphism
\Theta
can be used to construct a Lie groupoid
homomorphism
\mathcal{F}:G_{1}\ltimes M_{1}\to G_{2}\ltimes M_{2}

with the property that the induced map on orbit spaces coincides
with
f
. The result then follows at once from
[
30
, Proposition 5.1.7]
.
∎

In the following example, we illustrate an application of Lemma

1.41
.

Example 1.42

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

As in Example
1.11
, we fix an integer
n\geq 1
and positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying
\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
. We
consider the smooth
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
given by

(1.5)

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}\\
\left(w,(z_{0},\dots,z_{n})\right)&\mapsto(w^{m_{0}}z_{0},\dots,w^{m_{n}}z_{n}),\end{split}

(cf. (
1.2
)). This action is proper and locally free,
so that the quotient
(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{*}
inherits the structure of an orbifold. We denote an
element in
(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{*}
by
[z_{0}:\ldots:z_{n}]
. We remark that, just as for
projective spaces, we may exploit the equivalence relation to carry
out calculations in ‘local coordinates’ that we refer to as
homogeneous coordinates
(see, for instance, Example

1.96
below).

Let
E\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
be as in Example

1.11
. If
\widehat{f}:E\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
and
\Theta:S^{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
denote the
inclusions, then, by Lemma
1.41
, the induced continuous map
f:\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a good
C^{\infty}
map. In fact, it is a
diffeomorphism and the inverse is given by the ‘standard’ projection
maps
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}\to E
and
\mathbb{C}^{*}\to S^{1}
. Throughout
this paper, we use this diffeomorphism to identify
(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{*}
with
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
tacitly, trusting that this does not cause confusion.
\Diamond

To conclude this section, we give a sketch of a proof of the following
result regarding good
C^{\infty}
maps.

Lemma 1.43
.

Let
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
be a good
C^{\infty}
map. For any point
x\in M_{1}
, there is an induced conjugacy class of homomorphisms

\Gamma_{x}\to\Gamma_{f(x)}
.

Sketch of proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
1.41
, we give two
strategies. The first is to check the result directly as in

[
7
, Definition 4.4.9]
. The second (more direct) strategy is to use the Lie groupoid approach to orbifolds
(see Remark
1.38
): With the latter, the result is
immediate as a Lie groupoid morphism induces homomorphisms between
isotropy groups and Morita equivalences preserve the conjugacy class
of these homomorphisms.
∎

1.1.4.
Compact, connected, orientable orbi-surfaces with isolated
singular points

In this section, we state a classification result for a certain family
of two-dimensional orbifolds, which we call
orbi-surfaces
. This
result should be seen as the analog of the well-known classification of closed
two-dimensional manifolds for orbifolds.

Let
\Sigma
be an orientable orbi-surface, let
x\in\Sigma
be a point, and let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be a l.u.c. centered at
x
as in Remark

1.20
. The orbifold structure group
\Gamma
of
x
is a
subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}(2)
. Hence, if
\Gamma
is not trivial, then it is
a finite cyclic subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
for some natural number
c\geq 2
. If, in addition,
\Sigma
is compact and has only isolated singular
points, then it has finitely many such points. The following result,
stated below without proof, provides a complete classification of such orbi-surfaces.

Theorem 1.44
.

[
43
, Theorem
9.5.2]

(1)

If
\Sigma
be a compact, connected, orientable orbi-surface with isolated
singular points, then there exists a non-negative integer
g
such
that
|\Sigma|
is homeomorphic to the compact orientable surface of
genus
g
.

(2)

For
i=1,2
, let
\Sigma_{i}
be a compact, connected, orientable orbi-surface with isolated
singular points, let
g_{i}
be the genus of
|\Sigma_{i}|
, let

S_{i}
be the set of singular points of
\Sigma_{i}
, and let
c_{i}:S_{i}\to\mathbb{N}
be the map that sends a singular point to the order of its orbifold structure group. The two orbifolds
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are diffeomorphic if
and only if
g_{1}=g_{2}
and there exists a bijection
\sigma:S_{1}\to S_{2}
such that
c_{2}\circ\sigma=c_{1}
.

(3)

For any non-negative integers
g,n
and for any map
c:\{1,\ldots,n\}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
c(j)\geq 2
for all

j=1,\ldots,n
, there exists a compact, connected, orientable orbi-surface with isolated
singular points
\Sigma
such that
|\Sigma|
is homeomorphic to the compact orientable surface of
genus
g
, the set of singular points
S
of
\Sigma
has cardinality

n
and there exists a bijection
\sigma:S\to\{1,\ldots,n\}

with the property that
c(\sigma(p))
is the order of the orbifold
structure group of
p
, for any
p\in S
.

1.2.
Orbi-bundles

1.2.1.
Definition and Examples

In this section, we follow mainly
[
12
,
14
]
.

Definition 1.45
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space for
i=1,2
, let
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a
surjective continuous map, let
F
be a connected smooth manifold and let
G

be a Lie group acting on
F
. We say that
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
admits a
local trivializing l.u.c.

over the open set
U_{2}\subseteq|M_{2}|
with generic fiber
F
and
structure group
G
if there exist

•

a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})
uniformizing

U_{2}
,

•

a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
uniformizing

U_{1}:=\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(U_{2})
,

•

a smooth map
\rho:\Gamma_{2}\times\widehat{U}_{2}\to G
, and

•

a diffeomorphism
\lambda:\widehat{U}_{2}\times F\to\widehat{U}_{1}
,

such that

•

the map

\begin{split}\Gamma_{2}\times\widehat{U}_{2}\times F&\to\widehat{U}_{2}\times F\\
(\gamma,\widehat{x},y)&\mapsto(\gamma\cdot\widehat{x},\rho(\gamma,\widehat{x})(y))\end{split}

is an action of
\Gamma_{2}
on
\widehat{U}_{2}\times F
, where

\cdot
denotes the
\Gamma_{2}
-action on
\widehat{U}_{2}
,

•

the open set
U_{1}
is uniformized by the l.u.c.

(\widehat{U}_{2}\times F,\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1}\circ\lambda)
, and

•

the pair
(\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}_{1},\mathrm{id})
is a good
C^{\infty}
lift of
\mathrm{pr}
.

We denote a local trivializing l.u.c. for
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
over
U_{2}
by

(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\rho,\lambda).

Remark 1.46
.

The conditions in Definition
1.45
imply that
\lambda:(\widehat{U}_{2}\times F,\Gamma_{2},\lambda\circ\varphi_{1})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})
is an isomorphism of l.u.c.’s and that

(\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}\circ\lambda^{-1},\lambda^{-1})
is a
good
C^{\infty}
lift of
\mathrm{pr}
. This is summarized in the
following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\widehat{U}_{2}\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\widehat{U}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}\circ\lambda^{-1}}
\textstyle{U_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}}
\textstyle{\widehat{U}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{2}}
\textstyle{U_{2}.}

As the terminology suggests, local trivializing l.u.c.’s should be
thought of as being analogous to l.u.c.’s and local trivializations for
fiber bundles with a given structure group. In this sense, the following notion is the
analog of Definition
1.2
.

Definition 1.47
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space for
i=1,2
, let
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a
surjective continuous map, let
F
be a smooth manifold, let
G
be
a Lie group acting effectively on
F
, and let

U_{2},V_{2}\subseteq|M_{2}|
be open sets. Given local trivializing
l.u.c.’s
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
and
(\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mu)
for
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
with generic
fiber
F
and structure group
G
over
U_{2}

and
V_{2}
respectively, an
embedding
of
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
into
(\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mu)
is an embedding
\Lambda:(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{2})
together with a map
g:\widehat{U}_{2}\to G
such that the map

\begin{split}\widehat{U}_{2}\times F&\to\widehat{V}_{2}\times F\\
(\widehat{x},y)&\mapsto(\Lambda(x),g(x)(y))\end{split}

is an embedding between
(\widehat{U}_{2}\times F,\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1}\circ\lambda)
and
(\widehat{V}_{2}\times F,\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1}\circ\lambda)
. We denote such an embedding by

(\Lambda,g)
and say that
(\Lambda,g)

extends

\Lambda
.

We say that
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
and
(\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mu)
are
isomorphic
if there exists an
embedding
(\Lambda,g)
of
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
into
(\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mu)
such that
\Lambda:(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{2})
is an isomorphism.

Remark 1.48
.

It can be checked that the analogs of Remarks

1.3
and
1.5
hold for
local trivializing l.u.c.’s.

Definition 1.49
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space for
i=1,2
, let
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a surjective
continuous map, let
F
be a smooth manifold, and let
G
be a Lie
group acting on
F
. We say that
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
admits an
orbi-bundle atlas

with generic fiber
F
and structure group
G
if there exists an orbifold atlas

\mathcal{A}_{2}
on
|M_{2}|
such that

•

for each
(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, the map
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
admits a
local trivializing l.u.c. over
U_{2}=\varphi_{2}(\widehat{U}_{2})
with generic fiber
F
and structure group
G
of
the form
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
, and

•

for every pair
(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2}),(\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
with
U_{2}\cap V_{2}\neq\emptyset
, for
every
x\in U_{2}\cap V_{2}
, and for every

(\widehat{W}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2},\chi_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
with
x\in W_{2}
and with embeddings
(\widehat{W}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2},\chi_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{2})
and
(\widehat{W}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2},\chi_{2})\hookrightarrow(\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{2})
, there exist
embeddings of
(\widehat{W}_{1}\to\widehat{W}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2},\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\nu)
into
(\widehat{U}_{1}\to\widehat{U}_{2},\Gamma_{2},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\lambda)
and
(\widehat{V}_{1}\to\widehat{V}_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}_{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\mu)
that extend the
above embeddings of l.u.c.’s.

Remark 1.50
.

Given an orbi-bundle atlas as in Definition

1.49
, the collection

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{(\widehat{U}_{1},\Gamma_{1},\varphi_{1})\}
defines an orbifold atlas
on
|M_{1}|
. Hence,
a posteriori
,
M_{1}
is an orbifold. The
second bullet point in Definition
1.49

is a compatibility condition between
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. For this reason, we denote an orbi-bundle atlas
by
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and we refer to the local
trivializing l.u.c.’s of Definition

1.49
as
lying in

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

Definition 1.51
.

Let
|M_{i}|
be a topological space for
i=1,2
, let
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
be a surjective
continuous map, let
F
be a smooth manifold and let
G
be a Lie
group acting on
F
. Let
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and

(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
be orbi-bundle atlases for

\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
with generic fiber
F
and structure
group
G
.

(1)

The orbi-bundle atlas

(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})

refines

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
if every local trivializing
l.u.c. lying in
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
admits an
embedding into some local trivializing
l.u.c. lying in
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

(2)

The orbi-bundle atlases

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and

(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2})
are
equivalent
if they admit
a common refinement.

(3)

An
orbi-bundle
with generic fiber
F
and
structure group
G
is a
surjective continuous map
\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
endowed
with an equivalence class of orbi-bundle
atlases with generic fiber
F
and
structure group
G
. To simplify notation, we denote an
orbi-bundle by
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
.

Remark 1.52
.

By Remark
1.48
, Definition

1.51
agrees with the notion of
orbi-bundles in
[
7
, Section 4.1]
and
[
14
, Section
2.1]
. Moreover, an orbi-bundle atlas can be understood in
terms of the analog of transition maps (see
[
14
, page
123]
): this comes from unraveling the compatibility
condition between the atlases
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
in
Definition
1.49
.

Remark 1.53
.

If
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an orbi-bundle, then
\mathrm{pr}

is a good
C^{\infty}
map (see
[
7
, Remark 4.4.12b]
).

In analogy with the case of manifolds, if
F
is a vector space and

G
is (a subgroup of)
\mathrm{GL}(F)
, we call an orbi-bundle with
generic fiber
F
and structure group
G
a
vector orbi-bundle
.

Remark 1.54
.

If
M
is an orbifold, one can construct the
tangent

orbi-bundle
TM\to M
in a natural fashion (see, for instance,

[
1
, Section 1.3]
). The fiber
T_{x}M
over
x\in M
can be identified with the following orbi-vector space: If
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)

is a l.u.c. centered at
x
and
\widehat{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Remark
1.20
), then
T_{x}M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
. If
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a good
C^{\infty}

map, then there is a well-defined notion of
derivative
of
f

at
x\in M_{1}
, i.e., a
\Gamma
-equivariant linear map
d_{x}f:T_{x}M_{1}\to T_{f(x)}M_{2}
, where
\Gamma
is the orbifold structure
group of
x
(see
[
26
, Section 2.1]
).

Example 1.55

(Standard vector orbi-bundles
over orbifolds)
.

All the standard operations on
vector bundles over manifolds extend to vector orbi-bundles over
orbifolds (see
[
1
, Section 1.3]
). In particular, given an
orbifold
M
, one can construct the
cotangent

orbi-bundle
T^{*}M\to M
and all tensor bundles over
M
.
\Diamond

The construction of orbifolds using locally free and proper actions
described in Section
1.1.1
can be generalized to
construct orbi-bundles. For our purposes, we need only the following
special case: Let
\widehat{M},F
be smooth manifolds, let

G
be a Lie group acting on
F
and let
H
be a Lie group. We
suppose that

•

H
acts on
\widehat{M}
,

•

there exists a smooth map
\rho:H\times\widehat{M}\to G

such that the map

\begin{split}H\times\widehat{M}\times F&\to\widehat{M}\times F\\
(h,\widehat{x},y)&\mapsto(h\cdot x,\rho(h,\widehat{x})(y))\end{split}

defines an
H
-action on
\widehat{M}\times F
, where
\cdot

denotes the
H
-action on
\widehat{M}
,

•

the
H
-action on
\widehat{M}
is locally free, and

•

the
H
-actions on
\widehat{M}
and on
\widehat{M}\times F

are proper.

Since the
H
-actions on
\widehat{M}
and
\widehat{M}\times F
are
locally free and proper, we obtain orbifolds
\widehat{M}/H
and

(\widehat{M}\times F)/H
. Moreover, there is a surjective continuous
map
(\widehat{M}\times F)/H\to\widehat{M}/H
such that the
following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\widehat{M}\times F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\widehat{M}\times F)/H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}/H,}

where the horizontal arrows are the quotient maps and the vertical
arrow on the left is projection onto the first component. Using the
standard l.u.c.’s on
\widehat{M}/H
and

(\widehat{M}\times F)/H
(described in Section
1.1.1
), it can be checked directly that

(\widehat{M}\times F)/H\to\widehat{M}/H
is an orbi-bundle with
generic fiber
F
and structure group
G
. The following example gives
a concrete application of this construction.

Example 1.56

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying

\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
. Fix an integer
k
and consider the

\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}
given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{*}\times(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle(w,z_{0},\dots,z_{n},v)

\displaystyle\mapsto(w^{m_{0}}z_{0},\dots,w^{m_{n}}z_{n},\lambda^{k}v),

(cf. (
1.5
)). Together with the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
given in Example

1.42
, these two
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action satisfy the above
hypotheses, where
G=\mathbb{C}^{*}=\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C})
. The resulting complex vector
orbi-bundle

((\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{C}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})

of rank 1 is denoted by

\mathcal{O}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{n}}(k)
. Finally, we observe that it is
also possible to construct
\mathcal{O}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{n}}(k)
as an
appropriate
S^{1}
-quotient of the product
E\times\mathbb{C}
, where
E

is as in Example
1.11
. We leave the details of this
latter construction to the reader (see Example

1.42
).
\Diamond

Remark 1.57
.

Given an orbi-bundle
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
, there is a natural
notion of a
C^{\infty}
section
of
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
(see
[
7
, Section 4.1]
): This is a
C^{\infty}
map

s:M_{2}\to M_{1}
that is a set-theoretic section of
\mathrm{pr}

such that the
C^{\infty}
lifts of
s
in local trivializing
l.u.c.’s for
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
are equivariant sections. By
[
7
, Remark 4.4.12b]
,
any
C^{\infty}
section of
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is good.

In particular, it makes sense to define
tensors
on an orbifold

M
as
C^{\infty}
sections of the appropriate vector
orbi-bundle constructed in Remark
1.55
(see,
for instance,
[
1
, Definition 1.27]
). Moreover, the
de
Rham differential

d:\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\to\Omega^{\bullet+1}(M)
can be defined in the same way as for manifolds. Hence, it
also makes sense to consider closed and exact forms, as well as de
Rham cohomology (see
[
1
, Chapter 2.1]
for an approach
involving Lie groupoids). Finally,
integration
of differential
forms can be defined using the standard formulas.

Remark 1.58
.

By Remark
1.57
, we may consider a vector field
\xi
on an
orbifold
M
. The naive definition of the
flow
of
\xi
on
M

makes sense and satisfies the standard properties (see

[
19
]
for a more sophisticated approach using Lie
groupoids). If
M
has isolated singular points, this is
particularly simple using l.u.c.’s and their compatibility. In particular, it can be shown that the standard Cartan
calculus holds for orbifolds.

To conclude this section, we introduce
principal

G
-orbi-bundles
: These are orbi-bundles in the sense of Definition

1.51
, where
F=G
and
G
acts on
itself by left multiplication. In fact, just as for manifolds, it is
possible to go from orbi-bundles over
M
with typical fiber
F
and
structure group
G
to principal
G
-orbi-bundles by means of the
standard construction and vice versa (see, for instance,
[
14
, Section
2.1]
). In particular, this establishes the ‘standard’ correspondence
between complex line orbi-bundles and principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundles over orbisurfaces (see, for example,
[
12
]
).

1.2.2.
Seifert manifolds

To start, we define certain 3-manifolds endowed with an

S^{1}
-action, first introduced by Seifert in
[
48
]
.

Definition 1.59
.

A
Seifert manifold
is a 3-dimensional compact, connected oriented manifold

M
endowed with an effective, locally free,
orientation-preserving
S^{1}
-action. We call the
orbit map
\pi:M\rightarrow M/S^{1}
a
Seifert
fibration
.

Remark 1.60
.

Given a Seifert fibration
\pi:M\to M/S^{1}
, the construction
illustrated in Section
1.1.1
yields that
orbit space
\Sigma:=M/S^{1}
is a compact, connected oriented
orbi-surface.

Seifert manifolds and fibrations have been classified by
Raymond
[
44
]
. For completeness, in what follows we
illustrate this classification (see also
[
4
, Section I.3.c]

and
[
20
,
42
]
). Let
\pi:M\rightarrow\Sigma
be a
Seifert fibration. Since
S^{1}
is abelian, it makes sense to consider
the stabilizer of an orbit
S^{1}\cdot x
, where
x\in M
, which we
denote by
\Gamma
. Since the
S^{1}
-action on
M
is effective, the
only possible stabilizers are the trivial subgroup and the cyclic
subgroups
\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
given by
\alpha
-th roots of unity, where

\alpha>1
is an integer. In particular, every orbit is
diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
. We say that an orbit
S^{1}\cdot x
is
principal
(respectively
exceptional
) if its stabilizer is
trivial (respectively
\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
).

The Slice
Theorem (see
[
8
, Theorem
2.4.1]
) provides a description of
\pi:M\to\Sigma
near any
orbit, which we illustrate below. To this end, we fix
x\in M
and let

D^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
be an open disc centred at the
origin. Here,
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is identified with the normal space to the orbit

S^{1}\cdot x
at
x
. Since the
S^{1}
-action is
orientation-preserving, the image of the isotropy representation
\rho(\Gamma)
lies in
\mathrm{SO}(2)\simeq\mathrm{U}(1)
, so that

\rho
is completely determined by
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\rho(\gamma)=\gamma^{\nu}
, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma\subset S^{1}
. We consider the
\Gamma
-action on
S^{1}\times D^{2}

given by

(1.6)

\gamma\cdot(\lambda,w)=(\lambda\gamma^{-1},\gamma^{\nu}z).

This action is free and proper so that the quotient
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma
is a smooth manifold. We endow
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma
with the
S^{1}
-action given by

e^{i\theta}\cdot[\lambda,w]=[e^{i\theta}\lambda,w].

By the Slice Theorem, an

S^{1}
-invariant neighborhood of
S^{1}\cdot x
is
S^{1}
-equivariantly
isomorphic to
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma
. In particular, since the
S^{1}
-action on
M
is
effective, it follows that
\nu\neq 0
and, if
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}

for some
\alpha>1
, then
\gcd(\alpha,\nu)=1
. In the latter case
(i.e., if the orbit is exceptional),
there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma^{\nu}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{\alpha}}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\gcd(\alpha,\beta)=1
such that (
1.6
) becomes

e^{\frac{2\pi i}{\alpha}}\cdot(\lambda,w)=\left(\lambda e^{-\frac{2\pi i\beta}{\alpha}},e^{\frac{2\pi i}{\alpha}}w\right).

If we choose
0<\beta<\alpha
, we obtain the
Seifert
invariant
of an exceptional orbit, which we denote by
(\alpha,\beta)
.

Remark 1.61
.

A closer look at the above argument yields the following extra
results:

(1)

exceptional orbits are isolated in
\Sigma
, and

(2)

locally near an orbit with stabilizer
\Gamma
(and isotropy
representation determined by the integer
\nu
), the Seifert
fibration
\pi:M\to\Sigma
is isomorphic to the map
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma\to D^{2}/\Gamma
sending
[\lambda,w]\to[w]
, where
the
\Gamma
-action on
D^{2}
is given by the second component of
(
1.6
).

In particular, since exceptional orbits are precisely the singular points
of the orbifold
\Sigma
, by property
(1)
the orbifold structure on
B
has only isolated
singular points. In particular, by Remark

1.60
and part
(1)
of Theorem

1.44
, there exists a non-negative integer
g

such that
|B|
is homeomorphic to the compact orientable surface of
genus
g
. Finally, by property
(2)
,
\pi:M\to\Sigma
is a principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundle.

Remark 1.62
.

Let
S^{1}\cdot x
be an exceptional orbit with stabilizer
\Gamma

and Seifert invariant

(\alpha,\beta)
. It can be shown that the quotient
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma
is, in fact,
S^{1}
-equivariantly
isomorphic to a solid torus
S^{1}\times D^{2}
endowed with an
S^{1}
-action that is not trivial on both components – for
more details, see
[
4
, Section I.3.c]
. Moreover, this identification can be extended to an identification
between
(S^{1}\times\overline{D}^{2})/\Gamma
and
S^{1}\times\overline{D}^{2}
. Under this identification, the exceptional orbit corresponds to
S^{1}\times\{0\}
and a principal orbit lying on the boundary
S^{1}\times S^{1}
intersects the ‘meridian’
\{1\}\times S^{1}
in precisely

\alpha
points.

By Remark
1.61
, there exists a positive integer

n
such that
\Sigma
has precisely
n
singular points, that we denote by

[x_{1}],\dots,[x_{n}]
for some
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in M
. Let
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
denote the Seifert invariant of
S^{1}\cdot x_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. The remaining invariant of the Seifert
fibration
\pi:M\to\Sigma
is encoded by how the neighborhoods of the
exceptional orbits are ‘glued in’. To make this precise, for each

i=1,\ldots,n
, let
\Gamma_{i}
be the stabilizer of
x_{i}
and let
\mathcal{V}_{i}\subset M
be an
S^{1}
-invariant
open neighborhood of
[x]
that is
S^{1}
-equivariantly
diffeomorphic to
(S^{1}\times D^{2})/\Gamma_{i}
. Moreover, we fix a point

x_{0}\in M
with trivial stabilizer and let
\mathcal{V}_{0}\subset M

be an open neighborhood of
[x_{0}]
in
M
. (The reason for
this extra neighborhood becomes clear in what follows.) Finally, we choose

\mathcal{V}_{0},\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{n}
sufficiently
small so that their closures are pairwise disjoint. For
i=0,\ldots,n
, we set
D_{i}:=\pi(\mathcal{V}_{i})\subset\Sigma
; by construction, these are homeomorphic to
open discs centered at the respective orbits that have closures that are pairwise
disjoint. By Remark
1.61
, the complement
\Sigma\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{D}_{i}
is a surface with vanishing
second cohomology. Moreover, the restriction

(1.7)

\pi:M\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\to\Sigma\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{D}_{i}

is a principal
S^{1}
-bundle. Hence, it is a trivial principal

S^{1}
-bundle. In particular, by Remark

1.62
, in order to reconstruct the
manifold
M
from the non-negative integers
g,n
and the Seifert invariants

(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\ldots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n})
, it suffices to
understand how the closed solid tori
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{0},\ldots,\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
are glued to
M\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

To understand the gluing maps, we start by observing that, since
(
1.7
) is trivial, there exists a smooth section

\sigma:\Sigma\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{D}_{i}\to M\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
. Moreover,
\sigma
extends to
the closure
\Sigma\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}D_{i}
of
\Sigma\smallsetminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}\overline{D}_{i}
; by a slight abuse of
notation, we denote the extension also by
\sigma
. We set
\partial_{i}\sigma:=\sigma(\partial\overline{D}_{i})
and let
b_{i}\in\partial\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
be a principal orbit (see Remark

1.62
), for
i=0,\ldots,n
. Since

\sigma
is a section, the homology classes of
\partial_{i}\sigma
and
b_{i}
form a basis of
H_{1}(\partial\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i})
. A different basis of
H_{1}(\partial\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i})
is given by the homology classes of a choice of meridian
a_{i}
and
parallel
\ell_{i}
, which are the ‘standard’ ones for

\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
and the ones given by the images of
\{1\}\times S^{1}
and
S^{1}\times\{1\}
under the identification of Remark

1.62
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. (In what
follows we abuse notation and denote a curve and its homology class by
the same symbol.) Since
\partial\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\simeq S^{1}\times S^{1}
, the isotopy
class of the gluing map of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is determined by
the induced automorphism on
H_{1}(\partial\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i})
, which, in turn, is codified by how the basis

a_{i},\ell_{i}
is related to the basis
\partial_{i}\sigma
and

b_{i}
. It can be shown (see
[
4
, Section I.3.c]
), that there
exists
\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the section
\sigma
can be chosen to
satisfy
2
2

2

In some sense, the appearance of
\beta_{0}
is so that
(
1.8
) holds and can be thought of as a ‘balancing’ constant. This justifies the removal of an extra solid
torus in the beginning of the discussion.

(1.8)

\begin{split}a_{0}&=\partial\sigma_{0}-\beta_{0}b\\
a_{i}&=\alpha_{i}\partial\sigma_{i}-\beta_{i}b_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq n.\end{split}

Moreover, together with the identification of Remark
1.62
, (
1.8
) determines how
a_{i},\ell_{i}
is related to the basis
\partial_{i}\sigma
and

b_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,n
.

Definition 1.63
.

Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration. Its
Seifert
invariant
is given by

(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n})).

Remark 1.64
.

If two Seifert fibrations have equal Seifert invariants, then the
total spaces are diffeomorphic. Conversely, given any suitable
abstract data
(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}))
, there
exists a Seifert fibration with the above as its Seifert invariant
(see
[
3
, Theorem I.3.7]
).

Remark 1.65
.

If a Seifert fibration
\pi:M\to\Sigma
has Seifert invariant
(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}))
, then the
compact, connected orientable orbi-surface
\Sigma
only has isolated
singular points (see Remark
1.61
). Hence, by
Theorem
1.44
,
\Sigma

is classified by
g
,
the non-negative integer
n
and the pairs
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n})
.

Following
[
5
, Section 3]
, we state a result that shows how the Seifert
invariant of a Seifert fibration
\pi:M\to\Sigma
determines the Euler
class of
\pi:M\to\Sigma
. First, we need to define the latter
notion. Instead of using a conceptually clearer but more involved
approach (as in, e.g.,
[
17
]
), we follow a simpler,
ad
hoc
path as
in
[
4
, Section I.3.d]
or
[
5
, Section 3]
. Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration with Seifert invariant
(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}))
. Let
\alpha=\mathrm{lcm}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
, where
\alpha:=1
if

n=0
. We set
M^{\prime}:=M/\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
acts on
M
via
the inclusion in
S^{1}
. By
[
4
, Proposition I.3.8]
, the
quotient
M^{\prime}
can be endowed with a structure of smooth manifold such
that

•

the quotient map
q:M\to M^{\prime}
is smooth,

•

there is a free and proper smooth action of
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
on

M^{\prime}
, and

•

the quotient map is equivariant with respect to the projection
S^{1}\to S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha}
.

In the presence of exceptional orbits, the quotient map
q
fails to
be a covering; in fact, it is a
branched
covering along the
exceptional orbits. (It is what is known as an
orbifold covering
map
, see Definition
1.70
below.) Together with the second bullet point above, this
implies that the base space of the principal
S^{1}
-bundle
\pi^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}/(S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{\alpha})
can be identified
with
|\Sigma|
. Abusing notation, we denote
|\Sigma|
with the resulting
manifold structure by
B
. If
e^{\prime}\in H^{2}(|\Sigma|;\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
denotes the Euler
class of
\pi^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\to\Sigma
, then we define the
Euler class
of

\pi:M\to\Sigma
to be
e:=e^{\prime}/\alpha\in H^{2}(|\Sigma|;\mathbb{Q})\simeq\mathbb{Q}

(cf.
[
4
, Proposition I.3.9]
).

Proposition 1.66

(Section 4 in
[
5
]
)
.

The Euler class
e
of a Seifert fibration with Seifert invariant
(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}))

is given by

(1.9)

e=\beta_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}.

Sketch of proof.

The idea is to calculate
e
using standard obstruction theory as in
