the manifold case, using
[
5
, Section 3]
. We fix the notation
as in the derivation of the Seifert invariant of a Seifert
fibration. Since all meridians are homologous to zero in
M
,
(
1.8
) implies that

(1.10)

\partial\sigma_{0}=\beta_{0}b_{0}\text{ and }\partial\sigma_{i}=\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}b_{i}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n.

Any two principal orbits are homologous in
M
: To see this, first one can
prove it for any two principal orbits that are contained in an

S^{1}
-invariant open neighborhood of a principal orbit and then use
the fact that the open subset of principal orbits is path-connected
in
M
. Hence, using (
1.10
) and the fact that
\partial\sigma=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}\partial\sigma_{i}
, we obtain that

\partial\sigma=\left(\beta_{0}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\beta_{i}}{\alpha_{i}}\right)b_{0}.

Unraveling the obstruction-theoretic definition of
e
given in

[
5
, Section 3]
, the desired result follows.
∎

Remark 1.67
.

Under the correspondence between complex line orbi-bundles and principal

S^{1}
-orbi-bundles over (compact, connected orientable)
orbi-surfaces (see Section
1.2.1
), the Euler
class of a Seifert fibration is equal to the
first Chern class
(or degree)
of the corresponding complex line orbi-bundle. (For
our purposes, we take this to be a
definition
of the latter,
which can be alternatively defined intrinsically using the ideas in
[
17
]
.)

Remark 1.68

(Seifert orbifolds)
.

The above discussion of Seifert manifolds can be generalized to
Seifert orbifolds
(see
[
5
, Sections 2 and
3]
): the former are 3-dimensional, compact connected oriented
orbifolds that are the total spaces of principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundles
over compact, connected oriented orbi-surfaces that have only
isolated singular points. Such a principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundle
\pi:M\to\Sigma
is called a
Seifert orbi-fibration
. Associated
to
\pi:M\to\Sigma
is a Seifert invariant given as in
Definition
1.63
and the Euler class of
\pi:M\to\Sigma
can be obtained from the Seifert invariant by
(
1.9
).

To conclude this section, we use Seifert manifolds to construct
certain four-dimensional orbifolds that play an important role in our
paper.

Example 1.69

(From Seifert fibrations to projectivized plane bundles)
.

Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration
and let
\mathrm{pr}:L\to\Sigma
be the corresponding complex
line orbi-bundle. The
projectivization

\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})\to\Sigma
is an orbifold that is the total space of an
orbi-bundle with generic fiber
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. To see this, we consider
the manifold (!)
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
endowed with the following

S^{1}
-action:

(1.11)

\lambda\cdot(x,[z_{0}:z_{1}])=(\lambda x,[z_{0}:\lambda z_{1}]).

Since the
S^{1}
-action on
M
is locally free, so is this

S^{1}
-action on
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. Hence, the quotient
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
is an orbifold. In fact, since the exceptional orbits in

M
are isolated, it follows that
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
has isolated singular points. The set of singular points
is

\begin{split}&\{[x,[0:1]]\in(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}\mid[x]\text{ is singular in }\Sigma\}\\
\cup&\{[x,[1:0]]\in(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}\mid[x]\text{ is singular in }\Sigma\}.\end{split}

Moreover, since the above
S^{1}
-action preserves the projection
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\Sigma
, there is a
C^{\infty}
map
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}\to\Sigma
that can be checked to be an orbi-bundle with
generic fiber
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. It remains to show that the total space of
this orbi-bundle can be identified with
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})
. To this end, we use the idea of
[
4
, Lemma VIII.2.4]
:
the map
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}\to\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})
that sends
[x,[z_{0}:z_{1}]]
to
[xz_{0}:z_{1}]
is the desired
identification.
\Diamond

1.2.3.
Orbifold covering maps

For our purposes, it is also useful to introduce orbi-bundles with
zero dimensional
disconnected
fibers. To this end, we follow

[
5
, Section 1]
and
[
51
, Section 13.2]
.

Definition 1.70
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be orbifolds. A surjective continuous map

\mathrm{pr}:|M_{1}|\to|M_{2}|
is an
orbifold covering map
if,
for any
x\in M_{2}
, there exists an open neighborhood
U\subseteq M_{2}
of
x
and a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
uniformizing
U
such that, for any
connected component
V
of
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(U)
, there exist a
subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
and a
l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma^{\prime},\psi)
uniformizing
V
with
\varphi=\mathrm{pr}\circ\psi
. We denote such a map by
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
.

In other words, orbifold covering maps are locally modeled on taking
quotients of subgroups. Hence, in general, an orbifold covering
map fails to be a topological covering map, but it is one away from
singular points. Moreover, any orbifold covering map is automatically
a good
C^{\infty}
map.

If
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an orbifold covering map and the set
of singular points in
M_{2}
has codimension at least two (as in the
case that is of most interest to us), then there exists
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
such that, for any regular
x\in M_{2}
,
the cardinality of
\mathrm{pr}^{-1}(x)
equals
r
. Following
[
5
, Section 1]
, we call
r
the

geometric degree
of
\mathrm{pr}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
.

Example 1.71

(An orbifold covering map for the weighted projective line)
.

Let
p,q
be positive integers that are coprime. We claim that the
map

(1.12)

\begin{split}\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}&\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)\\
[z_{0}:z_{1}]&\mapsto[z^{p}_{0}:z^{q}_{1}]\end{split}

is an orbifold covering map of geometric degree
pq
. To see this,
we observe that
\mathrm{pr}
is induced by the smooth map

(1.13)

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}&\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}\\
(z_{0},z_{1})&\mapsto(z^{p}_{0},z^{q}_{1}).\end{split}

This map of (
1.13
) is surjective; hence so is
\mathrm{pr}
. Moreover, it
is a covering map when restricted to

(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
. Hence, the restriction of
\mathrm{pr}
to the
complement of the singular points in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
is an (orbifold)
covering map. Finally, by considering local charts near
[1:0]
and

[0:1]
in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and the standard l.u.c.’s near
[1:0]
and

[0:1]
in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
, it can be checked that
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)

satisfies the condition of Definition

1.70
near the singular points of

\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
. Finally, the geometric degree of
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
is
pq
.

To conclude this section, we use Example
1.71

to compute the degree of the complex line
orbi-bundle
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
(see Example

1.56
and Remark
1.67
). While this result
is known (see
[
12
, Corollary 1.7]
and

[
38
, Proposition 2.0.18]
), we provide a proof for completeness.

Lemma 1.72
.

Let
p,q
be positive integers that are coprime and let
k
be an
integer. The degree of the complex line orbi-bundle

\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
equals
k/pq
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
denote the complex line bundle of
degree
k
. We claim that the map

(1.14)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Pr}:\mathcal{O}(k)&\to\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)\\
[z_{0},z_{1},v]&\mapsto[z^{p}_{0},z^{q}_{1},v]\end{split}

is an orbifold covering map that makes the following diagram commute

(1.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}}

where
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
is the orbifold
covering map of Example
1.71
. This follows
by the same argument as in Example
1.71
once
we observe that
\mathrm{Pr}
is induced by the smooth map

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathrm{Pr}}:(\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}&\to(\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}\\
(z_{0},z_{1},v)&\mapsto(z^{p}_{0},z^{q}_{1},v),\end{split}

which makes the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{Pr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}}

where
\widehat{\mathrm{pr}}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\smallsetminus\{0\}
is given by (
1.13
). Moreover, this
argument also implies that
\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\simeq\mathcal{O}_{k}
.

Let
S(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and

S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
denote the principal

S^{1}
-orbi-bundles associated to
\mathcal{O}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and

\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
respectively. Then

\mathrm{Pr}:\mathcal{O}(k)\to\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)
induces an
orbifold covering map
S(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))

that we also denote by
\mathrm{Pr}
by a slight abuse of
notation. By construction, the analog of the commutative diagram
(
1.15
) holds replacing the top row with
\mathrm{Pr}:S(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Pr}

sends fibers to fibers. Moreover, since
\mathrm{pr}^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\simeq\mathcal{O}_{k}
, the preimage of a fiber of

S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
consisting of regular
points is the disjoint union of
pq
fibers of
S(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. Since
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
has geometric
degree
pq
and since
S(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
has Euler class
equal to
k
, by the covering formula of
[
5
, Section 3]
, the Euler class of
S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
equals
k/pq
. The result follows from Remark
1.67
.
∎

Combining Lemma
1.66
, Remark

1.68
and Lemma
1.72
, we obtain
the following result immediately.

Corollary 1.73
.

Let
p,q
be positive integers that are coprime and let
k
be an
integer. Let
S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
denote the principal

S^{1}
-orbi-bundle associated to
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
and let
(0;b_{0},(p,l_{p}),(q,l_{q}))
be its Seifert
invariant. Then

(1.16)

\frac{k}{pq}=b_{0}+\frac{l_{p}}{p}+\frac{l_{q}}{q}.

1.3.
Suborbifolds

In this section, we follow
[
6
,
36
,
52
]
.

Definition 1.74
.

Let
|M|
be a topological space, let
\mathcal{A}=\{(\widehat{U}_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in A}
be
an orbifold atlas on
|M|
, and let
|N|\subseteq|M|
be a subset
endowed with the subspace topology. A
suborbifold atlas
on

|N|
is a subset
B\subseteq A
together with a family of
connected embedded submanifolds
\{\widehat{V}_{\beta}\subseteq\widehat{U}_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in B}
such that

(1)

for all
\beta\in B
,

\varphi_{\beta}(\widehat{V}_{\beta})
is a non-empty open subset
of
|N|
,

(2)

\bigcup\limits_{\beta\in B}\varphi_{\beta}(\widehat{V}_{\beta})=|N|
, and

(3)

for all
\beta\in B
, there exists a subgroup

\Delta_{\beta}
of
\Gamma_{\beta}
so that

•

\widehat{V}_{\beta}
is
\Delta_{\beta}
-invariant, and

•

if
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\beta}
and
\widehat{x}\in\widehat{V}_{\beta}
are such that
\gamma\cdot\widehat{x}\in\widehat{V}_{\beta}
, then there exists
\delta\in\Delta_{\beta}
with
\gamma\cdot\widehat{x}=\delta\cdot\widehat{x}
.

If, in addition,
\Delta_{\beta}=\Gamma_{\beta}
for all
\beta\in B
, the suborbifold atlas is said to be
full
.

As proved in
[
52
, Proposition 3.3]
, if
M
is an orbifold
and a subset
|N|\subseteq|M|
has a suborbifold atlas, then
|N|

inherits the structure of an orbifold, which we denote by

N
. Moreover, the notion of full suborbifold atlas corresponds to
that of suborbifold atlas introduced in
[
51
, Definition
13.2.7]
.

Definition 1.75
.

Let
M
be an orbifold. A
(full) suborbifold
of
M
is a subset
|N|

that admits a (full) suborbifold atlas.

A full orbifold is also known as a
strong
suborbifold in the
literature (see,
e.g.
[
13
, Part (b) of Definition 2.6]
).

Remark 1.76
.

If
N
is a full suborbifold of
M
, the tangent orbi-bundle to
N
can
be seen as an orbi-subbundle of
TM
and so the
normal
orbi-bundle

\nu_{N}
to
N

in
M
may be defined as usual (see the discussion following

[
13
, Definition 2.6]
).

The proof of the existence of a tubular neighborhood for submanifolds
works
mutatis mutandis
for full suborbifolds. Hence, the
following result holds.

Theorem 1.77

(Tubular neighborhood theorem for full suborbifolds)
.

Let
M
be an orbifold and let
N
be a full suborbifold. Then there
exist an open neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
N
, an open
neighborhood
V\subseteq\nu_{N}
of the zero section and a
diffeomorphism
\varphi:U\to V
that equals the zero section when
restricted to
N
.

Example 1.78

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
, positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n-1}
satisfying

\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n-1})=1
, and a positive integer
m_{n}
. As in the
case of manifolds, the
weighted projective space
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1})
can be
viewed as a (full) suborbifold of
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})
as
follows. We observe that the inclusion
\mathbb{C}^{n}\smallsetminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
given by

(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n-1})\mapsto(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n-1},0)

is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant under the actions on
\mathbb{C}^{n}\smallsetminus\{0\}
and
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}
defined in Example

1.42
. Hence, there is an induced map

\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1})\to\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})
that is, in fact, a topological embedding; the image of this
map is precisely

(1.17)

\{[z_{0}:\ldots:z_{n-1}:z_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})\mid z_{n}=0\}.

It can be checked, either directly, or using
[
6
, Theorem
1]
, that the subset (
1.17
) is a (full) suborbifold of
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})
that is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1})
. The normal orbi-bundle to
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1})
in
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})
can be identified with the complement of
[0:\ldots:0:1]
in
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1},m_{n})
and is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{n-1}}(m_{n})
.
\Diamond

Remark 1.79
.

Let
p,q,k
be pairwise coprime positive integers. We can use Example
1.78
to calculate the
Seifert invariant of
S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
,
since this is the principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundle associated to the
normal bundle to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)
. We know that the
Seifert invariant is of the form
(0;b_{0},(p,l_{p}),(q,l_{q}))
.

First we determine
l_{p}
and
l_{q}
. Since
p,q
are coprime, by
Bézout’s lemma, there exist unique integers
n_{1},n_{2}
with

|n_{1}|<q
and
|n_{2}|<p
such that

(1.18)

n_{1}p+n_{2}q=1.

Moreover, since

p,q>0
,
n_{1}n_{2}<0
. Without loss of generality we may assume
that
n_{1}<0
. If we set
n_{1}^{\prime}:=-n_{1}
,
\beta:=n_{2}k
and

\alpha:=n_{1}^{\prime}k
, then, by (
1.18
),

(1.19)

\displaystyle q\beta-p\alpha=k.

If we view
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)
as
(\mathbb{C}^{3}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{C}^{*}
as
in Example
1.42
, then
l_{p}
(respectively
l_{q}
)
is obtained by understanding the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(respectively

\mathbb{Z}_{q}
) near the point
(1,0,0)
(respectively
(0,1,0)
). If

\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, by (
1.19
),

\lambda\cdot(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=(z_{0},\lambda^{q}z_{1},\lambda^{q\beta}z_{2})\text{ and }\mu\cdot(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})=(\mu^{p}z_{0},z_{1},\mu^{-p\alpha}z_{2})

for any
(z_{0},z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Let

(1.20)

\beta^{\prime}\equiv\beta\mod p\quad\text{and}\quad-\alpha^{\prime}\equiv-\alpha\mod q

be such that
0<\beta^{\prime}<p
and
0<q-\alpha^{\prime}<q
respectively.
Since the two structure groups act with exponent
\beta^{\prime}
and

q-\alpha^{\prime}
in the fiber direction of the normal bundle (given
by the
z_{2}
-coordinate),
l_{p}=\beta^{\prime}
and
l_{q}=q-\alpha^{\prime}
.

It remains to calculate
b_{0}
. To this end, by definition of

\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
and by
(
1.19
), there exists an integer
N
such that

(1.21)

\beta^{\prime}q-\alpha^{\prime}p=k-Npq.

By Corollary
1.73
,

\frac{k}{pq}=b_{0}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{p}+\frac{q-\alpha^{\prime}}{q}.

Hence, by (
1.21
),
b_{0}=N-1
, so that the Seifert
invariant is

(0;N-1,(p,\beta^{\prime}),(q,q-\alpha^{\prime})),

where

\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
are defined in (
1.20
).

If a complex line bundle
L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is
trivial, then the Seifert invariant of the corresponding Seifert
fibration satisfies the following a useful condition.

Lemma 1.80
.

Let
L\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a complex line
orbi-bundle whose associated circle orbi-bundle has Seifert invariant
(0;b,(m,l),(m,\hat{l}))
. If the degree of
L
is zero then
l+\hat{l}=m
.

Proof.

If the degree of
L
is zero, then, by Proposition
1.66
, we have

0=\deg(L)=b+\frac{l+\hat{l}}{m},

and so
l+\hat{l}=\alpha\cdot m
for some positive integer
\alpha
since
1\leq l,\hat{l}<m
. If
\alpha\geq 2
, then we have

l=\alpha\cdot m-\hat{l}\geq 2m-\hat{l}>m

which is impossible. Hence,
\alpha=1
and the result follows.
∎

Example 1.81

(Projectivized plane bundles, continued)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a compact, connected orientable orbi-surface and let

\mathrm{pr}:L\to\Sigma
be a complex line orbi-bundle. As in
the case of manifolds, the
projectivization
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})\to\Sigma
constructed in
Example
1.69
admits two ‘standard’
C^{\infty}

sections, namely
x\mapsto[0:1]
and
x\mapsto[1:0]
, where the
former lifts to a section of the rank-two complex orbi-bundle
L\oplus\mathbb{C}\to\Sigma
and the latter does not. The images of these two
sections are full suborbifolds of
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})
that are
diffeomorphic to
\Sigma
.
\Diamond

1.4.
Group actions on orbifolds

1.4.1.
Definition and examples

Definition 1.82
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and let
M
be an orbifold. A
(smooth)
action
of
G
on
M
is a continuous action of
G
on
|M|
such
that the map

\begin{split}\phi:G\times M&\rightarrow M\\
(g,x)&\mapsto g\cdot x\end{split}

is a good
C^{\infty}
map.

Remark 1.83
.

As in the case of manifolds, given a
G
-action on an orbifold
M
,
any element
\xi
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
gives
rise to a vector field
\xi^{M}
on
M
via the usual formula:

\xi^{M}(x):=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg\rvert_{t=0}\phi(\exp(t\xi),x)\quad\text{for any }x\in M,

where
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
denotes the exponential map.

Example 1.84

(Orbi-vector spaces, continued)
.

Let
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
be an orbi-vector space. We define

\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma):=N(\Gamma)/\Gamma
, where
N(\Gamma)\leq\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V})
is the normalizer of
\Gamma
in

\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V})
. A
representation
of a group
G
on
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is a homomorphism
\rho:G\to\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma)
. If
G
is a Lie group, a smooth action of

G
on
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is
linear
if there exists a representation

\rho
of
G
on
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
such that
\phi(g,x)=\rho(g)(x)
for
all
g\in G
and all
x\in\widehat{V}/\Gamma
. In analogy with
[
29
, Lemma
3.1]
, given a linear action of
G
on
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
,
there exist a Lie group
\widehat{G}
, a representation
\rho:\widehat{G}\to N(\Gamma)
and a short exact sequence of Lie groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GL}(V/\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Moreover,
\rho
is faithful if and only if
\widehat{\rho}
is
faithful.
\Diamond

Many of the smooth actions that we consider in this paper arise from
the following construction: Let
H
be a Lie group that acts properly
and in a locally free fashion on the manifold
\widehat{M}
. Let
G

be a Lie group that acts on
\widehat{M}
so that the
G
- and

H
-actions commute. The action of
g\in G
on
\widehat{x}\in\widehat{M}
is denoted by
g\cdot x
. Then there is a continuous

G
-action on
|\widehat{M}/H|
given by

\begin{split}\phi:G\times|\widehat{M}/H|&\to|\widehat{M}/H|\\
(g,[\widehat{x}])&\mapsto[g\cdot\widehat{x}].\end{split}

By Lemma
1.41
,
\phi
is a good
C^{\infty}

map. We stress that the
G
-action on
\widehat{M}/H
need not be
effective even if the
G
-action on
\widehat{M}
is. The following
examples illustrate the above construction.

Example 1.85

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
, positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying

\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
, and integers
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}
. The map

\begin{split}\phi:S^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})&\to\mathbb{C}P(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})\\
(\lambda,[z_{0}:\ldots:z_{n}])&\mapsto[\lambda^{a_{0}}z_{0}:\ldots:\lambda^{a_{n}}z_{n}]\end{split}

is an
S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}P(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
.
\Diamond

Example 1.86

(Example
1.12
, continued)
.

We let
M=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{4}
be the orbifold
constructed in Example
1.12
. The map

\begin{split}\phi:S^{1}\times M&\to M\\
\left(\lambda,\left[[z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]\right]\right)&\mapsto\left[[\lambda^{1/2}z_{0}:z_{1}],[\lambda^{1/2}w_{0}:w_{1}]\right]\end{split}

is an
S^{1}
-action on
M
. We observe that the expression involving
square roots of elements of
S^{1}
are well-defined because of the
definition of the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-action on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
.
\Diamond

Example 1.87

(Example
1.13
, continued)
.

Given an integer
c>1
, let
M_{c}=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{c}
be the orbifold constructed in Example

1.13
and let
m,n
be positive integers. The map

\begin{split}\phi:S^{1}\times M_{c}&\to M_{c}\\
(\lambda,[[z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]]_{c})&\mapsto[[z_{0}:\lambda^{\frac{m}{c}}z_{1}],[w_{0}:\lambda^{\frac{n}{c}}w_{1}]]_{c}\end{split}

is an
S^{1}
-action on
M_{c}
, where, as in Example

1.86
, there is no ambiguity in the
expressions involving
c
-th roots because of the
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
action on

\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
.
\Diamond

Example 1.88

(Projectivized plane bundles, continued)
.

Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration. The map

\begin{split}\phi:S^{1}\times((M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1})&\to(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}\\
(\lambda,[x,[z_{0}:z_{1}]])&\mapsto[x,[\lambda z_{0}:z_{1}]]\end{split}

is an
S^{1}
-action on the orbifold
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
constructed in Example
1.69

(see (
1.11
) for the
S^{1}
-action on
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
). Let
\mathrm{pr}:L\to\Sigma
be the complex line
orbi-bundle corresponding to
\pi:M\to\Sigma
. Using the
identification between
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})
given in Example

1.69
, we obtain an
S^{1}
-action on the latter. Note that if
[x]\in\Sigma
is a singular point with orbifold
structure group
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, then by (
1.11
)

[x,[\xi_{k}z_{0}:z_{1}]]=[x,[z_{0}:\xi_{k}^{-1}z_{1}]]=[\xi_{k}x,[z_{0}:z_{1}]]=[x,[z_{0}:z_{1}]],

and so each point in the fiber over
[x]
has
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
as stabilizer.

\Diamond

1.4.2.
Linearization at a fixed point

Lemma
1.43
has the
following immediate consequence that is important for our purposes.

Corollary 1.89
.

Let
G
act on an orbifold
M
. If
x,y\in M
lie in the same

G
-orbit, then
\Gamma_{x}\simeq\Gamma_{y}
. In particular, if
M
has
isolated singular points and
G
is
connected, then any singular point in
M
is fixed by the
G
-action.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting on an orbifold
M
. The local structure of a
G
-action in a neighborhood of a fixed point

x\in M
is relevant for the purposes of our paper. First, we observe
that there exists a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)

centered at
x
such that the open neighborhood
U
of
x
that is
uniformized by
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
is
G
-invariant. We call such a l.u.c.
\boldsymbol{G}
-invariant
. Moreover, the
G
-action on
U

can be described entirely by an action of a ‘larger’ Lie group on

\widehat{U}
. More precisely, the following result holds (for a proof, see
[
29
, Section 2]
or

[
13
, Proposition 2.8 and Corollary 2.9]
; cf. Example
1.84
).

Proposition 1.90
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting on an orbifold
M
, let
x\in M
be a fixed point and let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be a
G
-invariant
l.u.c. centered at
x
. There exist a Lie group
\widehat{G}
, a
smooth action of
\widehat{G}
on
\widehat{U}
fixing
0
, and a
short exact sequence of Lie groups

(1.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

such that the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}\times\widehat{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi,\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where
\widehat{\phi}:\widehat{G}\times\widehat{U}\to\widehat{U}
encodes the
\widehat{G}
-action. Moreover,
\Gamma
commutes with every connected subgroup of
\widehat{G}
.

On the other hand, as in the case of manifolds, there is a
linear
G
-action on the tangent space
T_{x}M
given by taking
derivatives of the
G
-action at
x
(see Remark

1.54
and Example

1.84
). The next result explains
the relation between the
G
-action near
x
and the linear
G
-action
on
T_{x}M
; it is the analog of the Bochner linearization theorem for
orbifolds (see
[
8
, Theorem 2.2.1]
).

Corollary 1.91
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group acting on an orbifold
M
. If
x
is a
fixed point, then there exist
G
-invariant open neighborhoods
U\subseteq M
and
W\subseteq T_{x}M
of
x
and
[0]
respectively,
and a
G
-equivariant diffeomorphism
f:U\to W
that sends
x
to
[0]
.

Proof.

Let
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
be a
G
-invariant l.u.c. centered at
x
and let

\widehat{G}
be the Lie group extending
G
given by Proposition

1.90
. Since
\widehat{G}
is compact,
by the Slice Theorem (see
[
8
, Theorem
2.4.1]
), there is no loss of generality in assuming that the

\widehat{G}
-action on
\widehat{U}
is the restriction of a linear

\widehat{G}
-action. By Proposition

1.90
, the map
\overline{\varphi}:\widehat{U}/\Gamma\to U
is a
G
-equivariant
diffeomorphism, where
\widehat{U}/\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
is
endowed with the restriction of a linear
\widehat{G}/\Gamma\simeq G
-action. Since the latter
linear action corresponds to the linear
G
-action on
T_{x}M
under
the isomorphism
T_{x}M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma
, the result follows.
∎

We conclude this section by stating the following consequence of
Corollary
1.91
(see
[
13
, Corollary
2.10]
for a proof).

Corollary 1.92
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group that acts effectively on an orbifold

M
. Any connected component of the fixed point set of the action
that has positive dimension
3
3

3

This restriction is
necessary as we are working only with
effective

orbifolds.
is
a full suborbifold of
M
.

1.4.3.
Quotients of orbifolds by finite
groups

In this subsection we show that the quotient of an orbifold by an
effective action of a finite group inherits the structure of an
orbifold (see Corollary
1.94
below), and then we
illustrate this construction with several examples that are relevant
for our purposes.

Let
M
be an orbifold of dimension
n
. The
orthonormal frame
orbi-bundle

\mathrm{Fr}(M)\to M
can be constructed as in
[
1
, Section
1.3]
: The total space can be obtained by gluing together the
orthonormal frame bundles of l.u.c.’s for
M
. (Alternatively, one can
define a Riemannian metric for an orbifold, prove that any orbifold
admits such a metric and construct the orthonormal frame
orbi-bundle as in the case of manifolds. We leave the details to the reader.) This principal

\mathrm{O}(n)
-orbi-bundle has the following important property (see

[
1
, Theorem 1.23]
for a proof).

Theorem 1.93
.

Let
M
be an orbifold of dimension
n
. The total space

\mathrm{Fr}(M)
of the
orthonormal frame orbi-bundle of
M
is a smooth manifold endowed with an
effective, locally free
\mathrm{O}(n)
-action such that the
quotient orbifold
\mathrm{Fr}(M)/\mathrm{O}(n)
is diffeomorphic to

M
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.93
, every orbifold
M
can be
thought of as a quotient orbifold
\widehat{M}/H
, where
H
is
compact. This fact has the following useful consequence, stated below
without proof (see
[
26
, Remark 2.15]
).

Corollary 1.94
.

If
G
is a finite group acting on an orbifold
M
, then the
quotient
M/G
can be endowed with the structure of an orbifold such
that the quotient map
M\to M/G
is an orbifold covering map.

Example 1.95

(Cyclic quotients of weighted projective lines)
.

Let
p,q
be positive integers that are pairwise coprime and let

a,b
be integers. We consider the following smooth action of

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
:

(1.23)

\begin{split}\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)\\
(\mu,[z_{0}:z_{1}])&\mapsto[\lambda^{a}z_{0}:\lambda^{b}z_{1}].\end{split}

By the weighted homogeneity of the coordinates in
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
,

(1.24)

\mu\cdot[z_{0}:z_{1}]=[z_{0}:\mu^{\frac{bp-aq}{p}}z_{1}]=[\mu^{\frac{aq-bp}{q}}z_{0}:z_{1}]

for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and any
[z_{0}:z_{1}]\in\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
. We set

k_{1}:=|aq-bp|
. A necessary and sufficient condition for this action to be effective
and so that the set of points with non-trivial stabilizer is discrete
is that
\gcd(k_{1},m)=1
. In this case, the only points with
non-trivial stabilizers are
[1:0]
and
[0:1]
. By Corollary

1.94
, the quotient
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b)
inherits the
structure of an orbifold that has two singular points (corresponding
to
[1:0]
and
[0:1]
), and with respect
to which the quotient map
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b)
is
an orbifold covering map. This orbifold structure can also be obtained
as a quotient orbifold (see Example

1.105
).

We claim that the orbifold structure group of the singular point
corresponding to
[1:0]
is
\mathbb{Z}_{mp}
. To see this, we consider the
standard
\mathbb{Z}_{mp}
-action on
\mathbb{C}
and observe that an open
neighborhood of
[1:0]\in\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)
is diffeomorphic to an open
neighborhood of
[0]\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
acts on
\mathbb{C}
via the
inclusion
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{mp}
. Since
\gcd(k_{1},m)=1
, the residual
\mathbb{Z}_{mp}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
can be identified with the

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
given by (
1.24
). Analogously, the structure group of the singular point
corresponding to
[0:1]
is
\mathbb{Z}_{mq}
. In particular, by Theorem

1.44
, if
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
are also such that

k^{\prime}_{1}:=|a^{\prime}q-b^{\prime}p|
is coprime with
m
, then
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b)

and
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
are diffeomorphic. Hence, we write
simply
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
for this quotient.

Example 1.96

(Complex
line bundles over cyclic quotients of weighted projective lines)
.

In analogy with Example
1.95
, let
p,q,k
be positive integers that are pairwise coprime and let

a,b,c
be integers. We consider the following smooth action of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
on
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)
:

(1.25)

\begin{split}\phi:\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)\\
(\lambda,[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}])&\mapsto[\lambda^{a}z_{0}:\lambda^{b}z_{1}:\lambda^{c}z_{2}].\end{split}

By the weighted homogeneity of the coordinates in

\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)
,

(1.26)

\begin{split}\lambda\cdot[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]&=[z_{0}:\lambda^{\frac{bp-aq}{p}}z_{1}:\lambda^{\frac{cp-ak}{p}}z_{2}]\\
&=[\lambda^{\frac{aq-bp}{q}}z_{0}:z_{1}:\lambda^{\frac{cq-bk}{q}}z_{2}]\\
&=[\lambda^{\frac{ak-cp}{k}}z_{0}:\lambda^{\frac{bk-cq}{k}}z_{1}:z_{2}],\end{split}

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and any

[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)
.
We set
k_{1}:=|aq-bp|,k_{2}:=|bk-cq|
and
k_{3}:=|cp-ak|
. A necessary and sufficient condition for this action to be effective
and so that the set of points with non-trivial stabilizer is discrete
is

\gcd(k_{1},m)=\gcd(k_{2},m)=\gcd(k_{3},m)=1.

In this case, the points that have non-trivial stabilizers are

[1:0:0]
,
[0:1:0]
and
[0:0:1]
. By Corollary

1.94
, the quotient
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)

inherits the structure of an orbifold that has three singular points,
corresponding to
[1:0:0]
,
[0:1:0]
and
[0:0:1]
. This
orbifold structure can also be realized as a quotient orbifold (see
Example
1.105
below). By (
1.26
),
the orbifold structure groups of the singular points
corresponding to
[1:0:0]
,
[0:1:0]
and
[0:0:1]
are

\mathbb{Z}_{mp}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{mq}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{mk}
.

In analogy with Example
1.78
, the subset
of
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)
given by
\{[z_{0}:z_{1}:0]\in\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)\}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}

is a (full) suborbifold that can be identified with the orbifold

\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}=\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b)
of Example
1.95
. The
normal orbi-bundle to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)
is
precisely
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, where the

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)
is given by a formula entirely
analogous to (
1.25
).
\Diamond

For our purposes, we need to compute the degree of the complex line
orbi-bundle
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}

of Example
1.96
and to relate it to the Seifert invariant of the corresponding circle
bundle. The first of these results can be proved with the techniques of Lemma

1.72
using the orbifold covering map
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Hence, we omit its proof.

Lemma 1.97
.

Let
p,q,k
be positive integers that are pairwise coprime, let
m

be a positive integer, and let

a,b,c
be integers such that

\gcd(|aq-bp|,m)=\gcd(|bk-cq|,m)=\gcd(|cp-ak|,m)=1.

The degree of the complex line orbi-bundle

\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}

is
k/pqm
.

Combining Lemma
1.66
, Remark

1.68
and Lemma
1.97
, the
following result holds.

Corollary 1.98
.

Let
p,q,k
be positive integers that are pairwise coprime, let
m

be a positive integer, and let

a,b,c
be integers such that

\gcd(|aq-bp|,m)=\gcd(|bk-cq|,m)=\gcd(|cp-ak|,m)=1.

Let
S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
denote the principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundle
associated to
\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and let

(0;b_{0},(mp,l_{mp}),(mq,l_{mq}))

be the Seifert invariant of
S(\mathcal{O}_{p,q}(k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c))\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Then

(1.27)

\frac{k}{pqm}=b_{0}+\frac{l_{mp}}{mp}+\frac{l_{mq}}{mq}.

1.5.
Symplectic orbifolds and Hamiltonian
actions

1.5.1.
Definition and Examples

The definition of a symplectic form carries on from manifolds to
orbifolds.

Definition 1.99
.

Let
M
be an orbifold. A
symplectic form
on
M
is a closed,
non-degenerate 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
. A
symplectic
orbifold
is a pair
(M,\omega)
, where
\omega
is a symplectic
form on
M
. A
symplectomorphism
between two symplectic
orbifolds
(M_{1},\omega_{1}),(M_{2},\omega_{2})
is a diffeomorphism

\varphi:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
that satisfies

\varphi^{*}\omega^{\prime}=\omega
. We denote a symplectomorphism by
\varphi:(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to(M_{2},\omega_{2})
.

As for manifolds, the existence of a non-degenerate
2-form on a connected orbifold
M
implies that the dimension of
M

is even. The simplest examples of symplectic orbifolds are
linear.

Example 1.100

(Symplectic orbi-vector spaces)
.

Let
(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
be a real symplectic vector space
and let
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})<\mathrm{GL}(V)

be the subgroup of linear maps that are symplectomorphisms. If
\Gamma<\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
is a
finite subgroup, then the orbi-vector space
\widehat{V}/\Gamma

inherits a symplectic form
\omega
. We call
(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
a
symplectic
orbi-vector space.

Remark 1.101
.

Let
(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
be as in Example

1.100
and let
\widehat{J}
be a
\Gamma
-invariant (almost) complex structure
on
\widehat{V}
that is compatible with
\widehat{\omega}
. (Such a
structure exists since
\Gamma
is compact.) This allows us to consider
\widehat{V}
as a Hermitian vector
space. Since the unitary group

\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})
is the maximal compact
subgroup of
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
(see

[
33
, Proposition 2.22]
),
\Gamma<\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})
. In particular, the real orbi-vector
space
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
can be endowed with the structure of a

Hermitian
orbi-vector spaces.

There are two basic constructions of symplectic orbifolds starting
from symplectic manifolds, which should be thought of as the analogs
of global quotients and quotients in the symplectic category (see Section

1.1.1
). Let
(\widehat{M},\widehat{\omega})
be
a symplectic manifold. First, we suppose that
\Gamma
is a finite group that acts on

\widehat{M}
effectively and by symplectomorphisms. The global
quotient
M:=\widehat{M}/\Gamma
inherits a symplectic form

\omega
; this is clearly the analog of a global quotient. More generally, we suppose that
G
is a Lie group that acts properly and
in a locally free fashion on
\widehat{M}
so that the action is
Hamiltonian
, i.e., there exists a smooth
G
-equivariant map
\Phi:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
to the dual of the Lie algebra of
G
, called
moment map
, such that

\omega(\xi^{M},\cdot)=d\langle\xi,\Phi\rangle\quad\text{ for
all }\xi\in\mathfrak{g},

where
G
acts on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with the coadjoint action,
\xi^{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is the vector field determined by
\xi
as in
Remark
1.83
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the natural pairing

\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}
. If
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
regular value that is fixed by the coadjoint action of
G
, then the

reduced space

M_{\alpha}:=\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)/G
is an orbifold
that inherits a symplectic form
\omega_{\alpha}
(see
[
32
]

and
[
29
, Lemma 3.9]
). This is the analog of a quotient
in symplectic geometry. The following examples are various instances of the above two constructions.

Example 1.102

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying
\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
. We
endow
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
with the standard symplectic form
\omega_{0}
. The

S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
given by (
1.2
) is
Hamiltonian. Hence,
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
inherits a symplectic
form
\omega
, which we call the
standard
symplectic form
on
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
. We observe that, if

m_{i}=1
for all
i=0,\ldots,n
, then
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})\simeq\mathbb{C}P^{n}
and
\omega
is the Fubini-Study form.

Example 1.103

(Example
1.12
, continued)
.

We let
M=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{4}
be the orbifold
constructed in Example
1.12
. We endow
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
with the symplectic form
\widehat{\omega}
given by taking the
product of the Fubini-Study form on each factor. Since the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-action
of (
1.3
) is symplectic,
M
inherits a symplectic form
\omega
.

Example 1.104

(Example
1.13
, continued)
.

Given an integer
c>1
, let
M_{c}=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{c}
be the orbifold constructed in Example

1.13
. We endow
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
with the same symplectic form
\widehat{\omega}
as in Example

1.103
. Since the
\mathbb{Z}_{c}
-action of (
1.4
) is
symplectic,
M_{c}
inherits a symplectic form
\omega_{c}
.

Example 1.105

(Cyclic quotients of weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

The orbifolds
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}=\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b)
and
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)
of Examples
1.95
and

1.96
admit symplectic forms. To see this, we
present them as quotient orbifolds. In what follows, we deal only
with
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, as the argument for
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,q,k)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}(a,b,c)
is entirely analogous. Moreover, we fix the
notation and conditions of Example
1.95
.

We start by considering
\mathbb{C}^{2}
endowed with the standard symplectic
form
\omega_{0}
. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times S^{1}
act on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

(1.28)

\begin{split}\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times S^{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
((\lambda,\mu),(z_{0},z_{1}))&\mapsto(\lambda^{a}\mu^{p}z_{0},\lambda^{b}\mu^{q}z_{1}),\end{split}

(cf. (
1.2
) and (
1.23
)). This action is locally free
and Hamiltonian with moment map
H:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
given
by

H(z_{0},z_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\left(p|z_{0}|^{2}+q|z_{1}|^{2}\right).

Since
pq\neq 0
,
pq
is a regular value of
H
. Hence, the reduced space

M_{pq}(a,b)
is an orbifold that inherits a symplectic form

\omega_{\mathrm{red}}
. The techniques used in Example

1.42
can be used to show that
M_{pq}(a,b)

is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Throughout this paper,
we use this diffeomorphism to identify
M_{pq}(a,b)
with
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(p,q)/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, trusting that this does not cause confusion.

Next we show that certain projectivized plane
bundles over orbi-surfaces admit a symplectic form (see Example

1.69
).

Example 1.106

(Projectivized plane bundles, continued)
.

Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration with Euler class
different from zero and such that
M
is a manifold. Let

\mathrm{pr}:L\to\Sigma
be the corresponding complex line
orbi-bundle. We aim to construct a family of symplectic forms on the
total space of the projectivization
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})\to\Sigma
. To this end, we fix the identification between
\mathbb{P}(L\oplus\mathbb{C})
and the
quotient
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
as in Example

1.69
.

Since the Euler class of
\pi:M\to\Sigma
does not vanish, by

[
25
, Theorem 1.4]
, there exists an
S^{1}
-invariant 1-form
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(M)
such that
\lambda(X^{M})=1
, where
X^{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is the infinitesimal generator of the

S^{1}
-action. (This can be thought of as a connection 1-form on the
Seifert fibration.) Moreover, in analogy with the case of principal

S^{1}
-bundles over manifolds, since the Euler class of
\pi:M\to\Sigma
does not vanish, there exists a
symplectic form

\omega_{\Sigma}\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)
such that
d\lambda=\pi^{*}\omega_{\Sigma}
. (The 1-form
\lambda
is a so-called
Besse
contact form
, see
[
25
]
for more details.)

We fix
s>0
. We consider the Fubini-Study symplectic form
\omega_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
on

\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and we let
h:\mathbb{C}P^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
be the smooth function given
by

[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto\frac{|z_{0}|^{2}}{|z_{0}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{2}}+\frac{1}{s}.

This is a moment map for the
S^{1}
-action on
(\mathbb{C}P^{1},\omega_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
given by

\lambda\cdot[z_{0}:z_{1}]=[z_{0}:\lambda z_{1}]
.
We denote the projections from
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}

to the first and second component by
\mathrm{pr}_{1}
and

\mathrm{pr}_{2}
respectively and consider the
following 2-form on
M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
:

s\left(\mathrm{pr}^{*}_{2}\omega_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}-d\left(\mathrm{pr}^{*}_{2}h\,\mathrm{pr}^{*}_{1}\lambda\right)\right).

By construction, this form is closed, has maximal rank equal to
four and its kernel is precisely the span of the infinitesimal
generator of the
S^{1}
-action of Example

1.69
. Hence, we obtain a symplectic form
\omega_{s}\in\Omega^{2}((M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1})
as desired.

We conclude this section with the following observation, which,
intuitively speaking, states that we may work with
full

submanifolds of symplectic suborbifolds as in the case of manifolds.

Remark 1.107
.

By Remark
1.76
, the tangent bundle of
a full suborbifold of
M
can be seen as an orbi-subbundle of
TM
. Hence, the notion of
Lagrangian/isotropic/symplectic
full suborbifolds of a symplectic
orbifold
(M,\omega)
can be defined as in the case of manifolds.

To conclude this section, we state the following important
property of symplectic orbifolds, which is the analog of a standard
result for symplectic manifolds (see
[
39
, Proposition 8]
for a
proof).

Proposition 1.108
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold. Then there exists a
compatible almost complex structure
J
on
M
, i.e.,

\omega(\cdot,J\cdot)
is a Riemannian metric on
M
.

Remark 1.109
.

Since the proof of
[
39
, Proposition 8]
is
entirely analogous to the case of symplectic manifolds, the space of
compatible almost complex structures on a given symplectic orbifold
is contractible.

Proposition
1.108
has the following immediate consequence.

Corollary 1.110
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold and let
N\subset M
be a
full symplectic suborbifold. Then
TM
admits the structure of a
complex vector orbi-bundle such that the restriction
TM|_{N}
has

TN
as a complex vector orbi-subbundle. In particular, the normal
bundle to
N
has the structure of a complex vector orbi-bundle.

We observe that, by Remark
1.109
, the structures of
complex vector orbi-bundles of Corollary
1.110
do
not depend on the choice of compatible almost complex structure up to isomorphism.

1.5.2.
Darboux’s theorem and its consequences in dimension four

A version of Darboux’s theorem holds for symplectic orbifolds (see,
for instance,
[
39
, Proposition 11]
), i.e., Example

1.100
is locally the only one up to symplectomorphism.

Theorem 1.111

(Darboux’s theorem for orbifolds)
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold. If
x\in M
is a
point with orbifold structure group
\Gamma
, then there exists a
l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
centered at
x
such that
\widehat{U}
is an open
subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\Gamma<\mathrm{U}(n)
and
\omega|_{U}
is
as in Example
1.100
.

Remark 1.112
.

With the notation of Theorem
1.111
, we observe
that the orbi-vector space
T_{x}M
can be identified with

\mathbb{R}^{2n}/\Gamma
and that it inherits a linear symplectic form

\omega_{x}
. Hence, it is a symplectic orbi-vector space (see Example
1.100
).

Since the fixed point set of any linear unitary action has real
codimension at least two, the following result is an immediate
consequence of Theorem
1.111
.

Corollary 1.113
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold. Then the singular set of

M
has codimension at least two.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold. By Theorem

1.111
, the local invariants of a neighborhood of a point
x\in M
with
structure group
\Gamma
up to symplectomorphisms are precisely the invariants of a Hermitian
orbi-vector space
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
up to unitary isomorphisms. For this
reason, and since we are particularly interested in the case of
four-dimensional symplectic orbifolds with isolated singular points and abelian orbifold structure groups,
in what follows we study Hermitian
orbi-vector spaces
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
where
\Gamma
is abelian and
[(0,0)]

is the only singular point.

Fix a faithful representation
\Gamma\hookrightarrow\mathrm{U}(2)
. Since
\Gamma

is finite and abelian, we may assume that the image of
\Gamma
in
\mathrm{U}(2)
is contained
in the maximal torus consisting of diagonal matrices
(S^{1})^{2}<\mathrm{U}(2)
. Moreover, since the rank of that torus is two,

\Gamma
has at most two generators.

Lemma 1.114
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is a finite abelian group and let
\Gamma\hookrightarrow\mathrm{U}(2)
be a faithful representation. If the quotient

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
orbifold has isolated singular points, then
\Gamma

is cyclic.

Proof.

Suppose that there exist
integers
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 2
with
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})>1
such that
\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m_{2}}
. We may assume that there exist

k_{1},k_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the
\Gamma
-action on

\mathbb{C}^{2}
induced by the representation is given by

\begin{split}\Gamma\times\mathbb{C}^{2}&\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
((e^{\frac{2\pi il_{1}}{m_{1}}},e^{\frac{2\pi il_{2}}{m_{2}}}),(z_{1},z_{2}))&\mapsto(e^{\frac{2\pi il_{1}k_{1}}{m_{1}}}e^{\frac{2\pi il_{2}k_{2}}{m_{2}}}z_{1},e^{\frac{2\pi il_{1}j_{1}}{m_{1}}}e^{\frac{2\pi il_{2}j_{2}}{m_{2}}}z_{2}).\end{split}

A simple calculation shows that the above action is effective if and
only if

(1.29)

\gcd(|k_{2}j_{1}-k_{1}j_{2}|,m_{1})=1=\gcd(|k_{2}j_{1}-k_{1}j_{2}|,m_{2}).

We set
\gcd(m_{1},m_{2})=c
and write
m_{i}=m^{\prime}_{i}c
for
i=1,2
. We
set
l_{1}=m^{\prime}_{1}k_{2}
and
l_{2}=-m^{\prime}_{2}k_{1}
. Then the subgroup of
\Gamma
generated by
(e^{\frac{2\pi il_{1}}{m_{1}}},e^{\frac{2\pi il_{2}}{m_{2}}})
fixes all points in the subspace
z_{2}=0
. Moreover, either
l_{1}
is not divisible by
m_{1}
or

l_{2}
is not divisible by
m_{2}
. Indeed, if otherwise, then
c>1
divides both
k_{1}

and
k_{2}
, contradicting (
1.29
). Hence, all points in the
subspace
z_{2}=0
have non-trivial stabilizer.
∎

By Lemma
1.114
, we may assume that

\Gamma
is cyclic, i.e., there exists an integer
m\geq 2
such that

\Gamma\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. As in the proof of Lemma

1.114
,
given any representation
\mathbb{Z}_{m}\to\mathrm{U}(2)
, there exist integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
the induced action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is given by

(1.30)

\begin{split}\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}&\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
(e^{\frac{2\pi ik}{m}},(z_{1},z_{2}))&\mapsto(e^{\frac{2\pi iak}{m}}z_{1},e^{\frac{2\pi ibk}{m}}z_{2}).\end{split}

A simple calculation yields the following result, which gives a
necessary and sufficient condition for the action of (
1.30
) to be such that the origin is the only point
with non-trivial stabilizer.

Lemma 1.115
.

Consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
given by (
1.30
). The
quotient
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is an orbifold such that
[(0,0)]
is the only
singular point if and only if

(1.31)

\gcd(a,m)=1=\gcd(b,m).

Remark 1.116
.

If (
1.31
) holds, by composing with a suitable isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}_{m}\to\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, without loss of generality we may assume that
either
a
or
b
in (
1.30
) equals 1.

Remark
1.116
motivates introducing the
following terminology that is also used in complex algebraic geometry
(see, e.g.,
[
45
]
).

Definition 1.117
.

Let
m\geq 2
be an integer and let
l\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
1\leq l<m
and
\gcd(l,m)=1
. Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be the
Hermitian orbi-vector space that is the quotient of the following

\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}^{2}

(1.32)

\displaystyle(\xi_{m},(z_{1},z_{2}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\xi_{m}z_{1},\xi_{m}^{l}z_{2}).

We call
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
the
model for a cyclic
isolated singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. The integers

(1,l)
are the
weights
of the
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action on

\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Remark 1.118
.

In Definition
1.117
, we choose to make the
weight of the
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
-action of the first coordinate to be equal to one. The other choice
would yield an integer
1\leq l^{\prime}<m
such that
\gcd(l^{\prime},m)=1
and
l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
.

The following result states that the ambiguity of Remark

1.118
disappears when considering the unitary
isomorphism type of the corresponding Hermitian orbi-vector space. Its
proof is a straightforward calculation and is left to the reader.

Lemma 1.119
.

There exists a unitary isomorphism between models for a cyclic
singular point of types
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
and
\frac{1}{m}(1,l^{\prime})
if and only if either
l=l^{\prime}
or

l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
.

As an immediate consequence of Theorem
1.111
,
Lemma
1.115
and Remark
1.116
, we
obtain the following result.

Corollary 1.120
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a four dimensional symplectic orbifold. Let
x\in M
be an isolated singular point with structure group
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
for
some integer
m\geq 2
. Then there exist an integer
1\leq l<m

with
\gcd(l,m)=1
, an open neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
, an
open neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
of
[0,0]
, and a symplectomorphism

\varphi:(U,\omega)\to(V,\omega_{0})
that sends
x
to
[0,0]
,
where
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is the global quotient constructed from
l
as in
Definition
1.117
and
\omega_{0}
is the
symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
that descends from the standard
symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Motivated by Definition
1.117
and Corollary

1.120
, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.121
.

Let
x
a singular point in a symplectic orbifold of
dimension four. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be the orbifold structure group of
x

and let
1\leq l<m

with
\gcd(l,m)=1
be the integer of Corollary

1.120
. We say that is a
cyclic
isolated singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
.

Example 1.122
.

Consider the weighted projective plane
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(3,5,7)
. The type of
the singular
points
[1:0:0]
,
[0:1:0]
and
[0:0:1]
is
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
,

\frac{1}{5}(1,4)
and
\frac{1}{7}(1,4)
respectively.
\Diamond

Example 1.123
.

The type of the three singular points in the quotient
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(3,5,7)/\mathbb{Z}_{11}(1,2,10)
are
\frac{1}{33}(1,23)
,

\frac{1}{55}(1,19)
and
\frac{1}{77}(1,25)
.
\Diamond

Example 1.124
.

(Example
1.12
, continued)
Let
M:=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{4}
be the
orbifold constructed in Example
1.12
. It has three isolated
singular points,
[[0:1],[0:1]],[[1:0],[1:0]]
and
[[0:1],[1:0]]

with orbifold structure groups given by
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
,

\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
respectively. By diagonalizing the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
around
\left([0:1],[0:1]\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\left([1:0],[1:0]\right)
,
there exist coordinates around these points for which the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-action is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle(\xi_{4},(z,w))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\xi_{4}z,\xi_{4}^{3}w).

Similarly, there exist coordinates around
\left([0:1],[1:0]\right)\in\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
for which the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle(\xi_{2},(z,w))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\xi_{2}z,\xi_{2}w).

(Note that
\left([1:0],[0:1]\right)
and
\left([0:1],[1:0]\right)
are in the same
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
-orbit).
We conclude that the type of
[[0:1],[0:1]],[[1:0],[1:0]]
is

\frac{1}{4}(1,3)
, while that of
[[0:1],[1:0]]
is
\frac{1}{2}(1,1)
.

\Diamond

1.5.3.
Compact, connected, symplectic orbi-surfaces

In dimension two, symplectic geometry is simply the geometry of
oriented (orbi-)surfaces; moreover, by Corollary

1.113
, the singular set is necessarily isolated. Hence, a connected, oriented
orbi-surface
\Sigma
with isolated singular points comes equipped with a
symplectic form
\omega
. If
x\in\Sigma
and
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
is a l.u.c. centered at
x
as in Remark

1.20
, then
\Gamma
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}(2)\simeq\mathrm{U}(1)

and
\omega|_{U}
is the quotient of the standard symplectic form

\omega_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
by the action of
\Gamma
.

In analogy with the case of manifolds, compact, connected,
symplectic orbi-surfaces can be
classified up to symplectomorphism (see
[
9
, Section 5]
and Theorem

1.44
).

Theorem 1.125
.

For
i=1,2
, let
(\Sigma_{i},\omega_{i})
be a compact, connected,
symplectic orbi-surface. The
symplectic orbifolds
(\Sigma_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(\Sigma_{2},\omega_{2})

are symplectomorphic if and only if
\Sigma_{1}
is diffeomorphic to

\Sigma_{2}
and
\int_{\Sigma_{1}}\omega_{1}=\int_{\Sigma_{2}}\omega_{2}
.

1.5.4.
Hamiltonian actions on symplectic orbifolds

In this paper we are particularly interested in the following type of
actions on symplectic orbifolds.

Definition 1.126
.

Let
G
be a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and let

(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold.

•

An action of
G
on
M
is
Hamiltonian
if there exists a
good
C^{\infty}
map
H:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, called
moment map
, that is
G
-equivariant with respect to the
coadjoint action of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and such that

\omega(\xi^{M},\cdot)=d\langle\xi,\Phi\rangle(\cdot)\quad\text{for all }\xi\in\mathfrak{g},

where
\xi^{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is the vector field
determined by
\xi
as in Remark
1.83
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the
natural pairing
\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}
.

•

A
Hamiltonian
G
-space
is a triple
(M,\omega,\Phi)
,
where
\Phi:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is the moment map for a
Hamiltonian
G
-action on
M
.

•

Two Hamiltonian
G
-spaces
(M_{1},\omega_{1},\Phi_{1})
and

(M_{2},\omega_{2},\Phi_{2})
are
isomorphic
if there exists a

G
-equivariant symplectomorphism
\varphi:(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to(M_{2},\omega_{2})
such that
\Phi_{2}\circ\varphi=\Phi_{1}
.

Remark 1.127
.

As for symplectic manifolds, a Hamiltonian action on a symplectic
orbifold is by symplectomorphisms. Moreover, we observe that, if
G

is compact and connected, then
G
-equivariance in the above
definition of an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
G
-spaces is automatic
as long as the symplectomorphism intertwines the moment maps.

Linear symplectic actions on symplectic
orbi-vector spaces

Linear symplectic actions play an important role in
understanding the behavior of Hamiltonian group actions near fixed
points (see Theorem
1.135
and Section

2
). For this reason, we recall here some of
their properties, following
[
29
, Lemmas 3.1 and 3.2 ]
. Let
(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
be a symplectic orbi-vector space
obtained as a global quotient of a real symplectic vector space

(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
as in Example

1.100
. By a slight abuse of notation, we set
\widehat{N}
to be the normalizer of

\Gamma
in
\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
. This is a
subgroup of the normalizer
N(\Gamma)
of
\Gamma
in

\mathrm{GL}(\widehat{V})
of Example

1.84
. As in
[
29
, Section
3]
, we set

\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega):=\widehat{N}/\Gamma
.

To start, we recall the following result without proof (see
[
29
, Lemma 3.2]
for
a proof).

Lemma 1.128
.

The natural

\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
-action on

(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
is Hamiltonian and a moment map for
this action is given by
\Phi:\widehat{V}/\Gamma\to\mathfrak{sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)^{*}
defined by

\langle\xi,\Phi\rangle([\widehat{v}])=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi[\widehat{v}],[\widehat{v}])\quad\text{for any }[\widehat{v}]\in\widehat{V}/\Gamma\text{ and any }\xi\in\mathfrak{sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega),

where
\xi[\widehat{v}]
is the value of the vector field
\xi
at

[\widehat{v}]
.

Moreover, let
\pi:\widehat{N}\to\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
and
\pi^{*}:\mathfrak{sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)^{*}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}

denote the quotient map and the induced isomorphism between the duals of the
Lie algebras respectively, and let
\widehat{\Phi}:\widehat{V}\to(\mathfrak{sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega}))^{*}
be the homogeneous
moment map for the standard

\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
-action on

(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
. Then the following diagram commutes

(1.33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathfrak{n}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)^{*},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}_{\widehat{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}

where
q:\widehat{V}\to\widehat{V}/\Gamma
is the quotient map.

Let
G
be a Lie group and let
\rho:G\to\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
be a Lie group homomorphism,
that we call a
symplectic representation
of
G
. In analogy
with Example
1.84
(see also

[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
), there exist a Lie group
\widehat{G}
, a representation
\rho:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{N}
and a short exact sequence of Lie groups

(1.34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

The representation
\rho
is faithful if and only if
\widehat{\rho}
is
faithful (see
[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
). By a standard argument
using functoriality, the following result is an immediate consequence
of Lemma
1.128
.

Corollary 1.129
.

Let
\rho:G\to\mathrm{Sp}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
be a symplectic
representation. The induced linear
G
-action on
(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
is Hamiltonian and a moment map
\Phi_{G}:\widehat{V}/\Gamma\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
for
this action is given by
\rho^{*}\circ\Phi
. Moreover, let
\Phi_{\widehat{G}}:\widehat{V}\to\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
be the moment map for the Hamiltonian

\widehat{G}
-action on
(\widehat{V},\widehat{\omega})
. Then the
following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}/\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}^{*}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}_{\widehat{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}

Remark 1.130
.

If
G
is
compact, then so is
\widehat{G}
. As in Remark
1.101
,
we may view
\widehat{V}
(respectively
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
) as a
Hermitian (respectively orbi-)vector space. Let
N_{\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})}(\Gamma)
denote the normalizer of

\Gamma
in
\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})
and set
\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma):=N_{\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})}(\Gamma)/\Gamma
. Then
\widehat{\rho}

and
\rho
factor through
N_{\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V})}(\Gamma)
and

\mathrm{U}(\widehat{V}/\Gamma)
respectively. Hence, in this case,
the study of symplectic representations of
G
on
(\widehat{V}/\Gamma,\omega)
reduces to that of
unitary representations on
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
.

Moreover, the subset of
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
of fixed
points under the
G
-action is, in fact, a
G
-invariant orbi-vector
complex subspace: To
see this, we identify the above subset with the quotient by
G

of the complex subspace of
\widehat{V}
that consists of fixed
point under the
\widehat{G}
-action. In particular, the subset of
\widehat{V}/\Gamma
that consists of fixed
points under the
G
-action is a full symplectic suborbifold (see Definition

1.75
and Remark
1.107
).

Examples of Hamiltonian circle actions

In this paper, we are mostly interested in Hamiltonian actions of
compact tori, with particular emphasis on
S^{1}
. In
this case, the moment map is invariant under the group action.
Moreover, we use the notation
H
for the moment map of a circle action
and we fix once and for all an identification between the dual of the
Lie algebra of
S^{1}
and
\mathbb{R}
.

Example 1.131

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and positive
integers
m_{0},\dots,m_{n}
satisfying
\gcd(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})=1
. We
endow the weighted projective space
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
with
the standard symplectic form
\omega
of Example

1.102
. Let
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}
be integers. The

S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})
of Example

1.85
is Hamiltonian and a moment map is
given by

(1.35)

\begin{split}H:\mathbb{C}P^{n}(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n})&\to\mathbb{R}\\
[z_{0}:\ldots:z_{n}]&\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}a_{j}\left(\prod_{s\neq j}m_{s}\right)\frac{|z_{j}|^{2}}{\|z\|^{2}},\end{split}

where
\|z\|^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{n}|z_{j}|^{2}
.
\Diamond

Example 1.132

(Example
1.12
, continued)
.

Let
M=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{4}
be the orbifold
constructed in Example
1.12
and let
\omega
be the
symplectic form on
M
constructed in Example

1.103
. The
S^{1}
-action on
M
of Example

1.86
is Hamiltonian and a moment map
is given by

(1.36)

\begin{split}H:M&\to\mathbb{R}\\
\left([[z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]]\right)&\mapsto\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{|z_{0}|^{2}}{|z_{0}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{2}}+\frac{|w_{0}|^{2}}{|w_{0}|^{2}+|w_{1}|^{2}}\right).\end{split}

\Diamond

Example 1.133

(Example
1.13
, continued)
.

Let
c>1
be an integer and let
M_{c}=(\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{c}

be the orbifold constructed in Example

1.13
with
l=1
. Let
\omega_{c}
be the
symplectic form on
M_{c}
constructed in Example

1.104
. We consider
m=1,n=c-1

and the corresponding
S^{1}
-action on
M_{c}
of Example

1.87
. This action is Hamiltonian and a moment map is
given by

(1.37)

\begin{split}H:M_{c}&\to\mathbb{R}\\
\left([[z_{0}:z_{1}],[w_{0}:w_{1}]]\right)&\mapsto\frac{1}{2c}\left(\frac{|z_{1}|^{2}}{|z_{0}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{2}}+(c-1)\frac{|w_{1}|^{2}}{|w_{0}|^{2}+|w_{1}|^{2}}\right).\end{split}

\Diamond

Example 1.134

(Projectivized plane bundles, continued)
.

Let
\pi:M\to\Sigma
be a Seifert fibration with Euler class
different from zero and such that
M
is a manifold. Given
s>0
, let
\omega_{s}
be the symplectic form on
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
constructed in Example
1.106
. The
S^{1}
-
action on
(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}
of Example
1.88
is Hamiltonian and
a moment map is given by

(1.38)

\begin{split}H:(M\times\mathbb{C}P^{1})/S^{1}&\to\mathbb{R}\\
[x,[z_{0}:z_{1}]]&\mapsto\frac{s|z_{0}|^{2}}{2(|z_{0}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{2})}.\end{split}

\Diamond

The equivariant Darboux theorem and its generalizations

Next we present the linearization theorem for
fixed points of Hamiltonian group actions, first proved in
[
29
, Lemma
3.5]
. In some sense, this result combines Corollary

1.91
and Theorem
1.111
. To this end, we
recall that, as in the case of manifolds, if a Lie group
G
acts on
(M,\omega)

in a Hamiltonian fashion and
x\in M
is a fixed point of the action,
then the linear
G
-action on
T_{x}M
preserves the linear symplectic
form
\omega_{x}
(see Remark
1.112
). In
particular, we obtain a symplectic representation
\rho:G\to\mathrm{Sp}(T_{x}M,\omega_{x})
that is known as the
symplectic slice representation
at
x
. The linear
G
-action on
T_{x}M
is
Hamiltonian with moment map
\Phi_{\mathrm{lin}}:T_{x}M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
; moreover, if
G
is compact, the representation

\rho
can be taken to be unitary (see Corollary
1.129
and Remark

1.130
).

Theorem 1.135

(Equivariant Darboux theorem for orbifolds)
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group and let
(M,\omega,\Phi)
be a
Hamiltonian
G
-space. If
x
is a
fixed point, then there exist
G
-invariant open neighborhoods
U\subseteq M
and
W\subseteq T_{x}M
of
x
and
[0]
respectively,
and a
G
-equivariant symplectomorphism
f:(U,\omega)\to(W,\omega_{x})
that sends
x

to
[0]
and such that
\Phi=\Phi(x)+\Phi_{\mathrm{lin}}\circ f
.

By Remark
1.130
, Theorem
1.135
has the
following immediate consequence (cf. Corollary

1.92
).

Corollary 1.136
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group and let
(M,\omega,\Phi)
be a
Hamiltonian
G
-space. Any connected component of the fixed point
set is a full symplectic suborbifold.

To conclude this section, we state a useful generalization of Theorem

1.135
to arbitrary full symplectic suborbifolds (see
Theorem
1.137
below). To this end, let
(M,\omega)
be a
symplectic orbifold and let
N
be a full symplectic suborbifold. Then
the normal orbi-bundle to
N
is a symplectic orbi-bundle (cf. Remark
1.76
). Since the standard Cartan calculus holds for orbifolds (see Remark

1.58
), the proof of the equivariant symplectic neighborhood
theorem for manifolds using the Moser trick works for fully embedded
symplectic suborbifolds. Hence, the following result holds.

Theorem 1.137

(Equivariant symplectic neighborhood theorem for orbifolds)
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group and let
(M_{i},\omega_{i},\Phi_{i})
be a
Hamiltonian
G
-space for
i=1,2
. Let
N
be an orbifold endowed
with a
G
-action and let

I_{i}:N\to M_{i}
be a full
G
-equivariant embedding for

i=1,2
. If the normal orbi-bundles of
N
in
M_{1}
and
M_{2}

are
G
-equivariantly isomorphic as symplectic orbi-bundles, then for
i=1,2
there exists a
G
-invariant
open neighborhood
U_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
of
I_{i}(N_{i})
and an
isomorphism
\varphi:(U_{1},\omega|_{U_{1}},H_{1}|_{U_{1}})\to(U_{2},\omega|_{U_{2}},H_{2}|_{U_{2}})
that extends the diffeomorphism
identifying
I_{1}(N)\simeq I_{2}(N)
.

Remark 1.138
.

If
N
is a fully embedded symplectic suborbifold of a symplectic
orbifold
(M,\omega)
, then the normal orbi-bundle
\nu_{N}
to
N
is both a
complex and symplectic orbi-bundle, and the two structures are
compatible in the standard sense (see Corollary

1.110
). In fact, just as in the case of
manifolds, the isomorphism class of
\nu_{N}
as a symplectic
orbi-bundle is the same as that of
\nu_{N}
as a complex orbi-bundle
(see
[
21
, Corollary A.6]
for the case of manifolds).

Properties of Hamiltonian torus actions on compact
symplectic orbifolds

To conclude this section, we recall a foundational result in the study
of Hamiltonian actions of tori on compact symplectic orbifolds, which
is one of the main results of

[
29
]
.

Theorem 1.139
.

Let
T
be a torus and let
(M,\omega,\Phi)
be a Hamiltonian

T
-space. If
M
is compact, then
\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)
is connected
for every
\alpha\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.

We remark that, as in the case of manifolds, the proof of Theorem

1.139
uses Morse theory, which, in the case of
orbifolds, was started in
[
29
]
and further continued in

[
19
]
. Finally, the following result is an immediate consequence of
Theorem
1.139
that we use throughout this paper.

Corollary 1.140
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space. If
M

is compact, the function
H
has a unique local maximum and minimum.

2.
Local normal forms

2.1.
Orbi-weights of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions

Since we are primarily interested in Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions on
symplectic orbifolds, motivated by Corollary

1.129
, Remark
1.130
and Theorem
1.135
, we take a closer look at
unitary representations of
S^{1}
on the Hermitian vector space

\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
, with the view of proving Lemma
2.2

and introducing orbi-weights at a fixed point (see Definition
2.4
). We begin by proving the following result, which
provides a description of (abelian) finite extensions of tori and, as
such, may be of independent interest.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite group and let
T
be a torus. Consider the following short exact sequence of Lie groups

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Let
\widehat{G}_{0}\trianglelefteq\widehat{G}
denote the identity component of
\widehat{G}
and set
\Gamma_{0}:=i^{-1}(\widehat{G}_{0})\trianglelefteq\Gamma.
Then

•

\widehat{G}_{0}
is a torus of the same dimension as
T
and
j|_{\widehat{G}_{0}}:\widehat{G}_{0}\rightarrow T
is a covering map,

•

\pi_{0}(\widehat{G})\simeq\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}
, and

•

\widehat{G}\simeq(\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0})/\Gamma_{0}
, where
\Gamma_{0}
is identified with a subgroup of
\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}
via

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\mapsto(\gamma,i(\gamma^{-1})).

Moreover, if
\Gamma
is abelian, then
\widehat{G}\simeq(\Gamma/\Gamma_{0})\times\widehat{G}_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
j:\widehat{G}\to T
, is a covering map, it induces an isomorphism of Lie algebras
j_{*}:\mathrm{Lie}(\widehat{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{Lie}(T)
. Hence,

\widehat{G}_{0}
is abelian and of the same dimension as
T
. Since
\widehat{G}
is compact,
\widehat{G}_{0}

is a torus. Moreover, since
\exp_{T}\circ j_{*}=j\circ\exp_{\widehat{G}}
, since

\exp_{\widehat{G}}(\mathrm{Lie}(\widehat{G}))=\widehat{G}_{0}
and since
\exp_{T}(\mathrm{Lie}(T))=T
, the
restriction of
j
to
\widehat{G}_{0}
is onto, so that

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{0}}

is a short exact sequence of Lie groups, where
i_{0}
(respectively
j_{0}
) denotes
the restrictions of
i
(respectively
j
) to
\Gamma_{0}
(respectively

\widehat{G}_{0}
). Hence,
j_{0}:\widehat{G}_{0}\to T
is a covering
map.

Since
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

is a diagram of exact sequences of Lie groups, we have
\pi_{0}(\widehat{G})\simeq\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}
.

Next we show that every element of
\Gamma
commutes with every
element of
\widehat{G}_{0}
. To show this, let
g_{0}\in\widehat{G}_{0}
and
h\in\Gamma
. Since
T
is abelian,
j\left(g_{0}\,h\,g_{0}^{-1}\right)=e\in T
. Hence,
g_{0}\,h\,g_{0}^{-1}\in\Gamma
. Since
\widehat{G}_{0}
is connected
and
\Gamma
is discrete, for a fixed
h\in\Gamma
, the map
\widehat{G}_{0}\to\Gamma
that sends
g_{0}
to
g_{0}hg_{0}^{-1}

is constant. Hence,
g_{0}\,h\,g_{0}^{-1}=h
for every
g_{0}\in\widehat{G}_{0}
and every
h\in\Gamma
, as desired.

Since
\Gamma
commutes with
\widehat{G}_{0}
, the map

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mu:\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\widehat{G}

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto i(g_{1})g_{2}

is a smooth homomorphism. Moreover, it is surjective. Indeed, let
g\in\widehat{G}
. Then
g\widehat{G}_{0}\subset\widehat{G}
is the connected component of
\widehat{G}

containing
g
. Since
\Gamma
surjects onto
\pi_{0}(\widehat{G})
,
there exists an element
h\in\Gamma
such that
i(h)\in g\widehat{G}_{0}
. Hence,
i(h)=g\,g_{0}
for some
g_{0}\in\widehat{G}_{0}
, as desired. Since

\displaystyle\ker\mu

\displaystyle=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}\mid g_{2}=i(g_{1}^{-1})\}\simeq i^{-1}(\widehat{G}_{0})=\Gamma_{0},

we have
\widehat{G}\simeq(\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0})/\Gamma_{0}
.

If
\Gamma
is abelian, then

\widehat{G}\simeq(\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0})/\Gamma_{0}
is also
abelian. Furthermore, since
\widehat{G}_{0}
is a torus, it is a
divisible group. Hence, we can extend the identity map
\widehat{G}_{0}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{0}
to a homomorphism
\widehat{G}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{0}
(see
[
27
, Lemma 4.2]
). The restriction of
this homomorphism to

\Gamma
is a homomorphism

I:\Gamma\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{0}
that extends

\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow\widehat{G}_{0}
. We consider
the isomorphism

\displaystyle\phi:\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto(g_{1},I(g_{1})g_{2}).

By a direct calculation, we see that

\phi(\ker\mu)=\Gamma_{0}\times\{1\}
, where
\mu:\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0}\to\widehat{G}
is as in (
2.3
),
and that
\phi
descends to an isomorphism

(\Gamma\times\widehat{G}_{0})/\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}\times\widehat{G}_{0}
, as desired.
∎

In what follows, we fix the notation of the subsection on linear
symplectic actions in Section
1.5.4
. Moreover, we fix the standard symplectic form

\omega_{0}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, so that
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
inherits a linear
symplectic form. By an abuse of notation, we denote the resulting
symplectic vector orbi-space also by
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{U}(n)
and let
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma)
be a representation.
Let
\widehat{G}
be the extension of
S^{1}
by
\Gamma
of
(
1.34
), let
\widehat{\rho}:\widehat{G}\to N_{\mathrm{U}(n)}(\Gamma)
be the representation given by Remark

1.130
, and let
\widehat{G}_{0}
denote the connected
component of the identity of
\widehat{G}
.
There exist a positive integer
p
that divides
|\Gamma|
,

a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and an isomorphism

\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}
such that

•

the covering map
\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}\to S^{1}
is given
by
\widehat{\lambda}\to\widehat{\lambda}^{p}
, and

•

for all
\widehat{\lambda}\in\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}
and for
all
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\lambda})(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=(\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{1}}z_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{n}}z_{n}).

Finally, the induced
S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
is
Hamiltonian and a moment map
H:\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is
given by

(2.4)

H([z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}])=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{a_{1}}{p}|z_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+\frac{a_{n}}{p}|z_{n}|^{2}\right).

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.2
, the

S^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
is given by

(2.5)

\lambda\cdot[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]=[\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{\frac{a_{n}}{p}}z_{n}]\text{ for any }\lambda\in S^{1},

(cf. Examples

1.86
and
1.87
).

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Since the following diagram commutes

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathrm{U}(n)}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(see the discussion preceding Corollary

1.129
, and Remark
1.130
),
by Theorem
2.1
, the restriction of
j
to
\widehat{G}_{0}
is a covering map

\widehat{G}_{0}\to S^{1}
. Hence, there exist an
isomorphism
\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}
and a positive integer
p

such that the subgroup
\Gamma_{0}<\Gamma
of Theorem

2.1
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and the covering map
\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}\to S^{1}
sends

\widehat{\lambda}
to
\widehat{\lambda}^{p}
. In particular,
p

divides
|\Gamma|
. Moreover, since

\widehat{\rho}
is unitary, there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(2.7)
