\widehat{\rho}_{0}(\widehat{\lambda})=\mathrm{diag}(\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{1}},\ldots,\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{n}})\quad\text{for any }\widehat{\lambda}\in\widehat{G}_{0}.

Finally, the
\widehat{G}
-action on
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\omega_{0})
is Hamiltonian and a
moment map
\widehat{H}\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

(2.8)

\widehat{H}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{1}|z_{1}|^{2}+\ldots+a_{n}|z_{n}|^{2}\right).

Since the derivative
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
of the covering map
S^{1}\to S^{1}

is given by multiplication by
p
and since
\widehat{H}
is

\Gamma
-invariant, the last statement follows by Corollary

1.129
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

With the notation of Lemma
2.2
, set
n=2
,
suppose that the representation
\rho
is faithful and that
\Gamma

is not abelian. We claim that, in this case,
a_{1}=a_{2}=\pm 1
. To
see this, we observe that, by
[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
,
\rho
is injective if and only if

\widehat{\rho}
is injective. Hence,
\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1
. By
definition,
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{G}_{0})
is contained in

N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\Gamma)
. Thus, if
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\Gamma
, then

(2.9)

\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{1}}&0\\
0&\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\lambda}^{-a_{1}}&0\\
0&\widehat{\lambda}^{-a_{2}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{1}-a_{2}}\beta\\
\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{2}-a_{1}}\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma

for all
\widehat{\lambda}\in\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}
. Since

\Gamma
is finite and not abelian, it follows that
a_{1}=a_{2}
,
whence the result.

The rational numbers that appear in Lemma
2.2
are the analog of
weights in the realm of orbifolds. To see this, let
(M,\omega,H)
be
a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M
be a fixed point. By Theorems

1.111
and
1.135
, the action near
x
is modeled on an
S^{1}
-action
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
induced by a unitary representation, where
\Gamma

is the orbifold structure group of
x
that acts on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
in a
unitary fashion. This is precisely the situation considered in Lemma

2.2
. Hence, we may introduce the following terminology, following

[
29
,
34
]
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be
a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M
be a fixed point with
orbifold structure group
\Gamma
. Let
p,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the integers given
by Lemma
2.2
. We say that the rational numbers
\frac{a_{1}}{p},\ldots,\frac{a_{n}}{p}\in\mathbb{Q}
are the
orbi-weights
at
x
.

Example 2.5
.

Let
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
act on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}^{2}

\displaystyle(\mu,(z_{1},z_{2}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(z_{1},\mu z_{2}).

If the action of
S^{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is given by

\lambda\cdot[z_{1},z_{2}]_{m}=[\lambda z_{1},z_{2}]_{m},\quad\lambda\in S^{1},

then the extension
\widehat{G}
of
S^{1}
by
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
in
(
2.6
) is
\widehat{G}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m}

and
\Gamma_{0}:=\widehat{G}_{0}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{m}=\{id\}
. In this
case, the orbi-weights at
[0]
are
1,0
.

If, on the other hand, the action of
S^{1}
is given by

\lambda\cdot[z_{1},z_{2}]_{m}=[z_{1},\lambda z_{2}]_{m},\quad\lambda\in S^{1},

then
\widehat{G}=S^{1}
and
\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. In this
case, the orbi-weights at
[0]
are
0,\frac{1}{m}
.
\Diamond

Example 2.6
.

(Weighted projective planes)
Consider the weighted projective plane

M=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q)
with
p,q,s
pairwise relatively
prime, equipped with (a multiple of) the standard symplectic
form of Example
1.11
and the Hamiltonian
circle action of Examples
1.85

and
1.131
given by

(2.10)

\begin{split}\phi:S^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q)&\to\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q)\\
(\lambda,[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{n}])&\mapsto[\lambda^{a}z_{0}:\lambda^{b}z_{1}:\lambda^{c}z_{2}],\end{split}

where
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
are such that
k_{0}:=aq-cp
,
k_{1}:=bq-cs
and
k_{2}:=as-bp
are positive and pairwise relatively prime.

This action has three isolated fixed points,
F_{1}=[1:0:0]
,

F_{2}=[0:1:0]
and
F_{3}=[0:0:1]
, with orbifold structure
group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{s}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
respectively. We consider the global
quotient
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
given by the following
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-action:

\begin{split}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}&\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\\
(\mu,(z_{1},z_{2}))&\mapsto(\mu^{s}z_{1},\mu^{q}z_{2}).\end{split}

A l.u.c. centered at
F_{1}
is given by the map that sends
[z,w]_{p}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
to
[1:z:w]\in M
.
By the weighted homogeneity of the coordinates in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q)
, the
S^{1}
-action in these coordinates is given by

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]

\displaystyle=[\lambda^{a}z_{0}:\lambda^{b}z_{1}:\lambda^{c}z_{2}]=[z_{0}:\lambda^{-\frac{as-bp}{p}}z_{1}:\lambda^{-\frac{aq-cp}{p}}z_{2}].

Since the above action is linear, the orbi-weights at
F_{1}
are
-\frac{k_{0}}{p},-\frac{k_{2}}{p}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]

\displaystyle=[\lambda^{\frac{as-bp}{s}}z_{0}:z_{1}:\lambda^{-\frac{bq-cs}{s}}z_{2}]

\displaystyle=[\lambda^{\frac{aq-cp}{q}}z_{0}:\lambda^{\frac{bq-cs}{q}}z_{1}:z_{2}].

Hence, the orbi-weights at
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are

\frac{k_{2}}{s},-\frac{k_{1}}{s}
and
\frac{k_{0}}{q},\frac{k_{1}}{q}
respectively.

If
k_{1}=0
and
k_{0},k_{2}
are relatively prime, the fixed point set of the
action in (
2.10
) is the union of
F_{1}
and the points of the orbi-sphere

\Sigma=\{[0:z_{1}:z_{2}]\in M\}\simeq\mathbb{C}P^{1}(s,q).

The orbi-weights at
F_{1}
are
-\frac{q}{p},-\frac{s}{p}
and the
orbi-weights at each point in
\Sigma
are
0,1
. Note that, since

k_{1}=0
, we have
bq=cs
. Hence, since
p,q,s
are pairwise relatively prime
and
k_{0},k_{2}
are relatively prime, we must have
k_{2}=s
and

k_{0}=q
.
\Diamond

2.2.
Local classification near fixed points in the four dimensional case

The aim of this section is to prove the local classification of Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-spaces on four dimensional symplectic orbifolds near a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
– see Theorem

2.9
below. We start by proving the following
result, which gives necessary and sufficient conditions for given
rational numbers to arise as orbi-weights at a fixed point that is
singular for the orbifold under the assumption that the
S^{1}
-action
be effective.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
m\geq 2
be an integer, let
p
be a positive integer that
divides
m
, and let
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. There
exists a faithful representation
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
such that
p,a_{1},a_{2}
are as
in Lemma
2.2
if and only if

(2.11)

\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1\quad\text{and}\quad\gcd(|a_{1}l-a_{2}|,m)=p.

Moreover,
[0,0]
is an isolated fixed point for the induced
S^{1}
-action on

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
exactly if
a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
. Finally, the
S^{1}
-action extends to a
toric action on
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that there is a faithful representation
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
such that the orbi-weights at

[0]
of the induced
S^{1}
-action are
a_{1}/p,a_{2}/p
. Let
\widehat{G}
be the extension of
S^{1}
by
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
of
(
1.34
), let
\widehat{\rho}:\widehat{G}\to N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})
be the representation given by Remark

1.130
, and let
\widehat{G}_{0}
denote the connected
component of the identity of
\widehat{G}
. By Lemma

2.2
, there exists a positive integer
p
and an isomorphism
\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq S^{1}
such that
\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{m}\cap\widehat{G}_{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Since the following diagram commutes

(2.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

for any
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
we have
\widehat{\rho}(\gamma_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}<\mathrm{U}(2)
. Hence, since
[0,0]
is an isolated singular
point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
, for any
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, there
exists
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
such that

(2.13)

\widehat{\rho}(\gamma_{0})(z_{1},z_{2})=(\mu z_{1},\mu^{l}z_{2})\text{
for all }(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

On the other hand, by Lemma
2.2
,

(2.14)

\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{\lambda})(z_{1},z_{2})=(\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{1}}z_{2},\widehat{\lambda}^{a_{2}}z_{2})\text{ for
all }\widehat{\lambda}\in S^{1}\simeq\widehat{G}_{0}.

By (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), and since
\Gamma_{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have that

(2.15)

a_{1}l-a_{2}=0\mod p.

By
[
29
, Lemma 3.1]
,
\rho
is injective if and only if

\widehat{\rho}
is injective. By (
2.14
), if

\widehat{\rho}
is injective, then
\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1
. Moreover,
by (
2.5
),
\rho(\lambda)=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}},\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}})
. Hence, setting
\lambda=e^{2\pi i\theta}
for some
\theta\in[0,1)
, we have that
\lambda\in\ker\rho
if and only if

e^{\frac{2\pi i\theta a_{1}}{p}}=\mu\text{ and }\quad e^{\frac{2\pi i\theta a_{2}}{p}}=\mu^{l}\text{ for some }\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}.

Equivalently,

(2.16)

\theta(a_{1}l-a_{2})\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\theta(a_{1}l-a_{2})=0\mod p,\quad\text{and}\quad e^{\frac{2\pi i\theta a_{1}}{p}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}.

Claim
.

If
\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1
and
a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
, then
\rho
is injective if and only if
\gcd\left(\frac{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}{p},\frac{m}{p}\right)=1
.

Proof of Claim.

For each implication, we split the proof in two cases, namely
p=1
and
p>1
. First we suppose that

\gcd\left(\frac{|a_{1}l-a_{2}|}{p},\frac{m}{p}\right)=1
.

Case
p=1
:
Since
\gcd(0,m)=m\neq 1
, then
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert\neq 0
. If
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert=1
, then the
result follows by (
2.16
). Hence, we may assume that

|a_{1}l-a_{2}|>1
. By (
2.16
), there exists an integer
0\leq q<\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert
such that

\theta=\frac{q}{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}\quad\text{ and }\quad e^{\frac{2\pi iqa_{1}}{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{m},

so that
e^{\frac{2\pi iqa_{1}m}{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}}=1
. Since
\gcd(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,m)=1
and

\gcd(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,a_{1})=\gcd(a_{2},a_{1})=1,

this can only be satisfied if
q=0
and so
\rho
is injective.

Case
p>1
:
By (
2.15
), there
exists an integer
\alpha\geq 0
such that
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert=\alpha\,p
. Therefore

(2.17)

\gcd\left(\frac{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}{p},\frac{m}{p}\right)=\gcd(\alpha,k)=1,

where
k
is such that
m=k\,p
. Suppose first that
\alpha=0
. Then

1=\gcd(\alpha,k)=\gcd(0,k)=k,

and so
p=m
. Hence, by (
2.16
),
\theta a_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,

1=\gcd(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,a_{1})=\gcd(0,a_{1})=a_{1}.

Hence,
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho
is injective. Next
we assume that
\alpha=1
. Then, by (
2.16
),
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho
is injective. Finally, if
\alpha>1
, then, by the second equation in (
2.16
), there exists an integer

0\leq q<p
such that

\theta=\frac{q}{\alpha}\quad\text{ and}\quad e^{\frac{2\pi iqa_{1}k}{\alpha}}=1.

Since

1=\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=\gcd(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,a_{1})=\gcd(\alpha\,p,a_{1}),

we have that
\gcd(a_{1},\alpha)=1
. Moreover, by assumption,

\gcd(k,\alpha)=1
and so
q=0\mod\alpha
. Hence, since
q<\alpha
, it follows that
q=\theta=0
and so

\rho
is injective.

Conversely, suppose that
\gcd\left(\frac{\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert}{p},\frac{m}{p}\right)=c\neq 1
.

Case
p=1
:
If
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert=0
, then
(
2.16
) is always satisfied by
\theta=\frac{1}{m}

and so
\rho
is not injective. Otherwise, since
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert\neq 1
, then
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert>1
. In this case,
\theta=1/c
satisfies (
2.16
) and so

\rho
is not injective.

Case
p>1
:
Note that there exists an integer
\alpha\neq 1
such that
\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert=\alpha\,p
. If
\alpha=0
, then (
2.16
) is satisfied by

\theta=\frac{1}{k}
and so
\rho
is not injective. If

\alpha>1
, then, since
c=\gcd(\alpha,k)
, we have that
\theta=1/c
satisfies (
2.16
) and so

\rho
is not injective.
∎

If
a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
, then, by the above Claim and since
\gcd\left(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,p\right)=p
by (
2.15
), we have that
\gcd\left(\lvert a_{1}l-a_{2}\rvert,m\right)=p
, so that condition (
2.11
) holds. Else, assume that
a_{2}=0
without loss of generality. Since

\gcd(a_{1},0)=1
, then
\lvert a_{1}\rvert=1
. This readily implies that

p=1
for, if not,

p=\gcd(\lvert a_{1}l\rvert,p)=\gcd(l,p),

which contradicts
1=\gcd(l,m)
. Hence, condition (
2.11
) holds.

It is a simple calculation to check that (
2.11
) is sufficient to define a faithful representation
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
such that the orbi-weights at

[0,0]
of the induced
S^{1}
-action are
a_{1}/m,a_{2}/m
. We leave this to the reader and remark only that
(
2.15
) ensures that
\rho
is well-defined, and that the
above Claim implies that it is injective in the case
a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
.

Suppose that
[0,0]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is not an isolated fixed point of
the
S^{1}
-action. Since the action is given by

\lambda\cdot[z_{1},z_{2}]=[\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1},\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{2}],

and since
\rho
is faithful, if
[z_{1},z_{2}]\neq[0,0]
is a fixed
point, then exactly one of
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
equals 0. Assume without
loss of generality that
z_{2}=0
. Then, for all
\lambda\in S^{1}
,
there exists
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
such that
\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}=\mu
. This can only occur if
a_{1}=0
. Hence, since
[0,0]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is a fixed point of the
S^{1}
-action, it is isolated
exactly if
a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0
.

Finally, we want to prove that the circle action on

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
extends to a torus action. By Theorem

2.1
,
\widehat{G}
is abelian. Hence, since
\widehat{\rho}

is a unitary representation, there exists a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
\widehat{\rho}(\widehat{G})
consists
solely of diagonal elements. Since the maximal torus
(S^{1})^{2}\subset\mathrm{U}(2)
equals the subset of diagonal elements of

\mathrm{U}(2)
and since
\mathbb{Z}_{m}<(S^{1})^{2}
, the result follows.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
, let
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
be a faithful representation, and let
p,a_{1},a_{2}
be the integers associated to
\rho
by Lemma
2.2
. Let
1\leq l^{\prime}<m
be the unique integer such that
l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
. Then the model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l^{\prime})
is isomorphic to

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
(see Lemma
1.119
). We
fix such an isomorphism and let

\rho^{\prime}
denote the corresponding faithful unitary
representation. Let
p^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime}
be the integers associated to

\rho^{\prime}
by Lemma
2.2
. Then
p=p^{\prime}
and the
multisets
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and
\{a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime}\}
are equal. Moreover,
since
l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
, equation (
2.11
) is satisfied by
p,a_{1},a_{2}
if and only if they
are satisfied by
p^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime}
.

In particular, if
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
, this allows us to
choose
a
preferred representative for the type of a cyclic isolated singular
point: Namely, we choose
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
so that
a_{1}>a_{2}
, i.e., the larger of the two orbi-weights is in the direction of
the coordinate corresponding to
1
. If
a_{1}=a_{2}
, then by Proposition
2.7

a_{1}=a_{2}=\pm 1
. Moreover, if
l^{2}=1\mod m
, then
l=l^{\prime}
, so that there is
no choice to be made. Finally, if
l^{2}\neq 1\mod m
, then there is
no preferred choice for the expression
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. This
comes into play in Section
3.2
.

The main result of this section, Theorem

2.9
, is an immediate consequence of Theorem
1.135
, Lemma
2.2
and
Proposition
2.7
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a four dimensional Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and
let
x\in M
be a cyclic quotient isolated singular point of type

\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
that is also a fixed point for the
S^{1}
-action. Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be the corresponding model and
let
\omega_{0}
be the
symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
that descends from the standard
symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Let
p,a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the integers
associated to the representation
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(T_{x}M)\simeq\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
defining the linear
S^{1}
-action on

T_{x}M
by Lemma
2.2
.

There exist an open
S^{1}
-invariant neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
, an
open neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
of
[0,0]
, and an
isomorphism of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces between
(U,\omega,H)
and

(V,\omega_{0},H_{\mathrm{lin}})
, where the map

H_{\mathrm{lin}}:\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

H_{\mathrm{lin}}([z_{1},z_{2}]):=H(x)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{a_{1}}{p}\lvert z_{1}\rvert^{2}+\frac{a_{2}}{p}\lvert z_{2}\rvert^{2}\right)

is the moment map for the linear
S^{1}
-action.

Moreover,
x
is an isolated fixed point of the
S^{1}
-action if and
only if

a_{1}a_{2}\neq 0\,,\,\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1\text{ and }\gcd(|a_{1}l-a_{2}|,m)=p.

Finally,
x
is not an isolated fixed point if and only if
p=1
and

\{|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|\}=\{0,1\}
.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.9
, its analog for
manifolds (see
[
21
, Corollary A.7]
), and Corollary

1.136
), we obtain the
following result, which describes properties of the fixed point set of
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions on four dimensional symplectic orbifolds
with discrete singular sets all of whose orbifold structure groups are
cyclic.

Corollary 2.10
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a four dimensional Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
with discrete singular set and such that all orbifold structure
groups are cyclic. Any connected component of the fixed point set of

M
is a full symplectic suborbifold of dimension at most
two. Moreover, if
\Sigma
is a two dimensional component, all
points in
\Sigma
are local extrema of
H
.

Remark 2.11
.

Under the hypotheses of Corollary
2.10
, we can
prove a slightly stronger result, namely that any two dimensional
component
\Sigma
of the fixed point set is
fully embedded
in the
sense of
[
36
, Definition 14]
, i.e., the inclusion
map
\Sigma\hookrightarrow M
is a good
C^{\infty}
map. This uses
the fact that the singular set of
M
is discrete
4
4

4

The example of a
full suborbifold that is not fully embedded of
[
52
, Example
5.4]
can be endowed with a suitable
S^{1}
-action to
illustrate what may go wrong if the singular set of
M
is not discrete.
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Our next result states that
the integers
p,a_{1},a_{2}
associated to a faithful unitary representation
of
S^{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
determine the isomorphism class of the
representation.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Let
\rho,\rho^{\prime}:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
be faithful representations. Let
p,a_{1},a_{2}
(respectively
p^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}
) be the integers associated to
\rho
(respectively

\rho^{\prime}
) by Lemma
2.2
. There exists a unitary
isomorphism
A\in\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
such that

(2.18)

A\circ\rho(\lambda)=\rho^{\prime}(\lambda)\circ A\text{ for all }\lambda\in S^{1}

if and only if
p=p^{\prime}
and either the multisets
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and

\{a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}\}
are equal and
l^{2}=1\mod m
, or
a_{1}=a_{1}^{\prime}
and
a_{2}=a_{2}^{\prime}
and
l^{2}\neq 1\mod m
.

In order to prove Proposition
2.12
, we need
the following result, stated below without proof as it is a simple
calculation.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Then the normalizer

N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})
is given by

(1)

\mathrm{U}(2)
if
l=1
,

(2)

the subgroup generated by the diagonal
elements and the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
if
l^{2}=1\mod m
and
l\neq 1\mod m
, and

(3)

the subgroup of diagonal matrices in all other cases.

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.

First, we assume that there exists
A\in\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})

such that (
2.18
) holds. Moreover, we start by assuming that

l^{2}\neq 1\mod m
. By part
(3)
of Lemma
2.13
,

N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})
is the subgroup of diagonal
matrices. Hence, there exist
\psi,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.19)

[e^{2\pi i\psi}\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1},e^{2\pi i\theta}\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}}z_{2}]=[e^{2\pi i\psi}\lambda^{\frac{a^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}}}z_{1},e^{2\pi i\theta}\lambda^{\frac{a^{\prime}_{2}}{p^{\prime}}}z_{2}]

for all
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and for all
\lambda\in S^{1}
. Let
[z_{1},z_{2}]
be such that
z_{1}z_{2}\neq 0
. Then by (
2.19
) and the fact that

\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})=N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
we have that

\lambda^{\frac{ma_{1}}{p}}=\lambda^{\frac{ma^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{\frac{ma_{2}}{p}}=\lambda^{\frac{ma^{\prime}_{2}}{p^{\prime}}},

for all
\lambda\in S^{1}
. Hence, since
m\neq 0
, we have

(2.20)

\frac{a_{1}}{p}=\frac{a^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{a_{2}}{p}=\frac{a^{\prime}_{2}}{p^{\prime}}.

Note that,
a_{i}=0
if and only if
a^{\prime}_{i}=0
. Moreover, in
this case, we have by Proposition
2.7
, that
a_{j}=a^{\prime}_{j}=\pm 1
for
j\neq i
(note that
p
and
p^{\prime}
are
positive) and so
p=p^{\prime}
. If none of

a_{1},a_{2},a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime}
are zero, then, by (
2.20
), we have
a_{1}a_{2}^{\prime}=a_{1}^{\prime}a_{2}
and then, since

\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=\gcd(a_{1}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime})=1,

(and
p
and
p^{\prime}
are positive), we have that

a_{1}=a_{1}^{\prime}
,
a_{2}=a_{2}^{\prime}
and
p=p^{\prime}
.

Next suppose that
l^{2}=1\mod m
and
l\neq 1
. In this case, by part

(2)
of Lemma

2.13
, we have that
N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})
is the subgroup
generated by diagonal
matrices and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Hence, there exist
\psi,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
such that either (
2.19
) or

[e^{2\pi i\psi}\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}}z_{2},e^{2\pi i\theta}\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1}]=[e^{2\pi i\psi}\lambda^{\frac{a^{\prime}_{1}}{p^{\prime}}}z_{2},e^{2\pi i\theta}\lambda^{\frac{a^{\prime}_{2}}{p^{\prime}}}z_{1}]

holds for all
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and for all
\lambda\in S^{1}
. The former case is dealt with above; the latter is entirely
analogous and yields that
a_{1}=a_{2}^{\prime}
and
a_{2}=a_{1}^{\prime}
.

Finally, assume that
l=1
. By part

(1)
of Lemma
2.13
, the group

N_{\mathrm{U}(2)}(\mathbb{Z}_{m})
equals
\mathrm{U}(2)
. Hence, there exist

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.21)

\begin{split}&[\alpha\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1}+\beta\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}}z_{2},e^{2\pi i\theta}(\bar{\alpha}\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}}z_{2}-\bar{\beta}\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}z_{1})]\\
=&[\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}}}(\alpha z_{1}+\beta z_{2}),\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}}}e^{2\pi i\theta}(\bar{\alpha}z_{2}-\bar{\beta}z_{1})]\end{split}

for all
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and for all
\lambda\in S^{1}
. By considering the cases
z_{1}=0
and
z_{2}\neq 0
, and

z_{1}\neq 0
and
z_{2}=0
and using the fact that
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
, the argument is the same as that of the case
l^{2}=1\mod m
and
l\neq 1
.
∎

To conclude this section we prove a result that describes the points with non-trivial
stabilizer of an effective unitary linear
S^{1}
-action on the model
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
be a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Let
\rho:S^{1}\to\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m})
be a faithful representation
and let
p,a_{1},a_{2}
be the integers associated to
\rho
as in
Lemma
2.2
. Any point
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
such that
z_{1}z_{2}\neq 0
has
trivial stabilizer for the induced
S^{1}
-action. Moreover, the stabilizer of a point
[z_{1},0]

(respectively
[0,z_{2}]
) with
z_{1}\neq 0
(respectively
z_{2}\neq 0
) is
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{1}|}
(respectively
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{2}|}
), where
k=m/p

and we set
\mathbb{Z}_{0}\equiv S^{1}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
is such that
z_{1}z_{2}\neq 0
and that
\lambda\in S^{1}
fixes it. By (
1.117
)
and (
2.5
), there exists
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
such that

\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}=\mu\text{ and }\lambda^{\frac{a_{2}}{p}}=\mu^{\ell}.

In particular, such a
\lambda
fixes every point
[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
with
z_{1}z_{2}\neq 0
. By continuity,
\lambda
fixes every point in

\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Since
\rho
is faithful,
\lambda=1
.

Next we determine the stabilizer of a point of the form
[z_{1},0]

with
z_{1}\neq 0
. If
a_{1}=0
, then
[z_{1},0]
is stabilized by

S^{1}
. So we may assume that
a_{1}\neq 0
. If
\lambda\in S^{1}

fixes
[z_{1},0]
, then
\lambda^{\frac{a_{1}}{p}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{m}
and so

\lambda^{\frac{ma_{1}}{p}}=1
and
\lambda^{ka_{1}}=1
. Thus
the stabilizer of
[z_{1},0]
is contained in
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{1}|}
. On the
other hand, it is immediate to see that
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{ka_{1}}}
fixes
[z_{1},0]
. Therefore, the stabilizer
of
[z_{1},0]
equals
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{1}|}
. Similarly, the points of the form

[0,z_{2}]
with
z_{2}\neq 0
have stabilizer
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{2}|}
.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

By Lemma
2.14
, a linear Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action on a model for a cyclic isolated
singular point of type
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
may enjoy properties that
cannot occur for linear linear Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions on four
dimensional symplectic vector spaces. We fix the notation in Lemma

2.14
and suppose that
k>1
. If both
a_{1},a_{2}\neq 0
, then the greatest common divisor of the two (finite) stabilizers

\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{1}|}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{|ka_{2}|}
equals
k
, i.e., they are not coprime. On the other
hand, if one of
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
is equal to zero, then there is a one dimensional orbi-vector subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
that
is pointwise fixed by the action and a complementary one that
consists of points with finite non-trivial stabilizer.

3.
The labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space

By an abuse of notation, henceforth whenever we say that

(M,\omega,H)
is a
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
, we assume that

•

M
is compact,

•

the singular set of
M
is discrete, and

•

every singular point of
M
has a cyclic orbifold structure group.

3.1.
Isotropy orbi-spheres

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space. For any subgroup
K

of
S^{1}
, we consider two subsets of
M
, namely
\mathrm{Stab}(M;K)\subseteq M

and
M^{K}\subseteq M
, that are defined as follows

(3.1)

\begin{split}\mathrm{Stab}(M;K)&:=\{x\in M\mid\mathrm{Stab}(x)=K\},\\
M^{K}&:=\{x\in M\mid h\cdot x=x\text{ for all }h\in K\},\end{split}

where
\mathrm{Stab}(x)
denotes the stabilizer of
x
. We endow both
subsets with the subspace topology. We observe that the closure of

\mathrm{Stab}(M;K)
is
M^{K}
and that these two sets coincide if
H=S^{1}
. By Corollary

2.10
, a connected component of
M^{S^{1}}
is
either an isolated fixed point
x
or a full symplectic submanifold
\Sigma
of dimension
two. In the former case, a neighborhood of
x
is equivariantly
determined by Theorem
2.9
. In the latter case, since
M
is compact,
\Sigma
is too. Hence,
by Theorem
1.125
, it is
classified by its diffeomorphism type (see Theorem

1.44
), and its symplectic area.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space. A connected
component of
M^{S^{1}}
of dimension two is called a
fixed orbi-surface
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fixed orbi-surface of a Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-space. By Corollary
2.10
, the
orbi-surface
\Sigma
is a local extremum of
H
. Hence, by
Corollary
1.140
, it is a global extremum of

H
. Moreover, by
Theorem
2.9
, if
x\in\Sigma
is a singular
point, then the integer
p
of Lemma
2.2
equals
one and the
orbi-weights
a_{1},a_{2}
at
x
satisfy
\{|a_{1}|,|a_{2}|\}=\{0,1\}
. In particular, we can choose a preferred representative
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)

for the type of
x
(see Remark
2.8
).

Next we turn to understanding a connected component of

\mathrm{Stab}(M;K)
for some fixed
proper subgroup
K\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{k}
of
S^{1}
, where
k\geq 2
. To this end, let
x\in M

be a point with stabilizer equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. By Corollary

1.89
,
x
is regular. Therefore,
the ‘standard’ local model for the
S^{1}
-action near
x
holds (see

[
21
, Lemma A.14]
and, more generally,
[
15
,
31
]
). To
recall this model, we let
\rho:\mathbb{Z}_{k}\to\mathrm{U}(1)
be the
symplectic slice representation at
x
and consider

Y:=S^{1}\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{k}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}=(S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{Z}_{k},

where the
Z_{k}
-action on
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}
is given by

\mu\cdot(e^{2\pi i\theta},h,z)=(\mu\cdot e^{2\pi i\theta},h,\rho(\mu^{-1})z),

for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and any
(e^{2\pi i\theta},h,z)\in S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}
. There exists a symplectic form
\omega_{Y}
on

Y
such that the
S^{1}
-action given by

\lambda\cdot[e^{2\pi i\theta},h,z]=[\lambda\cdot e^{2\pi i\theta},h,z]

is Hamiltonian with homogeneous moment map
\Phi_{Y}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
given
by

\Phi_{Y}([e^{2\pi i\theta},h,z])=z.

The following
result is a special case of the Marle-Guillemin-Sternberg local normal
form (see
[
15
,
31
]
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M

be a point with stabilizer equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. There exist an

S^{1}
-invariant
U\subseteq M
open neighborhood of
x
and an open
neighborhood
V\subseteq Y
of
[1,0,0]
such that

(U,\omega|_{U},\Phi|_{U})
and
(V,\omega_{Y}|_{V},\Phi_{Y}|_{V})
are
isomorphic in the sense of Definition
1.126
.

Theorem
2.9
, its analog for manifolds (see
[
21
, Corollary A.7]
) and Lemma
3.3
are the
fundamental tools to prove the following result.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
k\geq 2

be an integer. Let
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{k})
be a non-empty
connected component. The closure
\overline{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
is a
compact, full symplectic submanifold of
(M,\omega)
of dimension
2. Moreover, if the stabilizer of
y\in\overline{\Sigma}
contains
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
as a
proper subgroup, then
y\in M^{S^{1}}
. Finally,
H|_{\Sigma}:\overline{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}
is the moment map of the
effective Hamiltonian
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-action on
\overline{\Sigma}
induced by the

S^{1}
-action on
M
.

Proof.

Since
M
is compact, so is
\overline{\Sigma}
. Let
x\in\Sigma
. By the Principal Orbit Theorem applied to
\Sigma
(see
[
8
, Theorem 2.8.5]
), there
exists an open, dense subset of
\overline{\Sigma}
consisting of points with
stabilizer equal to
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. Hence, the quotient
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{k}
acts
effectively on
\overline{\Sigma}
. Moreover, by Theorem

2.9
, its analog for manifolds (see

[
21
, Corollary A.7]
) and Lemma
3.3
,
\overline{\Sigma}

is a full symplectic submanifold of
(M,\omega)
of dimension
2, and if the stabilizer of
y\in\overline{\Sigma}
contains
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
as a
proper subgroup, then
y\in M^{S^{1}}
. In fact,
\overline{\Sigma}
is a fully embedded symplectic
submanifold of
(M,\omega)
, i.e., the inclusion
\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow M
is a good
C^{\infty}
map. To see this, we may argue as in Remark

2.11
, using the fact that the singular
points of
M
are isolated. Hence, the restriction of
H
to

\overline{\Sigma}
is a good
C^{\infty}
map.
∎

In other words, if
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{k})
is a
non-empty connected component then
(\overline{\Sigma},\omega|_{\overline{\Sigma}},H|_{\overline{\Sigma}})
is a compact symplectic toric orbifold of dimension
two.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
k\geq 2

be an integer. Let
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{k})
be a non-empty
connected component and let
\overline{\Sigma}\subseteq M^{\mathbb{Z}_{k}}

denote its closure. Then

•

there are precisely two points
x_{\min},x_{\max}\in\overline{\Sigma}

that are fixed points for the
(S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{k})
-action on
\overline{\Sigma}
and,
hence, lie in
M^{S^{1}}
,

•

the following equation holds

(3.2)

H(x_{\max})-H(x_{\min})=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\overline{\Sigma}}\omega|_{\overline{\Sigma}},\text{ and}

•

letting
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\min}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\max}}
denote the orbifold
structure groups of
x_{\min}
and
x_{\max}
respectively and
setting
\gcd(n_{\min},n_{\max})=m
, then
\overline{\Sigma}
is
diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(n^{\prime}_{\min},n^{\prime}_{\max})/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, where

n_{\min}=mn^{\prime}_{\min}
and
n_{\max}=mn^{\prime}_{\max}
.

Proof.

By the classification of Lerman and Tolman
[
29
, Theorem
1.5]
, since
\dim\overline{\Sigma}=2
and since we are
considering an effective action of
(S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, setting
H(\overline{\Sigma})=[h_{\min},h_{\max}]
, we have that

•

there are precisely two fixed points
x_{\min},x_{\max}\in\overline{\Sigma}
for the
(S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{k})
-action on
\overline{\Sigma}
, where

H(x_{\min})=h_{\min}
and
H(x_{\max})=h_{\max}
,

•

equation (
3.2
) holds,

•

the set of singular points of
\overline{\Sigma}
is contained in

\{x_{\min},x_{\max}\}
, and

•

the labels attached to
h_{\min}
and
h_{\max}
are precisely
the orders of the orbifold structure groups of
x_{\min}
and

x_{\max}
respectively.

The first and second statements follow immediately. To prove the last, we
observe that, by

[
29
, Example 1.1 and Theorem
1.5]
, the genus of the underlying topological surface

|\Sigma|
is zero. By Example
1.95
,
\mathbb{C}P^{1}(n^{\prime}_{\min},n^{\prime}_{\max})/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
as in the statement has genus zero
and at most two
singular points that have orbifold structure groups given by

\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\min}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{n_{\max}}
. Hence, the result follows immediately by
Theorem
1.44
.
∎

Motivated by Corollary
3.5
and
[
21
]
, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
and let
k\geq 2
be an
integer. The closure
\overline{\Sigma}\subseteq M^{\mathbb{Z}_{k}}
of a non-empty connected component of
\Sigma\subseteq\mathrm{Stab}(M;\mathbb{Z}_{k})

is called a

\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-isotropy orbi-sphere
. We refer to the two
fixed points in
\overline{\Sigma}\cap M^{S^{1}}
as the
north
and
south
poles of
\overline{\Sigma}
, depending on the value of the moment map. Finally, whenever
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
is not relevant, we drop it from the terminology.

Remark 3.7
.

By Lemma
2.14
and Corollary

3.5
, a fixed point with
orbifold structure group given by
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
has an orbi-weight of
-\frac{k}{m}
(respectively
\frac{k}{m}
) if and only if it is the
north (respectively south) pole of a
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-isotropy orbi-sphere.

Remark 3.8
.

Unlike the case of smooth manifolds (see
[
21
]
), a

\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-isotropy orbi-sphere need not be a connected component of

M^{\mathbb{Z}_{k}}
. The reason is that a connected component of
M^{\mathbb{Z}_{k}}

in a linear action on an orbi-vector space need not be a
suborbifold (cf. Remark
2.15
).

Example 3.9
.

(Weighted projective planes, continued)
We fix notation as in Example
2.6
. Let

M=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q)
be a weighted projective plane endowed with
the standard symplectic form. We consider the Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action
of (
2.10
). Suppose first that
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2}\neq 0
. All
the points in the orbi-sphere
\{[0:z_{1}:z_{2}]\in M\}
through the
fixed points
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
are fixed by the subgroup

\mathbb{Z}_{k_{1}}
of
S^{1}
. Hence, this is a
\mathbb{Z}_{k_{1}}
-isotropy
orbi-sphere. Similarly, the orbi-sphere
\{[z_{0}:z_{1}:0]\in M\}

(respectively
\{[z_{0}:0:z_{2}]\in M\}
) is a
\mathbb{Z}_{k_{2}}
-isotropy
orbi-sphere (respectively
\mathbb{Z}_{k_{3}}
). To consider the remaining cases, without loss of generality we may assume that
k_{1}=0
. Then the orbi-sphere

\{[z_{0}:z_{1}:0]\in M\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{s}
-isotropy orbi-sphere and

\{[z_{0}:0:z_{2}]\in M\}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
-isotropy orbi-sphere.
\Diamond

Example 3.10
.

(Example
1.12
, continued)
The Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space constructed in Examples

1.12
,
1.86
,
1.103

and
1.132
has exactly two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-isotropy
orbi-spheres that share the same north and south poles, which are the
two fixed points of type
\frac{1}{4}(1,3)
– see Example

1.124
. In fact, the union of these two isotropy
orbi-spheres and the three fixed points of Example
1.124
are the only points in
M
with a
non-trivial stabilizer.

\Diamond

To conclude this section, we state the following result that is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.9
and Lemma
2.14
.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M^{S^{1}}
. Then

•

x
belongs to at most two isotropy orbi-spheres;

•

if
H(x)
is not extremal and
x
lies on two isotropy orbi-spheres, then
x
is the south pole of one and the north pole of the other; and

•

if
x
lies on a fixed orbi-surface and on an isotropy orbi-sphere, then it is a singular point of
M
and lies on exactly one isotropy orbi-sphere.

3.2.
The labeled multigraph

We associate a
labeled multigraph
to a
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
(M,\omega,H)
as follows (see
Table
1
).

Vertex set:

•

Each connected component of
M^{S^{1}}
corresponds to a vertex. A
vertex corresponding to a fixed orbi-surface is called
fat
5
5

5

Correspondingly, we use a
thicker symbol when drawing a fat vertex.
. Every vertex is labeled with the moment map value
of the corresponding connected component of
M^{S^{1}}
.

•

If a vertex
v
corresponds to a singular point
x\in M
, we
label
v
with the type of the singular point (see Remark

2.8
). More explicitly, if the
orbi-weights at
x
are distinct, then we label
v
with

\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
, where
1
is in the direction of the larger
orbi-weight. If the orbi-weights are equal and
l^{2}=1\mod m
,
then again we label
v
with

\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Finally, in the remaining case, we label
v

with
both

\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
and
\frac{1}{m}(1,l^{\prime})
.

•

If a vertex
v
corresponds to a fixed orbi-surface
\Sigma
, we
label
v
with the following data:

–

the genus
g
of
|\Sigma|
,

–

the type of each singular point in
\Sigma
(see
Remark
3.2
), and

–

the normalized symplectic area
a(\Sigma):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\omega|_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
.

Edge set:

•

For any
k\geq 2
, each
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-isotropy orbi-sphere corresponds to an edge
that joins the vertices corresponding to the north and south poles
(see Definition
3.6
). We label the edge with the
integer
k
.

\mathbb{Z}_{k}

Table 1.
The labeled multigraph at a glance.

For simplicity, we often omit the moment map labels in figures depicting a labeled multigraph. Moreover, whenever it is
not relevant for the specific example or proof, we label a vertex that corresponds to a singular point
with only one expression of the form
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
even if the
orbi-weights are equal and
l^{2}\neq 1\mod m
. We also stress that
the relative position of non-extremal vertices does not necessarily
reflect the corresponding moment map values. However, the relative
position of the north/south pole of a given isotropy orbi-sphere always
corresponds to the top/bottom vertex of the corresponding edge. Finally, by a slight
abuse of notation, when showing the
labeled multigraph of a general family of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces (as
in Example
3.12
below), we include edges that may
correspond to ‘free’
S^{1}
-invariant orbi-spheres (in Figure
3.1
below, any
one of
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2}
may be equal to one).

Example 3.12
.

(Weighted projective planes, continued)
We determine the labeled multigraph of the Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space

(M=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q),\omega,H)
of
Example
2.6
under the condition that the integers
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2}
are all positive (see Figure

3.1
below). To this end, we fix the notation in
Example
2.6
. We remark that

•

the real numbers
\alpha
and
\beta
in the moment map labels are
positive, and

•

the integers
\ell_{p}
,
\ell_{q}
and
\ell_{s}
in the types of
singular points can be calculated explicitly as in Remark
1.79
.

Moreover, the following hold

(3.3)

H(F_{1})>H(F_{2})>H(F_{3})\quad\text{and}\quad sk_{0}=qk_{2}+pk_{1}.

The moment map labels can be easily determined using the
localization formula for equivariant cohomology
(see Theorem
3.17
below). To this end, using the equivariant
symplectic form
\omega^{S^{1}}=\omega-Hy
, we have that

(3.4)

0=\int\limits_{M}\omega^{S^{1}}=-\frac{1}{y}\left(\frac{q\,H_{\text{min}}}{k_{0}k_{1}}+\frac{p\,H_{\text{max}}}{k_{0}k_{2}}-\frac{s\,H(x_{s})}{k_{1}k_{2}}\right),

where
H_{\min}=H(F_{3})
and
H_{\max}=H(F_{1})
. Set
\alpha:=H(F_{2})
and let
\beta
be the normalized symplectic area of the

\mathbb{Z}_{k_{0}}
-orbi-sphere. By (
3.3
), (
3.4
)
and Corollary
3.5
, we obtain the moment map labels in
Figure
3.1
.

{k_{0}:=aq-cp}
{\,k_{2}:=as-bp}
{\,k_{1}:=bq-cs}
{\frac{1}{p}(1,\ell_{p}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha+\frac{qk_{2}}{s}\beta}
{\frac{1}{q}(1,\ell_{q}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha-\frac{pk_{1}}{s}\beta}
\,{\frac{1}{s}(1,\ell_{s}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha}

Figure 3.1.
Labeled multigraph of Example
2.6
if there is no fixed surface.

\Diamond

Example 3.13
.

(Example
1.12
, continued)
By bringing together Examples
1.12
,

1.86
,
1.103
,

1.132
and
3.10
, we obtain that the Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space studied in

[
50
]
has the labeled multigraph in Figure

3.2
.

2
2
{\frac{1}{4}(1,3),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{0}}
{\frac{1}{4}(1,3),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\frac{1}{4}}}
{\frac{1}{2}(1,1),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{8}}\,\,

Figure 3.2.
Labeled multigraph of the Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space studied in
[

]
.

In particular, this example illustrates that, unlike what happens in
the case of manifolds (see
[
21
]
), the class of combinatorial
invariants that we associate to a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space
must
necessarily
include multigraphs. Moreover, two edges that are incident to
the same two vertices can have equal labels (cf. Remark
2.15
)!

\Diamond

As expected, the labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space is an
invariant of the isomorphism class. To state this result formally, by
a slight abuse of terminology, we
say that two labeled multigraphs are
equal
if there exists a
bijection between them that preserves all the labels.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(M_{i},\omega_{i},H_{i})
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space for

i=1,2
. If
(M_{1},\omega_{1},H_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},H_{2})
are
isomorphic, their labeled multigraphs are equal.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to(M_{2},\omega_{2})
be an
isomorphism. By definition, it is an
S^{1}
-equivariant
diffeomorphism. Hence,
\varphi
establishes a bijection between
connected components of
\mathrm{Stab}(M_{1};K)
and
\mathrm{Stab}(M_{1};K)
for any subgroup
K
of

S^{1}
. Moreover, this bijection preserves the property of being a pole of an isotropy
orbi-sphere. Therefore, there is a bijection

\overline{\varphi}
between the
multigraphs of
(M_{1},\omega_{1},H_{1})
and
(M_{2},\omega_{2},H_{2})
. Since
H_{2}\circ\varphi=H_{1}
, the moment map labels are
preserved by
\overline{\varphi}
. Since
\varphi
is an
S^{1}
-equivariant
diffeomorphism,
\overline{\varphi}
preserves the
labels of edges. Moreover, by Theorem
2.9
and
Proposition
2.12
,
\overline{\varphi}

preserves the labels of vertices corresponding to singular points
that are isolated (see also Remark
2.8
). Finally, since

\varphi
is a symplectomorphism, it restricts to a
symplectomorphism between fixed orbi-surfaces. By Theorems

1.44
and

1.125
,

\overline{\varphi}
preserves the labels of fat vertices.
∎

One of the main objectives of the rest of this paper is to establish
the converse to Lemma
3.14
(see Theorem

5.1
).

3.3.
The labeled multigraph determines the fixed point set

In this section, we show that the labeled multigraph of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space can be used to ‘read off’ local invariants of each connected component of the fixed point set of the
S^{1}
-action. First we consider the case of a vertex corresponding to an isolated fixed point.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
v
be a vertex
of the labeled multigraph of
(M,\omega,H)
that corresponds to an isolated fixed point
x\in M
. Then the labeled multigraph of
(M,\omega,H)
determines an open neighborhood of
x
up to
isomorphism.

Proof.

By Corollary
1.120
, Theorem

2.9
and Proposition

2.12
, it suffices to show that the labeled multigraph of
(M,\omega,H)
determines the type of
x
as a singular point of
M
, the moment map value
H(x)
, and the integers
p,a_{1},a_{2}
that are associated to
x
as in Lemma
2.2
.

By definition of the labeled multigraph of
(M,\omega,H)
, the
label of
v
(of the form
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
or

\left\{\frac{1}{m}(1,l),\frac{1}{m}(1,l^{\prime})\right\}
) determines
the type of
x
. Moreover, the moment map label of
v
is
precisely
H(x)
. By Corollary
3.11
, the
vertex
v
is incident to at most two edges. We only consider
the case in which
v
is incident to exactly two edges,
leaving the other cases to the reader since they are entirely
analogous. We suppose that the edges are labeled by the
positive integers
k_{1},k_{2}
. By Lemma
2.14
, we
have that

p=\gcd(k_{1},k_{2})\,,\quad\lvert a_{1}\rvert=\frac{k_{1}}{\gcd(k_{1},k_{2})}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lvert a_{2}\rvert=\frac{k_{2}}{\gcd(k_{1},k_{2})}.

It remains to determine the signs of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. To this
end, since
v
corresponds to an isolated fixed point
x\in M
, by Corollary
1.140
and Theorem

2.9
, then either
a_{1}a_{2}<0
or
a_{1}a_{2}>0
. The former occurs if and only if the moment map label of

v
is not extremal among all moment map labels. In this case,
by Corollary
3.11
, the fixed point
x
is the
south pole of one isotropy orbi-sphere and the north pole of
another. Hence, in this case, the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
can be used to determine the signs of
a_{1}
and

a_{2}
. The case
a_{1}a_{2}>0
occurs precisely if the moment
map label of
v
is either the maximum or minimum among
all moment map labels. By Corollary
1.140

and Theorem
2.9
, the former (respectively
latter) occurs precisely if
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are both negative
(respectively positive). Hence, the signs of
a_{1}
and
a_{2}

can be determined from the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
also in this case.
∎

In order to consider the remaining case of a fat vertex, we discuss
first a localization formula coming from equivariant cohomology in our context.

Intermezzo: the ABBV localization formula

The material described below is used throughout the paper
and readers are referred to
[
34
]
for more details.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space. We fix a generator
of the Lie algebra of
S^{1}
. Hence, we obtain an infinitesimal
generator
\xi^{M}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
for the action, as well as a generator

y
of the dual of the Lie algebra of
S^{1}
.
Consider the space
\Omega^{*}(M)^{S^{1}}
of
S^{1}
-invariant differential forms on
M
and let

\Omega^{*}(M)^{S^{1}}[y]=\Omega^{*}(M)^{S^{1}}\otimes\mathbb{R}[y]

be the ring of polynomials in
y
with coefficients in

\Omega^{*}(M)^{S^{1}}
, where
y
has degree two. The
Cartan
differential

d_{\xi^{M}}
on
\Omega(M)^{S^{1}}[y]
is defined on
homogeneous elements by

d_{\xi^{M}}(\alpha\otimes y^{i}):=d\alpha\otimes y^{i}-(\iota_{\xi^{M}}\alpha)\otimes y^{i+1}.

The
S^{1}
-
equivariant de Rham complex of

M
is

\left(\Omega^{*}(M)^{S^{1}}[y],d_{X}\right)
, and its cohomology
H^{*}_{S^{1}}(M)
is the
S^{1}
-
equivariant de Rham cohomology of

M
. For example, the equivariant symplectic form

(3.5)

\omega^{S^{1}}:=\omega-Hy

is an
S^{1}
-equivariant cohomology class of degree two.

Let
\Sigma
be a fixed orbi-surface and let

\nu_{\Sigma}
denote its normal orbi-bundle. By fixing an

S^{1}
-invariant almost complex structure that is compatible with

\omega
, we may see
\nu_{\Sigma}
as a complex line bundle. Since

\Sigma
is fixed, we obtain a fiberwise linear complex
S^{1}
-action
on
\nu_{\Sigma}
. Moreover, since the singular points of
M
are
isolated by assumption, the regular locus of
\Sigma
is
connected. Hence, just as in the case of smooth manifolds, the
isotropy weight
\lambda
of the fiberwise linear action does not depend on the
regular point and must be equal to
\pm 1
by effectiveness. Finally, we define the
equivariant Euler class
of
\nu_{F}
by

(3.6)

\displaystyle e^{S^{1}}(\nu_{\Sigma})=e(\nu_{\Sigma})+y\,\lambda_{\Sigma}.

We remark that, in this case, the equivariant Euler class is equal to
the equivariant Chern class (cf. Remark
1.67
).

Example 3.16
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
has
k
singular points and that the degree of
\nu_{\Sigma}
is
\beta_{\Sigma}
, so that

c_{1}(\nu_{\Sigma})=\beta_{\Sigma}u
for a generator
u\in H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow M
denote the inclusion. Then

(3.7)

\iota_{\Sigma}^{*}\,c_{1}^{S^{1}}(TM)=c_{1}^{S^{1}}(T\Sigma)+\beta_{\Sigma}\,u+\lambda\,y=\left(2-2g-k+\beta_{\Sigma}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\right)u+\lambda y,

where
c_{1}^{S^{1}}(TM)
is the equivariant first Chern class of
TM
,
the integer
m_{i}
is the order of the orbifold structure group of the singular
point
x_{i}
on
\Sigma
and
g
is the genus of
\Sigma
. In
(
3.7
) we use the fact that, since
\Sigma\subset M^{S^{1}}
, the circle acts with weight
0
on
T\Sigma
and also that
c_{1}^{S^{1}}(T\Sigma)=c_{1}(T\Sigma)=\chi(\Sigma)u
, where

(3.8)

\chi(\Sigma)=2-2g+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{m_{i}}-1\right)

is the orbifold Euler characteristic of
\Sigma
(see
[
12
]
).
\Diamond

The following result is a special case of the ABBV localization
formula for orbifolds (see
[
34
, Theorem 2.1]
).

Theorem 3.17
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian

S^{1}
-space and let
\alpha
be an

S^{1}
-equivariant closed form. Then

(3.9)

\int_{M}\alpha=\sum\limits_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{\iota_{\Sigma}^{*}\alpha}{e^{S^{1}}(\nu_{\Sigma})}+\sum\limits_{x}\frac{\alpha(x)}{m_{x}\lambda_{1,x}\lambda_{2,x}},

where the first sum is over the fixed orbi-surfaces, the second
is over the isolated fixed points, and, for each isolated fixed
point
x
, the integer
m_{x}
is the order of its
orbifold structure group and
\lambda_{1,x},\lambda_{2,x}

are its orbi-weights.

Back to the case of a fat vertex

Let
\Sigma

denote the corresponding fixed orbi-surface. By Corollary

2.10
,
\Sigma
is a full symplectic suborbifold
of
(M,\omega)
. Hence, by Corollary
1.110
, the
normal bundle to
\Sigma
can be endowed with the structure of a
complex vector orbi-bundle. Since
\dim\Sigma=2
, the normal bundle
to
\Sigma
can be viewed as a complex line orbi-bundle.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
v
be a
fat vertex of the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
that corresponds to a fixed orbi-surface
\Sigma
. Then the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
determines the Seifert invariant of the
principal
S^{1}
-orbi-bundle associated to the normal orbi-bundle to

\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
n
be the number of singular points that lie on
\Sigma
. Then
the Seifert invariant that we are interested in is of the form

(g;\beta_{0},(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(\alpha_{n},\beta_{n})),

where
g
is the genus of
|\Sigma|
,
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
is the
Seifert invariant of the singular point
x_{i}\in\Sigma
, and

\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
is uniquely determined by equation
(
1.8
).

Since there is a label for each singular point on
\Sigma
, then
n

can be recovered from the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
. Moreover, the genus label of
v
is precisely

g
. Next we show that the Seifert invariant of
x_{i}
is determined
by the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
. We fix
i=1,\ldots,n
and let
\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
be the type label that corresponds to

x_{i}
. Let
1\leq l^{\prime}<m
be such that
l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
. Then the
Seifert invariant of
x_{i}
is either
(m,l^{\prime})
, if
H(\Sigma)
is
the minimum of
H
, or
(m,l)
, if
H(\Sigma)
is
the maximum of
H
. (By Remark
3.2
, these are the
only cases to consider.)

It remains to show that
\beta_{0}
is determined by the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
. By Proposition
1.66
, and
Remarks
1.67
and
1.68
, it
suffices to show that the degree of the normal orbi-bundle to
\Sigma
is determined by the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
. This can be checked directly using the localization formula for orbifolds (see
Theorem
3.17
), as follows. First we assume that there are two extremal fixed orbi-surfaces

\Sigma_{\pm}
, labeled so that
H(\Sigma_{-})
(respectively

H(\Sigma_{+})
) is the minimum
H_{\min}
(respectively maximum

H_{\max}
) of
H
. By applying Theorem
3.17
to the equivariant
symplectic form
\omega^{S^{1}}:=\omega-Hy
, we obtain

(3.10)

\begin{split}0&=\int_{\Sigma_{-}}\frac{\omega-H_{\min}y}{\beta_{-}u_{-}+y}+\int_{\Sigma_{+}}\frac{\omega-H_{\max}y}{\beta_{+}u_{+}-y}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{H(x_{i})y}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}\,\,y^{2}}\\
&=\int_{\Sigma_{-}}{\scriptstyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\left(\frac{\beta_{-}u_{-}}{y}\right)^{j}}\left(\frac{\omega}{y}-H_{\min}\right)+\int_{\Sigma_{+}}{\scriptstyle\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\beta_{+}u_{+}}{y}\right)^{j}}\left(H_{\max}-\frac{\omega}{y}\right)\\
&\mbox{}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{H(x_{i})}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}\,y}\\
&=\frac{1}{y}\left(area(\Sigma_{-})+\beta_{-}H_{\min}+\beta_{+}H_{\max}-area(\Sigma_{+})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{H(x_{i})}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}\right),\end{split}

where

•

u_{\pm}\in H^{2}(\Sigma_{\pm};\mathbb{Z})
is a generator,

•

\nu_{\pm}
is the normal orbi-bundle to
\Sigma_{\pm}

with degree given by
\beta_{\pm}
and equivariant Euler class equal to
e^{S^{1}}(\nu_{\pm})
,

•

we assume that there are
n
isolated fixed points

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, and

•

the order of the orbifold structure group of
x_{i}
is

m_{i}
and the orbi-weights are

\lambda_{i,1},\lambda_{i,2}
.

In order to see that (
3.10
) holds, we remark that, since
\nu_{\pm}
is a complex line orbi-bundle, the
equivariant Euler class equals the equivariant first Chern
class
c_{1}^{S^{1}}(\nu_{\pm})
(see Remark

1.67
). Moreover, since every the non-zero
orbi-weight of any point in
\Sigma_{\pm}
is
\mp 1
, we have that

c_{1}^{S^{1}}(\nu_{\Sigma_{\pm}})=\beta_{\pm}u_{\pm}\mp y
. On the other
hand, integrating the equivariant form
1
and using the
localization formula again, we have that

(3.11)

0=\int_{M}1=\frac{1}{y^{2}}\left(-\beta_{-}-\beta_{+}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}\right).

Hence, by (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), the following holds:

(3.12)

\begin{pmatrix}H_{\min}&H_{\max}\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{-}\\
\beta_{+}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}area(\Sigma_{+})-area(\Sigma_{-})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{H(x_{i})}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}\\
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
H_{\min}<H_{\max}
, (
3.12
) admits a
unique solution
(\beta_{-},\beta_{+})
. Hence, since all the other
quantities in (
3.12
) can be reconstructed from the
labeled multigraph, the result follows.

Suppose next that
(M,\omega,H)
has a unique fixed orbi-surface

\Sigma
and denote by
\beta
the degree of its normal orbi-bundle. By integrating
1
and using the localization formula of
Theorem
3.17
, we obtain that

(3.13)

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}.

Since the right hand side of (
3.13
) can be reconstructed
from the labeled multigraph, the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

The last case in the proof of Proposition

3.18
can be shown alternatively as
follows. Without loss of generality, by adding a suitable constant we may assume that
H(\Sigma)\neq 0
. Then, if we integrate
\omega^{S^{1}}
, we obtain that

(3.14)

\beta=\beta_{\pm}=\frac{1}{H(\Sigma_{\pm})}\left(\pm\,area(\Sigma_{\pm})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\frac{H(x_{i})}{\lambda_{i,1}\lambda_{i,2}}\right),

depending on whether
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\pm}
is a maximal or a minimal
orbi-surface. Note that (
3.13
) together with
(
3.14
) give us a necessary compatibility condition
for the labels of the multigraphs of a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space that
has a unique fixed orbi-surface.

Example 3.20

(Weighted projective spaces, continued)
.

We determine the labeled multigraph of the Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space

(M=\mathbb{C}P^{2}(p,s,q),\omega,H)
of
Example
2.6
under the condition that
k_{1}=0
(see
Figure
3.3
and cf. Example
3.12
). Here
A
is a positive
real number that represents the area label. Moreover, as in Example
3.12
, the positive integers

\ell_{p}
,
\ell_{q}
and
\ell_{s}
are obtained as in Remark

1.79
. Finally, the relation
between the moment map labels of the minimum and maximum can be
obtained by combining Lemma
1.72
and (
3.14
).

s
q
\frac{1}{p}(1,l_{p}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H=\alpha+\frac{A\,q\,s}{p}

This example shows that, if
M

(M,\omega,H)

Example 3.21

4.
Equivariant operations

4.1.
Symplectic weighted blow-up

In this section we describe how to perform equivariant symplectic
weighted blow-ups at singular points, a construction that we use to
desingularize Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces equivariantly.
Recall that, topologically, a symplectic blow-up at a regular point
corresponds to cutting out a symplectic ball around the point and
collapsing the boundary of the resulting space along the fibers of the
Hopf fibration. Smoothly this can be achieved by symplectic
reduction using an auxiliary circle action (see
[
16
]
).
This was generalized to
symplectic cutting
by Lerman
[
28
]
.
Symplectic
weighted
blow-ups were introduced by
Meinrenken and Sjamaar in
[
35
]
, and by Godinho in

[
14
]
: Topologically, the idea is to remove a symplectic orbi-ball centered
around a singular point
6
6

6

Strictly speaking, one may carry out
a weighted blow-up at a regular point, but, since we do not consider
this case in this paper, we restrict to singular points.
and to collapse the orbits of some
(weighted) circle action on the boundary of the resulting space. In
what follows, we describe this operation.

Let
(M^{2n},\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic orbifold and
let
x\in M
be a singular point that is isolated with cyclic orbifold
structure group
\Gamma
.
By Darboux’s Theorem for orbifolds (see Theorem
1.111
), there exists
a l.u.c.
(\widehat{U},\Gamma,\varphi)
centered at
x
such that

\widehat{U}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
, where

\Gamma<\mathrm{U}(n)
acts in a linear fashion,
\omega_{\lvert U}
is as in Example
1.100
, and
[0]
is the only
singular point in
U
. Since
\Gamma<\mathrm{U}(n)
is abelian, we may assume without loss of generality that
\Gamma
is contained in
the maximal torus of
\mathrm{U}(n)
consisting of diagonal matrices. We fix a
\Gamma
-invariant
ball
\widehat{B}\subseteq\widehat{U}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
around
0
and

\widehat{m}:=(\widehat{m}_{1},\dots,\widehat{m}_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
so
that
\gcd(\widehat{m}_{1},\dots,\widehat{m}_{n})=1
. Moreover, we
denote a circle acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by
\widehat{S}^{1}
.
The
\widehat{S}^{1}
-action on
\widehat{B}
given by

(4.1)

\begin{split}\widehat{S}^{1}\times\widehat{B}&\rightarrow\widehat{B}\\
(\lambda,z_{1},\ldots,z_{2})&\mapsto(\lambda^{\widehat{m}_{1}}z_{1},\dots,\lambda^{\widehat{m}_{n}}z_{n}),\end{split}

is effective, fixes only
0
and commutes with the

\Gamma
-action. Since
\widehat{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
, the moment map

\widehat{H}:\widehat{B}\to\mathbb{R}
for the above

\widehat{S}^{1}
-action given by

\widehat{H}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{m}_{i}|z_{i}|^{2}

is proper. The
\widehat{S}^{1}
-action of (
4.1
) descends to an effective Hamiltonian action
of the quotient
\widehat{S}^{1}/(\widehat{S}^{1}\cap\Gamma)\simeq S^{1}
on
the orbifold
B=\widehat{B}/\Gamma\subseteq U
that fixes only
[0]
. Let
H:B\to\mathbb{R}
be the moment
map for this action such that
H([0])=0
and let

m:=(m_{1},\dots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}_{+}
be the orbi-weights for this action at

[0]
. (By Lemma
2.2
, for each
i=1,\ldots,n
, the orbi-weight
m_{i}
equals

\widehat{m}_{i}/p
, where
p=|\widehat{S}^{1}\cap\Gamma|
.) By
Corollary
1.129
,

H([z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]_{\Gamma})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}|z_{i}|^{2}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small. Since
p\varepsilon
is a
regular value of
\widehat{H}
,
\varepsilon
is a regular value of

H
. Moreover, since
[0]_{\Gamma}\in B
is the only singular point,
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)
is a smooth
submanifold of
B
. Hence, the quotient
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
is an orbifold
that inherits a symplectic form from
\omega
by symplectic reduction.
Moreover, it satisfies the following properties:

(a)

the singular points are isolated and have cyclic orbifold
structure groups, and

(b)

it is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(\widehat{m}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{m}_{n})/\Gamma^{\prime}
, where

(4.2)

\Gamma^{\prime}:=\Gamma/(\Gamma\cap\widehat{S}^{1}).

Since a singular point in
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
corresponds to an
orbit with finite stabilizer, property
(a)
is a consequence of the local normal
form for Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions (see
[
15
,
31
]
). On the other
hand, property
(b)
follows from the fact that
\widehat{H}
is
proper and the definition of
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(\widehat{m}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{m}_{n})
– see Example
1.11
.

For any
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we set

B_{\eta}(x):=H^{-1}([0,\eta))
. This is an
S^{1}
-invariant open symplectic
ellipsoid centered at
x
and contained in
U
. Given
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, we consider the following
S^{1}
-action
on the product

Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}:=B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle S^{1}\times Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}

\displaystyle\,\,\rightarrow\,\,Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}

\displaystyle\left(\lambda,[z_{1},\dots,z_{n}]_{\Gamma},w\right)

\displaystyle\,\,\mapsto\,\,\left([\lambda^{m_{1}}z_{1},\dots,\lambda^{m_{n}}z_{n}]_{\Gamma},\lambda^{-1}w\right).

We endow
Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}
with the symplectic form given by
the sum of the pullback of
\omega
and of the pullback of the
standard symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}
. Then the above circle action is
Hamiltonian and a moment map is given by

\widetilde{H}\left([z_{1},\dots,z_{n}]_{\Gamma},w\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(m_{1}\lvert z_{1}\rvert^{2}+\cdots+m_{n}\lvert z_{n}\rvert^{2}-\lvert w\rvert^{2}\right).

Since
\varepsilon
is a regular value of
H
, it is also a regular
value of
\widetilde{H}
. Moreover, since
([0]_{\Gamma},0)\in Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}
is the only singular point,
\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)
is, in fact, a smooth
submanifold of
Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}
. We observe that the restriction of
the inclusion
B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)\hookrightarrow Z_{\varepsilon,\delta}
to
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)
is a smooth embedding

H^{-1}(\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)
. Hence, it can be checked that the symplectic reduction

(\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1},\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
is a
symplectic orbifold that satisfies the following properties:

(A)

the smooth embedding
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)
descends to a full embedding

H^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
– see Remark

2.11
,

(B)

the only singular points in

\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
lie in the image of the above
embedding, and

(C)

the complement of
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
in

\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
is symplectomorphic to the
open subset
H^{-1}((\varepsilon,\varepsilon+\delta))=B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)\smallsetminus\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}
of

B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)
.

We denote the image of the embedding
H^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1}
by
\Sigma_{0}
. Explicitly, it is
given by

(4.3)

\Sigma_{0}:=\left\{\left([z_{1},\dots,z_{n}]_{\Gamma},0\right)\in\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)\right\}/\,\,S^{1}.

By
property
(C)
, the symplectic orbifolds
(M\smallsetminus\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(x)},\omega)
and

(\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1},\omega_{\mathrm{red}})
can be glued along

B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)\smallsetminus\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}
to obtain the symplectic orbifold

(4.4)

\left(\widetilde{M}_{x,\varepsilon}:=\left(M\smallsetminus\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}\right)\coprod_{B_{\varepsilon+\delta}(x)\smallsetminus\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(x)}}\widetilde{H}^{-1}(\varepsilon)/S^{1},\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon}\right).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic orbifold and let
x\in M
be a
singular point with cyclic orbifold structure group
\Gamma
. The

(symplectic) weighted blow-up of

(M,\omega)
at
x
of

size

\varepsilon
and
weights

(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}_{+}
is the symplectic orbifold

(\widetilde{M}_{x},\widetilde{\omega})
in (
4.4
). The

exceptional divisor
of the weighted blow-up is the (fully
embedded) suborbifold

\Sigma_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}(\widehat{m}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{m}_{n})/\Gamma^{\prime}

of (
4.3
), where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is as in (
4.2
).

Remark 4.2
.

By looking at the construction of a weighted blow-up at a singular
point, it is clear that there are simple constraints on the possible
size, e.g., the symplectic size of a Darboux chart. To simplify the
exposition, we omit stating explicitly that
\varepsilon>0

satisfies these constraints, assuming implicitly that it does. We
trust that this does not cause confusion.

Since we are primarily interested in performing weighted blow-ups on symplectic orbifolds with
isolated singular points all of whose orbifold structure groups are
cyclic, the following result plays an important role.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic orbifold with isolated singular
points such that all orbifold structure groups are cyclic. Let
x\in M
be a singular point. For all
\varepsilon>0
and all

(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}_{+}
, the
weighted blow-up of
(M,\omega)
at
x
of size
\varepsilon
and
weights
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
has isolated singular points and is such
that all orbifold structure groups are cyclic.

Proof.

This result follows immediately by the conditions on the singular
points of
M
, the above construction of the weighted blow-up, and
properties
(a)
,
(A)
and
(B)
above.
∎

Let
G
be a compact Lie group. As expected, a weighted blow-up can be
performed on a Hamiltonian
G
-space so that the result is still a
Hamiltonian
G
-space by making
G
-equivariant choices and using a
local circle action that commutes with the
G
-action (see

[
35
, Section 4.1]
)
7
7

7

Strictly speaking, in
[
35
, Section
4.1]
it is not checked that the moment map is good in the sense
of Definition
1.37
. However, this follows at once by
observing that a weighted blow-up is obtained by symplectic cutting
and that the map used to perform the cut is good by construction.
. Moreover, for a fixed singular point
x
, whenever the size is sufficiently
small, the result only depends on the weights and the size up to isomorphisms of Hamiltonian
G
-spaces (see
[
35
, Proposition
4.2]
). In the case of a
G\simeq S^{1}
-action, we denote an

S^{1}
-equivariant weighted blow-up at an isolated singular point
x

with cyclic structure group
\Gamma
of size
\varepsilon
and weights

(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
by

\text{WBU}(\lvert\Gamma\rvert,k_{1},\ldots,k_{n},m_{1},\ldots,m_{n},\varepsilon),

where
k_{1}\geq\ldots\geq k_{n}
are the orbi-weights of the

S^{1}
-action at
x
(see Definition
2.4
).

4.1.1.
Weighted blow-ups of Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces

In this section, we consider two families of
S^{1}
-equivariant weighted blow-ups on
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces that play an important role in some of our
key results (see Proposition
4.5
, and Theorems
5.5
and
5.8
). Before
discussing these results further, we observe that weighted
blow-ups preserve Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-spaces (see the beginning of
Section
3
). This is an immediate
consequence of Lemma
4.3
and of

[
35
, Section 4]
.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M
be a singular point. Any

S^{1}
-equivariant weighted blow-up of
(M,\omega,H)
at
x
is a
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space.

Our aim is
to describe the effect of the aforementioned blow-ups on the labeled multigraphs
(see Propositions
4.5
and
4.9
below). Our
choice to split these results is dictated by how we use each family in
our classification results: Proposition
4.5
is used in
the desingularization process of Theorem
4.12
, while
(the reverse construction to that of) Proposition
4.9
is needed to obtain the minimal
models of Theorems
5.5
and

5.8
. Moreover, there are some small geometric
differences between the two families (see Remarks

4.8
and
4.10
). Finally, we believe that this makes it
easier for the reader to extract the necessary information.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
(M,\omega,H)
be a
Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-space and let
x\in M
be a singular point of type

\frac{1}{m}(1,l)
. Let
1\leq l^{\prime}<m
be the integer such that
l\cdot l^{\prime}=1\mod m
and let
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
ll^{\prime}+m\gamma=1
. Let
\frac{a_{1}}{p},\frac{a_{2}}{p}
be the
orbi-weights of the
S^{1}
-action at
x
, where
\gcd(a_{1},a_{2})=1
and

a_{1}\geq a_{2}
.
Let
b_{1},b_{2}>0
be integers such that

(4.5)

\gcd(b_{1},b_{2})=1\quad\text{and}\quad b_{1}l-b_{2}=b\,m,\quad\text{for some $b\in\mathbb{Z}$}.

Let
s_{1}
be the integer that satisfies
1\leq s_{1}<b_{1}
and

s_{1}=b\mod b_{1}
if
a_{1}m\geq a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2}
, or

s_{1}b=1\!\!\!\mod b_{1}

otherwise. Let
s_{2}
be the integer that satisfies
1\leq s_{2}<b_{2}
and
s_{2}=-(bl^{\prime}+b_{1}\gamma)\!\!\!\mod b_{2}
if
a_{2}m\geq a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1}
, or

(bl^{\prime}+b_{1}\gamma)s_{2}=-1\!\!\!\mod b_{2}

otherwise. Finally, we set
k:=m/p
.

An
S^{1}
-equivariant weighted blow-up of
(M,\omega)
at
x

\text{WBU}\left(m,\frac{a_{1}}{p},\frac{a_{2}}{p},\frac{b_{1}}{m},\frac{b_{2}}{m},\varepsilon\right)

results in the following ‘local’ changes in the labeled multigraph of

(M,\omega,H)
near the vertex that corresponds to the connected
component of
the fixed point set to which

belongs:

Non-extremal fixed point:

{\,\,\frac{1}{b_{1}}(1,s_{1}),\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha+\frac{k\lvert a_{1}\rvert}{b_{1}}\varepsilon}

Isolated extremum

(

{\hskip 56.9055pt\frac{1}{\lvert a_{1}\rvert}(1,s_{1}),\frac{1}{\lvert a_{2}\rvert}(1,s_{2}),}

Point on fixed orbi-surface

In

. Here
